# The Binding of the Manuscript

**Description of the Exterior**

This volume is bound in a classic 18th-century style. The front and back covers are decorated with **marbled paper** <note>Marbling is a decorative technique where paint is floated on water and then transferred to paper, creating stone-like patterns.</note> featuring a complex pattern of grey, black, and red swirls. 

The spine is constructed from **red-brown leather**, providing both durability and a dignified appearance for the collection.

# Prologus
<note>original: "Prologus" — Prologue</note>

## The musical discipline of Boethius.

The musical discipline of Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook for a thousand years.</note>, contained in five volumes, rests upon the deepest foundations of arithmetic and philosophy. For this reason, it cannot be understood by everyone everywhere. It is the habit of the half-educated singers of our time to think that the more obscure a work is, the more barren it is. Yet, to the learned and those looking more deeply, the more subtle and demonstrable a work is, the more solid and better it appears. Thus, while it has always been neglected by the unlearned, it has always been held in great value among the more skilled.

Therefore, we—who strive to be of use to everyone and to contribute something to the common good—have produced this brief compendium divided into three small books. We have reduced its long-windedness into a narrow space, its roughness into a plain, and its darkness into light. We have omitted nothing that pertains to the art and its practice. We have constructed a very useful work both for singers, whom we call *practicos* <note>original: "practicos" — practitioners or performers</note> in Greek, and for observers, whom we call *theoricos* <note>original: "theoricos" — theorists</note> in Greek. From this work, once they have read and understood it, they will see that they shall attain much honor and pleasure. They will admit that they are wonderfully delighted by this new form of our most beautiful art, and that we have brought much help to the common education of all through this present labor. 

From here, as if from an overflowing and public fountain, one may draw whatever I have been able to collect over a long time, with many vigils and diligent nighttime studies, from the reading of the most approved authors and the discipline of the most famous teachers. Through this, it will be possible to learn with very swift and easy study, and to reach the highest peak of music with a most peaceful step. 

Let no one fear the majesty of philosophy; let no one fear the complexity of arithmetic; let no one fear the twists and turns of proportions. For here, anyone—even if they are unrefined in every way—may emerge as a most excellent and skilled musician, provided they are willing to apply their hearing to the discipline and are not entirely devoid of reason. Indeed, because we have striven to serve the understanding, we have maintained such a balance of speech and moderation of style that in reading these things, the more skilled may be abundantly refreshed, the half-educated may profit greatly, and the entirely unlearned may be most gently educated. 

We are not undertaking here to instruct only philosophers or mathematicians; anyone who has been taught the first rudiments of grammar will understand these words of ours. Here the mouse and the elephant can swim together; Daedalus and Icarus can fly together. <note>original: "hic mus et elephas pariter natare dedalus et icarus pariter volare possunt." This is a classical metaphor meaning the text is shallow enough for a beginner (the mouse) to navigate, but deep enough for a master (the elephant) to find substance.</note> Furthermore, it will be the duty of the prudent and grateful reader to grant pardon if he does not find in this work of ours that pride of speech which is found in Cicero and Sallust; if I use fewer ornaments...

<insert>
<margin>NATIONAL LIBRARY OF FLORENCE</margin>
**Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja: Practical Music, and Manuscript Poetic Works**
**Designated as number 106 among the Codices.**
</insert>

<summary>This prologue introduces Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja's "Musica Practica," where he argues that traditional music theory (like Boethius) is too difficult for modern singers and proposes a more accessible, practical guide.</summary>
<keywords>Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja, Boethius, Music Theory, Education, Quadrivium, Renaissance music, Latin translation</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the preface or introductory letter of a musical treatise, likely from the late 15th or early 16th century, given the references to classical figures and the lament over the "corruption" of modern music.</meta>

...nor does our discourse proceed with elegant cultivation. For I may say of music what we read Marcus Manilius said of astronomy: *The matter itself refuses to be adorned, being content to be taught.* <note>original: "Ornari res ipsa negat contenta doceri." This is a common trope in technical writing of the Renaissance, suggesting that the beauty of the subject—music—speaks for itself without needing flowery language.</note>

Truly, regarding this distinguished part of philosophy—namely, music—if anyone were to consider its nature, beauty, and nobility, he would be drawn not by my own embellishments, but would fly and follow of his own accord the venerable footsteps of Orpheus the Thracian, Amphion the Theban, Arion of Lesbos, Mercury, Linus, Solomon, Pythagoras, Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, Chorebus, Lycaon, Theophrastus <note>OCR reads "prophrasti," likely a corruption of Theophrastus, a Greek philosopher who wrote on music.</note>, Timotheus, and the others who achieved an immortal name through this discipline. 

These were the men whom venerable antiquity so admired that it said they moved wild beasts with the sweetness of song, possessed the hearts of men, recalled souls into bodies, bent the shades of the underworld to mercy, and drew hard mountain ashes <note>The "ornus" is a type of flowering ash tree, famously moved by Orpheus's music in classical myth.</note> from the mountains. Although these things may seem fabled and beyond belief, there is no doubt that music is the producer of miraculous works. 

It is well known that Saul, king of the inhabitants of Jerusalem <note>original: "faule folimorũ regẽ". Solima is a poetic abbreviation for Hierosolyma (Jerusalem).</note>, was usually cured by the melody of the <term>cithara|A stringed instrument of antiquity, often used to refer to any plucked instrument like a lyre or lute</term> when he was vexed by an evil spirit. David used the song of the <term>psaltery|A stringed instrument played by plucking, similar to a zither</term> as a kind of vehicle to lift his mind toward prophesying. Elisha, the great disciple of the father of the Hebrew Carmelites, called for a musician when he wished to prophesy. 

Who does not know that weeping infants are calmed by the lullabies of nurses, and the burning heat of the blood in the young is extinguished? Even horses, at the blast of the trumpet, prick up their ears, their limbs tremble, they neigh loudly, leap about, and—unable to stand still—thirst for the fight and for Mars <note>In this context, Mars refers to war or the heat of battle.</note>. Priscian is the authority who says that among the Sicilians there is a fountain that seems to dance to the sound of the lyre. Therefore, music undoubtedly possesses the greatest energy and immense authority over human souls, whether it wishes to soothe or to uplift. 

But if in our time so many miracles are by no means performed through music, it is not to be blamed on the art—which is most excellently described above—but on those using the art badly. For if those most proven musicians whom we mentioned above were recalled to life, they would deny that the music of our time was invented by them; it has been rendered so inept, inharmonious, and scattered by the corruption of certain singers. 

Moved by these reasons, we have published the present work, hoping that even if there are perhaps some now living who, pricked by malice, prefer to detract rather than to improve, there will be many in the future and among posterity who—the venom of envy having been buried—will praise our labor and grant favor to our work, driven by a love of virtue. 

Therefore, let the division of the work be as follows: 
In the first book, we shall set forth the subtle practice of music. 
In the second, we shall discuss the theory accurately. 
In the third, we shall treat <term>semi-mathematical and semi-physical music|A reference to the medieval view of music as a science of ratios (math) that affects the body and soul (physics)</term>, which we will prove by reason. 

First, therefore, let us discuss what music is and what harmony is.

<summary>This preface introduces a treatise on music, defending the dignity of the art through classical and biblical examples (Orpheus, David, Saul) while lamenting the decline of musical quality in the author's contemporary era. The author outlines a three-part structure for the work covering practice, theory, and the mathematical/physical nature of sound.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, Orpheus, King Saul, David, cithara, psaltery, musical corruption, practice and theory, semi-mathematical music, Renaissance music</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction following the prologue, moving from general praise of music to the formal definitions and divisions of the science of music.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<sig>.a.3.</sig>

### The prologue finished, the treatise begins, where first he discusses what music is and what harmony is.

**H**armony and music are believed by many to be the same, but we feel quite differently. From the opinions of certain musicians, through long investigation, we have gathered that harmony is the blending of concordant voices; music, however, is the very logic of that concord, or a deliberate and subtle investigation through reason. Music is threefold: for one is mundane <note>original: *mundana*; this refers to the "Music of the Spheres," the ancient belief that the movement of celestial bodies creates a divine harmony</note>, another is human <note>original: *humana*; the harmonious relationship between the human soul and body</note>, and the third is called instrumental. We will deal with mundane and human music in the second and third books, since they are speculative and theoretical. The third consideration, which is entirely concerned with instruments, will claim a smaller place for itself.

Instruments are of two types: one consists of nature, while the other consists of art. The natural instrument is the human voice, by which we can naturally raise and lower the pitch. The instrument of art is said to be that which is made by craft, not nature, such as the monochord <term>monochord: a teaching tool with a single string and a movable bridge used to demonstrate mathematical musical intervals</term>, the kithara <term>kithara: an ancient stringed instrument similar to a lyre</term>, and others that serve melody. Regarding the most accurate practical consideration of these, three things occur to us to be examined: namely, voice, sound, and number (or measure). In this context, "voice" is used loosely for the sound of both humans and instruments; as Aristotle's opinion is in the book *On the Soul* <note>original: *De Anima*</note>: voice is the sound of a living thing only, yet "voice" is also used for inanimate things like musical instruments, which only bear a similarity because they sound distinctly. "Sound," however, is not taken simply, but rather for the sound of two or more strings struck together, or of two or more people singing in unison. "Number," likewise, is not considered simply, but in relation to its musical qualities <note>original: *passiones*; referring to the mathematical proportions that create musical intervals</note>. The first consideration is called "fixed song" <note>original: *cantus firmus*</note> by the moderns, but "plainchant" <note>original: *cantus planus*</note> by some.

The second is counterpoint, which is known to have been called "organization" <note>original: *organizationem*; a reference to *organum*, the earliest form of Western polyphony</note> by the ancients; and the third is figured song <note>rhythmic, polyphonic music</note>, which is called "organ song" by many. Therefore, we will divide this compendium of ours according to this threefold consideration. In the first consideration, we will provide three main things. First, we will show the correct divisions of the instrument composed by art <note>the monochord</note> to those being taught, and we will advise them to commit to memory the sound of the strings according to those divisions. Second, we will discuss the natural organ <note>the voice</note> through "arsis" and "thesis"—that is, through raising and lowering, or through tension and relaxation...

<summary>This page defines music as the theoretical logic behind the practical blending of sounds (harmony) and introduces the classic Boethian divisions of music into mundane, human, and instrumental categories.</summary>
<keywords>harmony vs music, musica mundana, musica humana, musica instrumentalis, monochord, Aristotle, cantus firmus, plainchant, counterpoint, arsis and thesis</keywords>

<meta>The text continues from the previous page, completing a sentence regarding the practical instruction of singing with instruments. Image quality is high, and the text uses common 16th-century abbreviations (e.g., '7' for 'et', tildes for omitted 'm' or 'n').</meta>

# Chapter Two

...joining [the singing] with the instrument of the art, we shall accustom [students] to sing in harmony until they have learned to sing correctly without it. Thirdly, we will clearly describe the melodies or notes by which every song can be recognized, sung, and composed. Truly, because there are diverse kinds of instruments established by art, lest the discipline become more obscure through variety, we will give a single method and rule for dividing strings. From this, the name "monochord" <note>original: "monochordi"; from the Greek 'monos' (single) and 'chorde' (string). It was a one-stringed tool used to teach musical intervals.</note> was taken by the Greeks; afterwards, passing through other things, we shall arrive at the intended end.

->The elementary division or composition of the monochord<-

**R**egular monochords are precisely divided by numbers and measurements by Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), a philosopher whose works on music theory were the standard textbooks for centuries.</note>. But just as that is useful and pleasant for theorists, so it is laborious and difficult to understand for singers. However, since we have promised to satisfy both, we will provide a very easy division of the regular monochord. Let no one think we found this with little effort; indeed, we discovered it with sweat through many sleepless nights, reading the precepts of the ancients and avoiding the errors of the moderns <note>original: "neotheicorū", likely a variant of 'neotericorum', referring to contemporary or recent theorists whose methods the author finds flawed.</note>; and any man, even one only moderately educated, will be able to understand it easily. Therefore, let a string <term>chorda: a gut string or wire used for a musical instrument</term> of any length be taken, which is stretched over a piece of wood having some hollowness [to act as a resonator]. Let the furthest point to which the string is tied be marked with point **a**. Let the other location, placed in the region from which the string is pulled and twisted <note>referring to the tuning peg or tension mechanism</note>, be marked with point **q**. Now, let the quantity **q-a**, that is, the length of the entire string, be divided into two equal parts, and let the point of equal distance be noted with the letter **b**. Again, we divide the quantity of the string **b-a** in the middle, and in the middle of that division we establish **d**. The quantity **b-d** shall be cut again, and **f** will be placed in the middle of the section. Understand that the same is to be done for the other half of the string, namely **b-q**, since in the first division the middle spot will be represented by **p**; and in the division of **b-p**, let the letter **l** be placed at an equal distance from both; and between **l** and **p**, observing the same rule of intervals, let **n** be inserted. And if we divide **f-n** through the middle, we shall mark it with the letter **i**. By this middle division, however, we shall not proceed further to smaller parts until we have made other divisions. But we will divide the whole **a-q** into three parts, and measuring from letter **q**, **m** shall be placed at the end of the first third <term>triens: a mathematical term for one-third of a whole</term>, and **e** at the two-thirds mark <term>bes: a mathematical term for two-thirds of a whole</term>. Then, let **e-q** be divided again into three, and coming from letter **q** toward **e**, the sign of a "square b" <note>original: "b. quadruz"; this refers to 'B-natural', written as a square-shaped 'b' (♮), as opposed to the rounded 'b' for B-flat.</note> will be fixed at the two-thirds mark.

<summary>This page introduces the monochord as a foundational tool for singers and provides a step-by-step mathematical guide to dividing its string to find specific notes. The author contrasts his "easy" method with the more complex theories of Boethius, positioning it as a practical guide for both theorists and performers.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, Boethius, music theory, string division, musical intervals, B-natural, geometry in music</keywords>

# Chapter Three

By the quantity of the <term>square B</term> <note>original: "b. quadr."; this refers to B-natural</note> and with the letter q doubled, let it be marked as b. But again, we shall cut the space between m and b through the middle, and we will mark the middle point of the section with the letters z and k. If we double the quantity of z, k, and q, we place the letter c at the end of the doubling. But let g be situated between e and square b with equal spaces on both sides. If, however, we divide the quantity of g into two equal parts, it shall be marked with the letter o. Thus, the whole <term>monochord</term> <note>A one-stringed instrument used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</note> is divided by a proper partition, as you may recognize in the figure below.

->A correct understanding of the given divisions<-

**Otherwise**, now that the monochord has been divided, it remains to consider those quantities more deeply and precisely. It should be known, therefore, that the entire space between a and b is called a **tone**, and by the Greeks it is called a <term>phtongos</term> <note>original: "phtongon"; Greek for a musical sound or pitch</note>, which among us is translated as "sound." This will be more easily understood by the following example: let the string be struck while extended in its full length and let the sound be noted; then let a finger or some other thinner object, not spread over a great width, be placed under it. When the string is struck again, it will happen that it emits a somewhat higher sound. When you wish to compare the higher sound to the lower sound (or the lower to the higher), the distance is that of a **tone**. But that which you will compare as a **semitone** occurs not because it is half of a tone, but because the voice does not ascend to the full integrity of a tone; it is called an "imperfect tone." However, the practical singer should not be overly worried about this imperfection of the semitone, since it is mostly a matter of theory <note>original: "speculativa"</note> and foreign to practical singers. But the practical singer ought to know that it is not a perfect tone; for we shall defer subtle disputes of this kind to the second book. 

That quantity or space which extends between c and d is a tone, and between d and e it is likewise a tone. But between e and f it is a **semitone**; between f and g a tone; between g and h a tone; between h and i a semitone; between i and the square k a semitone; between the square b and k a semitone; between k and l a tone; between l and m a tone; between m and n it becomes a semitone; n to o is a tone; o to p is a tone. And so the sounds which come from the striking of related and neighboring divisions, when compared to one another, emit a tone or a semitone. 

But the comparison of distant tones produces several species of tones or semitones. Hence, the space between c and e, because it consists of two tones, is called a **ditone** or **bitone** <note>A major third</note>. But the distance from a to c is called a **semiditone** <note>A minor third</note>, because it is an imperfect ditone made by removing half a tone. However, the distance from b to e is called a <term>diatessaron</term> <note>A perfect fourth</note> because it is capable of four voices, or it is called a **tetrachord** because it is a division and interval of four strings. For there is one string or voice at b, a second at c, and a third...

<vocab>monochord, tone, semitone, sound (phtongos), ditone, bitone, semiditone, perfect fourth (diatessaron), tetrachord</vocab>

<header>Chapter Three</header>

## Chapter Three

The fourth <note>This refers to the *diatessaron*, or the interval of a perfect fourth</note>, within which three intervals are enclosed: namely, two [whole] tones and one semitone. Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works on music became the standard textbook for the Middle Ages</note> divides the monochord <note>A scientific instrument with a single string used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</note> by tetrachords <note>A scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth</note> in this manner: **b.c.** is the first tetrachord; the second is **e.h.** This is done so that **e** is both the end of the first division and the beginning of the second. Similarly with **b.l.** When the end of the preceding [tetrachord] is taken as the beginning for the following one, it is called a "conjoined" <note>original Latin: *conjunctum*</note> tetrachord, which the Greeks call **synemmenon** <note>Meaning "linked" or "connected"</note>. 

However, when **h**—the end of the first or preceding tetrachord—is not used as the beginning of the second or following one, but the next tetrachord instead takes its start from **a. b-natural** <note>The transcription "qdr." likely refers to *quadratum*, indicating a "square" or natural B</note> and, proceeding through **k. l.**, stops at **m.**, it is called "disjoined" in Latin, but in Greek it is named **diezeugmenon** <note>Meaning "separated" or "disconnected"</note>. This is because those boundary tetrachords are not coupled together without a gap; rather, the beginning of the second is separated from the end of the first by the distance of a whole tone. 

There is also a fifth tetrachord, **m. p.**, which is called **hyperboleon**, meaning the "highest" <note>original: *excellentium*</note>; none of the strings of the higher tetrachords surpass these strings in the height and loftiness of their pitch. 

The first tetrachord is called **hypaton** in Greek, which means the "principal" or "lowest" in Latin <note>In ancient Greek music theory, the "highest" strings physically were the "lowest" in pitch, as the instrument was held with the low notes at the top</note>. The second tetrachord is **messon** in Greek, or "of the middle" in the Roman [Latin] tongue. Harmony is divided in this way because, with nature as our teacher, it is so governed that after two tones, it is tempered by a semitone. 

The strings, whose names vary, are eight in number. These are: **Hypate**, **Parypate**, **Lichanos**, **Mese**, **Paramese**, **Trite**, **Paranete**, and **Nete**. 

**Hypate** is called the "principal." From this, the Greeks call a consul *Hypatos*. **Parypate** means "next to the principal." **Lichanos** is so named because, according to the opinion of Boethius, it was struck or touched by the index finger, which is called *lichanos* in Greek, while the thumb struck the **hypate**. **Mese**, meaning "middle," is so named because it is placed in the middle position in a seven-stringed instrument <note>original: *heptachordo*</note>. **Paramese** means "next to the middle"; but **trite** is so called because it is the third note before the **Nete**. **Paranete** means "near the nete," and **nete** is the "lowest"—not indeed in its sound, but in name <note>Again, referring to its physical position on the instrument being the "bottom" string, even though it produces the highest pitch</note>. 

Furthermore, as these strings are arranged in different tetrachords, they are given different names with a certain addition. For they are called **Hypate hypaton**, **Parypate hypaton**, **Lichanos hypaton**, and then follows **Hypate messon**, **Parypate messon**, **Lichanos messon**, and **Mese**. Then **Trite synemmenon**, **Paranete synemmenon**, and **Nete synemmenon** for the conjoined scale. But the **Paramese** is disjoined from the **Mese** by a whole tone, and there follows the **Trite diezeugmenon**, **Paranete diezeugmenon**, and **Nete diezeugmenon**, followed by **Trite hyperboleon**, **Paranete hyperboleon**, and **Nete hyperboleon**. 

But because the **Mese** was not placed exactly in the middle position when there were fourteen strings <note>original: *nervi*, literally "sinews" or "nerves," referring to the gut strings</note>, a string was added: this is called **proslambanomenos** <note>Meaning "the added note"</note> or *prosodos* by the Greeks, but by our people it is named the "acquired," "assumed," or "added" note. For if to any tetrachord...

It provides the Greek etymology for the names of individual strings, such as Hypate and Mese, based on their physical position and pitch.</summary>

# Chapter Three

Let a species be added and let the **diapente** <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a perfect fifth</note> be formed: because it consists of five voices. It is also called a **pentachord** <note>a five-note scale or a five-stringed instrument</note> because it is composed of five strings, four intervals, and three whole tones with one semitone <note>a half-step</note>: as from *a* to *e*. But if a semitone is subtracted from the *diapente*, a **tritone** <note>an interval of three whole tones, famously known as the "Devil in Music" for its dissonance</note> is produced, as from *f* to *b-natural*. 

After the *diapente*, the previous intervals are repeated up to the **diatessaron** <note>the interval of a perfect fourth</note> in this manner: a *diapente* with a semitone (as from *a* to *f*); a *diapente* with a tone (as from *e* to *h*); a *diapente* with a semitone (as from *a* to *g*); and a *diapente* with a ditone <note>two whole tones; a major third</note>, as from *e* to *b-natural* <term>b. q̃dr̃: b-quadratum, the square 'b' used to indicate a natural or sharp note</term>. 

But a *diapente* joined with a *diatessaron* composes a species called the **diapason** <note>the interval of an octave</note>: which in Latin means "through all" or "from the whole," since every harmony—which the Greeks call *symphonia*—and every distinction of melody is contained within it. Therefore, it was rightly said by the ancients that after the *diapason*, there is always a repetition; however, the seven intermediate notes have some distinction among them. Whence that line was wisely placed by Virgil in the sixth book of the *Aeneid*: 

*And also the Thracian priest <note>referring to Orpheus</note> in his long robe*
*Plays the seven distinctions of voices in rhythms.* 
<note>original: "Nec non thrabicius longa cū ueste sacerdos. Obloquitur nuēris septē discrimia uocū."</note>

Indeed, the eighth note is similar to the first; therefore Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great, traditionally credited with organizing the system of chant and musical notation</note> established seven different letters and no more, but repeats them and places them again because the greatest affinity and similarity of form is found between the first and the eighth. This is so much the case that they are not perceived to differ except by the diversity of sharpness <note>high pitch</note> and gravity <note>low pitch</note>: therefore a *diapason* from *a* to *b* was called "grave" but from *b* to *p* was "acute." Yet the acute *diapason* was also noted with the same letters by which the grave was marked. 

But it should be noted that while the **phthongi** <note>from the Greek 'phthongos,' meaning a musical sound or tone</note> are equal among themselves in sound, they differ in the size of the drawing, according to whether they have more or less gravity, or contain larger or smaller spaces. As a result, the tone *a-b* has double the interval compared to the tone *b* to *b-natural*; and *c-d* similarly to *k-k*. And the same must be said of the semitones and other larger species, as is shown in the figure. 

But henceforth they should not be placed in this way, lest the understanding of beginners be obscured; but just as the tones are equal to each other in sound, so we shall place the distances between them as equal. However, we shall diminish the intervals of the semitones so that it may be clearly established that a semitone is less than a tone: the figure placed below will make all the aforesaid things clear.

# Chapter Three

**T**herefore, regarding the arrangement of the figure where we have printed the Greek names of the strings and the Latin letters: Although the entire range of musical difference is contained within a single octave <note>original: "diapason"</note>, we have nevertheless placed two, which he calls the double octave <note>original: "bis diapason"</note>, so that we might imitate the teachings of Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose work "The Fundamentals of Music" shaped medieval theory</note> and the Greeks. The *Enchiridion* <note>original: "enchiridion"; likely referring to the "Enchiridion Musicae," a famous early musical handbook</note>, however, calls it the double octave <note>original: "disdiapason"</note>. 

From these things, the error of those who begin poorly is made manifest; for they begin thus: there are twenty letters—$\Gamma$, A, B, C, D, E, F, G, a, b, c, d, e, f, g, aa, bb, cc, dd, ee. In truth, there are not twenty, but only seven distinct letters, after which no others follow but rather the same ones are, as it were, born again. But these people begin from the letter $\Gamma$ <note>the Greek letter Gamma, used to represent the lowest G</note> and end in the third 'c', because they falsely claim that Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great, traditionally credited with organizing plainchant</note> wished to place the first letter of his name there, and called it by the Greek name $\Gamma$ because the teachings of music were handed down by the Greeks. 

But if this is permitted to them, they still wander furthest from the truth in their division, when they say there are eight "grave" <term>low-pitched</term> notes, seven "acute" <term>high-pitched</term>, and five "super-acute" <term>very high</term>; for it has been shown that the eighth letter differs from the first only in its sharpness <note>meaning it is an octave higher</note>. 

Further, they try to fortify themselves with the authority of Boethius, as they say; for they say the first high note is the *Mese* <note>the "middle" note in the ancient Greek system</note>, which is 'h', and the letter 'a' is the second. It is gathered, therefore, that among the low notes, $\Gamma$ is placed by them. And if it is said by Boethius that $\Gamma$ is among the low notes, it was not well stated by Gregory because of the addition of the letter $\Gamma$. Just as the first 'a' relates to 'b', or the first 'a' to the second 'a', so it relates to $\Gamma$. But the second 'a' differs from the first 'a' only in pitch; therefore, $\Gamma$ would also differ from 'a' only in pitch. 

Therefore, if twenty letters were given, the division should be made so that there would be seven low, seven high, and six very high notes. But the addition of the letter did not please Gregory, since he used only fifteen. Tinctoris <note>Johannes Tinctoris, a prominent 15th-century music theorist</note>, however, being foreign to this reasoning, says the first $\Gamma$ is the lowest, the second is low, and the third is high. But we shall show shortly where this error invaded the singers. Now let us proceed to explain the teachings of others.

### A subtle application of the aforementioned figure for the use of singers

**G**uido the monk—who was perhaps a better monk than he was a musician—embraced all twenty of these letters, using the tetrachord <term>a scale of four notes</term> while composing the hexachord <term>a scale of six notes</term>. And he was compelled toward this kind of hexachord for this reason: because the number six <note>original: "senarius"</note> is called "perfect" by mathematicians. This is because its parts <note>factors</note>, when taken together, constitute the number six itself—namely 1, 2, and 3, which together make six. And each note of this hexachord is named from the first six syllables of six words <note>This refers to the hymn "Ut queant laxis," from which the syllables Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La were derived</note>.

<summary>This page critiques the traditional 20-note scale and the historical misattribution of certain musical naming conventions to Pope Gregory, while introducing Guido of Arezzo's hexachord system based on the mathematical "perfection" of the number six.</summary>
<keywords>Guido of Arezzo, hexachord, Boethius, Pope Gregory, Tinctoris, music theory, octave, Gamma, mathematical perfection, tetrachord</keywords>

<note>original: "gltorum h. m." — likely an abbreviation for "gloriosorum horum modorum," referring to "these glorious modes."</note>

# Chapter Four

The names of the notes were taken from the hymn of Saint John the Baptist, namely: *Ut queant laxis resonare fibris. Mira gestorum: famuli tuorum: Solve polluti labii reatum: Sancte Ioannes.* <note>original: "So that your servants may sing with relaxed vocal chords the wonders of your deeds, wash the guilt from our defiled lips, Saint John." This hymn was famously used by Guido d'Arezzo because each phrase starts on a successively higher note of the scale.</note> From this, if we look correctly at the first syllables after each phrase marker, we extract these six voices: **ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la**. Each of these is separated from its neighbor in the series by a whole tone, except for **fa**, which is separated from **mi** by the space of a <term>semitone</term> (a half-step). 

Therefore, the scale will have two tones below it and two above. When the first letter **G** is paired with the syllable **ut**, the whole note is called **Gamma-ut** <note>The Greek letter Gamma (Γ) was used to represent the lowest note of the medieval system; "Gamma-ut" is where we get the English word "gamut."</note>. Similarly, from the letter **A** and the syllable **re**, we get **A-re**; from the letter **B** and the syllable **mi**, **B-mi**; from the letter **C** and the syllable **fa**, **C-fa**; from **D** and **sol**, **D-sol**; and from **E** and **la**, **E-la**.

In order to imitate the teaching of <note>Boethius (c. 480–524 AD) was the primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages.</note> Boethius, who divides all harmony into <term>tetrachords</term> (groups of four notes), when the system reaches the fourth position, such as **C-fa-ut**, it sends out another <term>hexachord</term> (a six-note scale) like a new branch or offshoot. If the syllable **ut** is placed alongside **C-fa**, the entire compound is called **C-fa-ut**. This continues through **D-sol-re** and **E-la-mi**, where the first hexachord ends. 

But as the letter **F** follows, the syllable **ut** is placed again with the **fa** of the second tetrachord. This is the fourth voice of the second tetrachord. Thus, since it is **F-fa**, and **ut** is joined to it, it takes that name [F-fa-ut]. It is followed by **G-sol-re** and **A-la-mi**. 

Furthermore, so that he would not seem to have ignored the similarity of the outer notes that enclose an <term>octave</term> (the diapason), he begins to place the hexachord again. Since we have two voices located in the previous two tetrachords (specifically the second and third), the letter **G-sol-re** with **ut** added to it is called by that name, namely **G-sol-re-ut**. This continues to **A-la-mi-re**, where the third hexachord is completed. 

At this point, two notes are joined together as a pair: **fa** and **mi**. The first is **B-fa** and the second is **B-natural mi** <note>original: "b. qdr mi" refers to B-quadratum or "Square B." In medieval music, B could be "soft" (B-flat/fa) or "hard" (B-natural/mi).</note>. As the letters show, we recognize these two voices to be unequal, one being higher than the other; thus, that whole tone is divided into two semitones. 

The scale then continues with **C-sol-fa-ut**. Just as the second hexachord is joined to the first at that spot, the whole compound is named **C-sol-fa-ut**. It continues to **D-la-sol-re**, and so on through **E-la-mi**, **F-fa-ut**, **G-sol-re-ut**, **A-la-mi-re**, **B-fa**, and **B-natural mi**, just as before. 

These hexachords could be multiplied into infinity, according to the capacity of the instrument. But since in every science one must eventually reach an end, the repetition of hexachords now ceases. Therefore, in **C-sol-fa**, **ut** is no longer placed. Instead, we proceed to **D-la-sol**, where the sixth hexachord remains, while the seventh <gloss>the syllable la</gloss> completes it. 

Thus, he placed seven hexachords across seven voices, as seemed right to him, which the figure provided below will make clear. Do you see the correct figure of Guido? Ripe <note>Likely referring to another music theorist or a specific diagrammatic style.</note>, however, does not do it this way, but rather through measures <unclear>weight of</unclear>...

# Chapter Five

### A clear demonstration of the errors of certain individuals regarding the aforementioned matters

**A**lthough not all the points he has set forth regarding these matters have been disclosed by us, I do not believe the reader should think we have done so out of recklessness. Indeed, if God favors our prayers and efforts, we will address every disorganized and poorly digested point made by him and others in their proper places, so that not a single "iota" <note>A reference to the smallest letter of the Greek alphabet, meaning the smallest detail.</note> necessary to the subject remains untouched. Furthermore, we shall soon point out other things which they understood poorly and in which error deceived them.

It has indeed been stated and demonstrated above that the <term>tetrachord synemmenon</term> <note>original: "tetrachordū synemenon"; the "conjunct" group of four notes used to facilitate a B-flat.</note> is either joined to or disjoined from the <term>diezeugmenon</term> <note>The "disjunct" tetrachord.</note>. Certain people, however, remain ignorant of these things—as we investigated in a long dispute with our very close friend **Tristan de Silva of Spain**, a man of the keenest intellect. When they reach the <term>mese</term> <note>The central "middle" note of the Greek musical system.</note>, they place the *diezeugmenon*. After this, they place the *synemmenon*, then the <term>hypoboleon</term> <note>A term for an added "lowest" tetrachord.</note>, and in doing so, they cause the <term>nete hyperboleon</term> <note>The highest note of the standard Greek system.</note> to be distant from the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The lowest note of the system.</note> by three strings beyond two <term>diapasons</term> <note>The interval of an octave; "two diapasons" equals two octaves.</note>. This is clearly contrary to the truth and to the teachings of **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard textbook for a millennium.</note>. 

Boethius himself declares this most accurately in his first book, in the chapter beginning "But since he again places the *mese*," and it is further clearly explained with numbers and a measured diagram in his fourth book, in the chapter beginning "The two tetrachords which are joined to one another." But we shall speak of these things in our *Theories*; I would not have cited anything here except to show that the error of many regarding this is exposed, and that many people, being easily led, follow these same teachings.

**Marchetto** <note>Marchetto da Padova (fl. 1300), a highly influential Italian music theorist.</note>, however, does not do this, but places a disjunction after the *mese*; after which he places the *hyperboleon*, then the *synemmenon*. In this way, he arranges eighteen strings, to which he adds one below—namely **Gamma**—and another above—namely **E**—increasing the number to twenty strings. 

But if anyone wishes to understand him in a healthy way, they could, in my judgment, say that by subtracting that *synemmenon* tetrachord, the remaining parts are arranged in that manner. Then, after the *nete hyperboleon*, since it is similar to the *mese*, the *synemmenon* tetrachord is placed in the same way it ought to follow after the *mese*. This was arranged so as not to impede the *diezeugmenon*. He could be defended in many other ways as well; for my part, I do not doubt he is "saved," since Christ on the cross prayed for those who "know not what they do" <note>A witty, somewhat biting use of Luke 23:34 to suggest Marchetto was ignorant of the true musical system.</note>. A certain Brother **Johannes Carthusiensis** <note>John of the Carthusians (also known as Johannes Gallicus), a 15th-century theorist.</note>, a Frenchman, tries to defend him while simultaneously calling him unlearned and in need of the rod <note>In need of schoolmasterly discipline.</note>. As for me, I value this Marchetto only as much as four "little markets" <note>A pun on the name "Marchetus" and the Latin "mercatus" (market) or "marchetto" (a small coin/measure).</note>.

<summary>The author critiques various music theorists for their incorrect arrangement of the Greek musical system (tetrachords), specifically attacking Marchetto da Padova's twenty-note system as being contrary to Boethius's foundational teachings.</summary>

# Chapter Six

I do not doubt that Roger Caperon <note>original: "Rogerio caperon gallo"; a 15th-century French music theorist often criticized by his peers</note>, the Frenchman, could be swallowed whole in a single gulp. For this same Roger Caperon says: "There are four figures which are added in song: namely, *Coruph*, *synemenon*, *apotome*, and *crisis*." He calls the <term>Gamma</term> <note>the lowest note of the medieval musical scale</note> *Coruph*, because the *synemenon* <note>the "soft" B or B-flat</note> is added to it as 'b', and the *apotome* <note>the "greater semitone," the mathematical difference between a B-flat and a B-natural</note> as '♮'. But he calls the high 'e' <note>original: "e. superacutaz"</note> *crisis*, in which the followers of <term>Guido</term> <note>referring to Guido of Arezzo, the developer of modern musical notation</note> place the note 'la'. He is correct to say that the *synemenon* is 'b' if he understands it as the distance of a semitone from 'a' (that is, B-flat), though he does not state this clearly. He also speaks well regarding the *apotome* if he understands the distance between 'b' and '♮'; for this is what <term>Boethius</term> <note>a 6th-century philosopher whose "Fundamentals of Music" was the standard textbook for a millennium</note> and Philolaus <note>a Greek Pythagorean philosopher</note> call the "greater semitone." However, in this way, he sets down twenty strings, falling into the same error as others.

### A brief notice of various instruments.

Having moderately explained the regular division of the <term>monochord</term> <note>a one-stringed instrument used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</note>, it remains for us to teach how to alternately raise and lower the human voice by bringing it under the rule of this instrument. We will achieve this better if first, while following the tracks of the highest principles, a notice of various instruments is declared to us, so that we may know how to temper the natural "organ" (the human voice) with other instruments.

Of these instruments, some have such excessive tension that it thins the notes, or such looseness that it dulls them and sinks them into gravity <note>low pitch</note>. There are also strings that differ in both length and thickness, as found in the <term>cithara</term> and <term>lyre</term>, the <term>polychord</term>, the <term>clavichord</term>, the <term>clavicymbal</term> <note>a precursor to the harpsichord</note>, the <term>psaltery</term>, and many other instruments. New names have been given to these by posterity, and we shall make plain mention of them in the second book.

Nevertheless, all these cannot escape our system of division. Indeed, the strings of a monochord—if they are of the same thickness, length, and tension—will necessarily emit the same sound if struck at the same distance, just as we find with ancient monochords. But according to whether each string is struck closer to or further from the place where they are wound <note>the tuning pins or bridge</note>, it emits a lower or higher sound according to the proportions of the division given above. Now, however, not all strings are of the same thickness, nor are they tempered with the same tension. Therefore, if the frequent practice of music were to fall from memory, we could by no means find the truth of harmony through those physical monochords. Instead, we would have to return to the primary mathematical division to note the sounds. For if anyone wishes to tune their instrument harmoniously, let them turn to the sound of our theoretical instrument; once that is weighed and considered, they will recognize the truth. There are, however, some modern monochords that have only one...

# Chapter Six

The <term>octave<note>original: "diapason"</note></term> is divided toward the higher parts in this manner: since at least six strings are tempered in that way, and are of the same thickness, then the distance between the strings—whether large or small—produces the sharpness or depth of the tones and other species according to the measurement of the proportion. But those made this way are very easily tuned, because each sound is very easily brought into harmony with its own octave.

There are also other instruments whose strings are arranged in the opposite way. In these, the closer the finger is placed to the location where the string is wound <note>the tuning pegs</note>, the lower the sounds they return, and vice versa, such as the <term>lyre<note>original: "lyra"</note></term>. But this does not hinder our division; for the plucking of the string does not occur from the middle of the string—that is, from the place where it is wound to point 'b'—but from the place of the <term>bridge<note>original: "ligature," the point where the string is tied or fixed</note></term> to point 'b'. Thus, the closer the finger is placed to the bridge of the string, the higher the sound will be, because the string is shorter. And the closer it approaches the place where it is wound, the lower it sounds, because the string is longer. Therefore, if we wish to divide this instrument, the same result will occur by exchanging and transposing the letters: that is, let letter 'b' remain in the middle as before, but let 'q' be transposed to the place of 'a', and 'a' to the place of 'q', and let each of the remaining letters be moved into the place of the other.

Furthermore, a <term>whole tone<note>original: "tonus"</note></term> is divided into two <term>semitones<note>original: "semitoia"</note></term> in any new, perfect instrument. This is just like our <term>middle notes<note>original: "messes & paramesses"</note></term> through the <term>third string of the conjunct tetrachord<note>original: "trite synemenon," a specific note in the Greek pitch system</note></term>, a division of which we shall speak shortly. When we wish to produce a whole tone in such instruments, we must skip over two divisions of the strings.

In this instrument, therefore, divided down to the semitones, several strings are placed—some thicker, others thinner. Currently, five strings are used, arranged so that the thickest, in its full length, sounds a tone below the <term>lowest note of the system<note>original: "proslambanomeno"</note></term>, which we call <term>Gamma-ut<note>original: "gamaut," the lowest note of the medieval musical scale</note></term>. The second string, the <term>lowest of the low<note>original: "hypapate hypaton"</note></term>, is a <term>fourth<note>original: "diatessaron"</note></term> distance from it. The third, the <term>index-finger note of the middle<note>original: "lichanos messon"</note></term>, is a <term>major third<note>original: "ditono"</note></term> higher than that one. The fourth sounds the <term>middle note<note>original: "messe"</note></term>. The fifth emits the sound of the <term>second-to-highest of the disjunct<note>original: "paraneten diazeugmenon"</note></term> or the <term>highest of the conjunct<note>original: "netes synemeno"</note></term>, sounding an <term>octave and a fifth<note>original: "diapason & diapente," a compound interval of a twelfth</note></term> with the first string.

However, this is not done by necessity; they can be harmoniously arranged in other different ways. For example, the first could be the <term>lowest note<note>original: "proslambanomenos"</note></term>, the second the <term>index-finger note<note>original: "lichanos"</note></term>, the third the <term>middle note<note>original: "messe"</note></term>, and others elsewhere. These can similarly be located elsewhere at the will of the player. But because this [five-string] arrangement is now more commonly in use, we have set it down this way. In other instruments that sound by breath, the width of the <term>reeds<note>original: "calamorum," referring to pipes or flutes</note></term> creates the sharpness of pitch according to the same ratio mentioned above, and...

# Chapter Seven

...regarding depth of pitch: therefore, pipes <note>original: *calami*, literally "reeds" or "stalks," used here for organ pipes or recorders</note> which are twice as large will sound an <term>octave</term> <note>original: *diapason*; the interval of an eighth</note> lower. Other pipes of intermediate size will produce lower or higher sounds according to their greater or lesser thickness, provided that the opening where the sound is created corresponds to both the length and the thickness. 

There are also flutes <note>original: *fistule*</note> and <term>pipe-harps</term> <note>original: *sambuce*; while often a stringed instrument, the context of "holes" suggests a wind instrument like an elderwood pipe here</note> in which length creates the difference in pitch; for these have at least eight holes. These are opened so that they may all be covered by the fingers; for if there were more, they would be useless, because either the lower holes could not be closed, or the upper ones would remain uncovered and emit a sound we did not intend. Therefore, the closer the holes are to the end-opening, the lower the sounds they return; and the closer they approach the mouth of the player, the higher they cry out. 

But if half of any hole is covered by the finger, it creates a <term>semitone</term> <note>the smallest interval in traditional Western music, a "half-step"</note> compared to the full opening. There are other instruments of this kind which are different, however, because they have only four holes plus the mouthpiece; and with those four, they encompass any song in high and low pitch, which is most wonderful. But this happens because a single hole can produce the sound of a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: *diapente*; the interval of a fifth</note>, an <term>octave</term>, both together, or even a double octave, whether above or below. This occurs if the breath is emitted with double, triple, quadruple, or one-and-one-third the pressure. 

But those desiring full knowledge of these <term>instruments</term> <note>original: *instrumentis*</note> and their inventors, and the speculation of how they gradually arrived at perfection, should seek out our *Theory*, in which they will find wonderful things most pleasant to know. If we had included those things in the small volume of this first book, it would have made the teaching more difficult. Therefore, having set these things aside, we shall come to the remaining "natural instrument" [the human voice], as we promised:

## A subtle method of joining the voice with the instrument:

Those unwilling to imbue us with this teaching often put those things that should come first at the end, and vice versa. Thus, when they want to prove something that will follow from what has been said, they realize they have made no mention of it in the proper places, and they insert it elsewhere where it fits less well. Therefore, that style of teaching or subject matter is called "formless" or "chaos" and "confusion." 

We, however, do not think one should proceed in this way. Instead—as one can be led more easily from one point to the next—we have arranged these matters in the most suitable disciplinary order to serve the understanding. Thus, having looked with a moderate understanding at the instrument made by art, we wish to explain the "natural instrument" [the voice] through an orderly discourse and a more refined arrangement. Therefore, we advise that the student hold...

# Chapter Seven

After the evaluation has been made by us, let the student place the **monochord** <note>a single-stringed instrument used to teach musical intervals</note> before him. Striking the string, let him emit a voice in unison with it. Then, with a finger placed upon the second letter, namely **b**, and comparing the string with the wood <note>the neck or bridge of the instrument</note>, let him strike **b** upon the string and note the quality of the sound. Then, let him emit a voice in unison and equal to the sound of the string. And so, ascending in order through the other letters as far as the *mese* <note>original: "messez"; the middle note in ancient Greek music theory</note>, let him emit voices, and in the same way let him go back down. For in this way, he will be able to do this from any letter to its octave. 

However, since it is necessary for the student to have faith in the teacher, we wish to bind those being instructed by certain rules, so that we command them to begin singing not from just any letter, but from the letter **c** up to the next **c**. Nor should they touch the first **b** <term>♭</term> <note>the "soft" or flat B</note>, but the second <term>♮</term> <note>the "hard" or natural B</note>, which is a whole tone away from **a** both in ascent and descent. 

But someone will ask: what should one utter with the mouth? A word, or only a sound? It is said that it does not matter for the first or second time which of the two you do, but it is only necessary to note the sounds and the distances between the sounds, which are included in the eight voices comprising the <term>diapason</term> <note>the octave</note>. But afterward, so that the memory may recall the sounds, let each one be uttered with a different name. This was the custom among the ancients; for instance, <term>Odo</term> <note>Odo of Cluny, a 10th-century music theorist</note> used to say *noe noë anne cane agis* <note>meaningless chant formulas used to remember melodic shapes</note>, which signify nothing. Others indeed used *tri pro de nos trite*, which signified the series of modes about which we will speak in their proper place. 

Others, however, used only the letters of the alphabet, namely **a. b. c. d. e. f. g.**, such as <term>Gregory, Augustine, Ambrose, and Bernard</term>. <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo, who developed the system of solmization</note> however, used <term>Γ</term> <note>Gamma-ut, the lowest note of the medieval system</note>, *mi, fa, sol, la*, as we have said before. Although he did that by chance—since he also demonstrates all his examples with letters—his followers afterward clung to those syllables so much that they think they are absolutely necessary to music, which is laughable. 

Therefore we, who have labored long in studying and keeping watch to inquire into the truth of this art, assign new words to the individual strings, denoting the effect of the whole harmony. Thus, in the lowest voice let **psal** be said; in the second **li**; in the third **tur**; in the fourth **per**; in the fifth **vo**; in the sixth **ces**; in the seventh **is**; in the eighth **tas**. And thus the conclusion of the syllables will be: *psallitur per voces istas* <note>Latin: "It is sung through these voices." The author proposes this 8-syllable phrase as a mnemonic for the octave scale.</note>, since the whole harmony is made of eight voices. 

We place them from the low letter **c** to the high letter **c** because they teach one to sing perfectly. Therefore, they must begin from the letter **c**, because the song begins from that same letter, and the first semitone is enclosed between these two marks ⸣ ⸣; and the second sounds between these two marks ∫ ∫. The first [semitone] is therefore **e–f**, namely **tur–per**. Through the <term>♭</term> <note>flat</note>, a <term>♭</term> semitone sometimes occurs from **a** to **b** [flat], and sometimes from **a** to the [natural] <term>♮</term> [leading] into the high **c**, on account of the <term>tetrachord synemmenon</term> <note>the "conjoined" tetrachord including B-flat</note> and <term>diazeugmenon</term> <note>the "disjoined" tetrachord including B-natural</note>.

<summary>This page explains how to use the monochord for vocal practice and critiques traditional solmization systems (like Guido's), proposing a new eight-syllable mnemonic system: "psal-li-tur-per-vo-ces-is-tas."</summary>

# Chapter Seven

And if your <term>scitonia</term><note>A term used by Ramos de Pareja to describe the division of the musical scale or the placement of notes on his specific system.</note> is located there in divisions, those three places are denoted by the letter *f*. This is *ces is tas* <note>These are specific syllables in Ramos's experimental solmization system, which used the phrase "psal-li-tur-per-vo-ces-is-tas".</note>. From these sounds, sounding equal with the strings of an instrument, we will easily be able to tune the natural instrument [the human voice] with the one made by art [the musical instrument]. But if we wish to climb above the <term>diapason</term><note>An octave.</note>, we will place the syllable *psal* on that same sound as before. The two-syllable phrase *ces tas* will correspond with *psal*, and those following with *vo*, *li*, and with *tur*, and the rest as before. The same must be done in the bass, because as we have often said, after the octave the scale ascends again; and as often as we transcend or descend beyond the octave, we renew the syllables.

Regarding these eight notes, therefore, the teaching is correct. When the song has reached the octave by these steps, let the singer gradually lower the voice by the same steps and reversed syllables, and let them do this for a long time until they have learned to do the same without striking the <term>monochord</term><note>A one-stringed instrument used to teach intervals and tuning.</note>. When this is accomplished, let them ascend again from the first to the second, and again from the first to the third, singing both <term>mediately</term><note>Singing every note in the sequence (1-2-3).</note> and then <term>immediately</term><note>Skipping the middle notes to sing the interval directly (1-3).</note>, and descend from the third to the first mediately and then immediately. In the same way, from the first to the fourth, mediately and then immediately, both in rising and falling; and in the same manner from the first to the fifth, let them proceed mediately then immediately in raising and lowering the voice. 

But just as they did from the first to the fifth in raising and lowering, so let them strive to <term>jubilate</term><note>To sing with joy or practice melodic extensions.</note> from the second to the sixth, from the third to the seventh, and from the fourth to the eighth. In this exercise, however, we always command that a "foot be fixed" <note>To pause or establish a steady foundation.</note> on the fifth note, and this for many reasons which will be discussed in the <term>tropes</term><note>The musical modes or categories of melodies.</note>. Similarly, when one ascends from the beginning to the octave, let them rest on the fifth note, and starting again on that same sound, reach the octave, so that they say "it is sung through *vo*" <note>original: "psallitur per uo"</note>, and after a quiet interval is made, let them say *vo ces is tas*, often lowering it *tas is ces vo*. 

Afterward, with the voice released after an interval, let them say *vo per tur li psal*; then from the first note let them stretch the voice to its octave, namely *psal tas*, and conversely lower it *tas psal*. In these and in other things, let each person take as much exercise for themselves as they see is necessary for singing promptly and with ease. But so that the pronunciation of the sound is not hindered by a multitude of letters in a single syllable, we wish that singers remove the letters that follow after the vowel if they cause harm, such as *s* and *p*. The *p* that precedes *s* should always be removed, since here we are teaching music, not <term>orthography</term><note>The rules of correct spelling.</note>. 

However, someone might doubt, and not without merit, why eight distances [intervals] are placed when there are only seven differences [distinct notes], as has been handed down by us; they might think I have committed an error. I will say that although there is the greatest conformity and similarity between...

->g<-
<margin>the first and the eighth</margin>

<meta>image quality: high; catchwords: none visible. This page continues the discussion of musical theory, transitioning from the physical production of speech sounds to the mathematical and cosmological justification for an eight-note system.</meta>

# Chapter Seven

We have asserted that sounds differ in highness and depth, and we have never denied it. Therefore, we have demonstrated both the differences and the similarities between them when we placed the same vowel letters—specifically 'a'—to show their similarity and agreement. However, we have revealed the difference in highness <term>acumen: high pitch or sharpness</term> and depth <term>gravitas: low pitch or weight</term> using other letters, such as 's' or 'c' at the beginning of a word. 

Once these differences are understood, we see that a sound is deep when the breath is drawn from the depths of the chest, whereas a sharp sound is emitted from the surface of the mouth. The deeper the pronunciation occurs near the lungs, the lower it sounds; and the closer it comes to the teeth, the sharper it sounds. Thus, we recognize that the letter 'r' joined with 'a' is made through the contact of the tongue and the closing of the teeth. But we do not doubt that 's' joined with 'a' is produced by applying the tongue to the palate. 

It is clear from what has been said that the word *sal* is deeper than *sas*. If we weigh the difference between depth and highness in the pronunciation of 'l' and 's' placed at the end of a syllable, it will be established that our placement of them is most appropriate. For the "liquid" letter 'l' naturally emits a deep sound, while the thickness of the letter 's' rises into highness like a hiss. Indeed, no voice is sharper than a whistle.

However, if one were to argue about the number—claiming that our "octonary" <note>octonarius: a group of eight</note> does not seem to have as much value as the "septenary" <note>septenarius: a group of seven</note> by which the world and the heavens revolve—they might ask why Gregory <note>Likely referring to Pope Gregory the Great, traditionally associated with the development of Gregorian Chant and the seven-letter naming system (A-G).</note> only used seven letters. They might also argue that eight lacks the perfection of the "senary" <note>senarius: a group of six</note>, for the sake of which Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo, the medieval theorist who created the "hexachord" system of six notes (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la).</note> reduced the voices to six. Since six and seven are considered more perfect than eight, and since the same things we do with eight can be done with them, it would seem that those who followed the six or seven-note systems had a more correct understanding than we who follow the eight-note system. 

To this, we must say that the number eight possesses great musical perfection and dignity, and it was not established without reason, but out of necessity. First, its perfection is proven by comparison: just as some prove the perfection of the seven because there are seven planets, we prove the perfection of eight by the same reasoning. When the firmament <note>The "fixed" stars; the eighth sphere of the medieval cosmos.</note> is added to the seven planets, the number eight results. In comparison to those other systems, a "graver" error is committed by leaving out what is most excellent: the eighth sphere. 

If we account for the silence, just as the Earth and its elements are the first and lowest, the spheres follow: 
1. The Moon 
2. Mercury 
3. Venus 
4. The Sun 
5. Mars 
6. Jupiter 
7. Saturn 
8. The Starry Heaven 

<note>original: "celum stellatuz"</note> It is this comparison [that justifies our system]...

<summary>This page discusses the physical mechanics of high and low pitches in speech and defends the use of an eight-note musical system (octave) by comparing it to the eight celestial spheres of the medieval cosmos.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the significance of numbers, transitioning from the number seven to a geometrical and musical defense of the number eight (the octave), citing classical authorities like Cicero and Macrobius.</meta>

# Second Treatise

...nor indeed does Marcus Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher.</note> do so in the sixth book which he composed *On the Republic* <note>original: "de re publica"</note>. He uses different words, but nevertheless to this same effect. And this is as much as concerns the number seven; but as for the number six, because these are mathematical ratios, we shall say that mathematical bodies must be submitted to thought and not to the senses. In these, the principle is the point, which has neither length, nor width, nor depth, nor height. This, being extended, creates a line which is of one dimension: namely, of length without width and depth, and it is contained by two points terminating the length on either side.

If you double this line, a second mathematical body is made, which is extended in two dimensions, namely in length and width, lacking height, which is called a surface. And this is contained by four points, given that two points are assigned to each of the two lines. If indeed these two lines are duplicated—as if two others were placed on top of the two underneath—depth is added, and thus a solid body is created, which without doubt is contained by eight corners. And we can see this in a die or tile, which is called a *cube* <note>original: "cubus"</note> in the Greek name.

To these geometric ratios let the natures of numbers be joined; for the <term>monad</term> <note>The number one, considered here as the indivisible source of all numbers and geometric points.</note> is thought of as a point, because just as a point is not a body but creates a body from itself, so the monad is not said to be a number but the origin of numbers. Therefore, the first number is the <term>binary</term> <note>The number two.</note>, which is similar to a line extended from a point under a double termination of points. This number—that is, the binary—doubled from itself makes four. The <term>quaternary</term> <note>The number four.</note> also, when doubled, returns the <term>octonary</term> <note>The number eight.</note>, which number imitates a solid body.

For we have said that two lines placed upon two lines create the complete solidity of a body with the dimension of eight corners. And this is why it is said among geometers that "twice two, twice over" <note>original: "bis bina bis," referring to 2x2x2=8.</note> is a solid body; Macrobius also mentions this in his book on the *Dream of Scipio* <note>original: "Somnium Scipionis," a famous late-antique commentary on Cicero that was a primary source for medieval science and music theory.</note>, in different words but containing the same meaning. And he concludes that, by an equal progression of numbers up to eight, there is the solidity of a body; and he follows this by saying that therefore, among the principles, they assign fullness to this number.

We therefore conclude that whoever cuts off or diminishes the eight voices from our music takes away perfection and fullness from it. Thus, the number eight is not imperfect, but in music, it is full and perfect, since it contains the whole, and the entire harmony, as has often been said, is enclosed within eight voices. Therefore, it is not in vain but by necessity that we use the octonary. So much for these things; now let us hasten to representing the voices with figures in plainchant <note>original: "in plano," referring to *cantus planus*.</note>.

<summary>This page uses Euclidean geometry and Pythagorean number theory to argue that the number eight represents "solidity" and "fullness." The author concludes that the eight-note scale (the octave) is the perfect and necessary foundation of music.</summary>
<keywords>octave, geometry, Cicero, Macrobius, monad, binary, quaternary, octonary, music theory, cube, solid body</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the Second Treatise of the manuscript. It follows the introductory material of the first treatise, shifting focus from the general theory of music to the specific nature of the human voice and how it can be represented visually.</meta>

# Second Treatise

->Second treatise in which is shown how and in what manner the voice ought to be represented on a flat surface<-

**N**ow let us distinguish between the musical voices. <note>original: "voces." In medieval theory, "vox" refers both to the human voice and the concept of a musical "note" or "pitch."</note> A voice is an unbroken striking of the air that reaches the sense of hearing. The human voice is twofold: one kind is **continuous**, while the other is **discrete**. <note>original: "uox continua" and "uox discreta." This classification comes from the philosopher Boethius.</note> Continuous voices are those that are joined by a common boundary, such as when someone strikes a string and twists it while it is vibrating; the result is that it sounds lower at the beginning and becomes continually sharper <note>meaning higher in pitch</note>, and thus the sounds of the low and high voice become continuous. This also happens in the groaning of the sick, and likewise occurs with certain people when reading, who, by keeping their voice continuous while reading, gradually ascend or descend in pitch. Concerning such voices, to use the words of <term>Boethius</term> <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages.</note>, we do not wish to treat them because they are excluded from the science of <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "harmonie scientia." Here meaning the mathematical study of musical intervals.</note>.

Discrete voices, however, have their own proper "places" <note>specific, fixed pitches</note>. Therefore, the discrete sounds of instruments and harmonic voices are subject to the rules of art. Boethius distinguishes the human voice in another way in his first book, calling it "incontinuous" and "discrete." He says that the **continuous** voice is that which we use when speaking or reading prose; for then the voice hastens and does not linger on high or low sounds. Rather, the continuous impulse of the voice works as swiftly as possible to get the words out, to deliver the meaning, and to express the speech. He calls the **discrete** voice that which we sustain in singing, in which we serve the melodic measures <note>original: "modulis," refers to musical intervals or patterns.</note> rather than the words. This kind of voice is slower, and through its melodic variations, it creates a certain interval—not an interval of silence or stillness, but rather of a slow and sustained song. 

To these, as <term>Albinus</term> <note>Likely referring to Alcuin of York (c. 735–804), a famous scholar of the Carolingian Renaissance.</note> asserts, a third difference is added which includes "middle voices." These are used when we read the poems of heroes; we pronounce these neither with a continuous run like prose, nor with the sustained and slower drawl of a song. Similarly, we use this when we read prayers, chapters, and lessons in church, about which we will speak more clearly in the treatise on <term>tropes</term> <note>original: "tropis." Here referring to the various melodic formulas or tones used for liturgical recitation.</note>. 

Therefore, since the voice is of the nature of "successive things," <note>A philosophical concept: things that exist only in time, where one part must vanish for the next to appear.</note> it exists while it is being made, but once it is made, it is gone. For this reason, it is difficult to represent it to the imagination through rules and shapes. A likeness of a voice cannot truly be made, especially when depicted on a flat surface, because when it is uttered, it does not move like a "flowing point" creating a line in length only, or in length and width like a line and a surface. Instead, it spreads out in a sphere <note>original: "in orbem."</note> and into space, so that it is heard by the ears through the six differences of position: that is, up, down, forward, backward, <insert>left, and right</insert>.

<vocab>Tractatus (treatise), uox continua (continuous voice), uox discreta (discrete voice), Boetius (Boethius), harmonia (harmony/music theory), musica (music), modulatio (melodic measure), cantilena (song/chant)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "voice" (sound) and classifies it into continuous (speech-like) and discrete (song-like) types, citing Boethius as an authority. It explains the difficulty of visually representing sound on a flat surface because sound moves through three-dimensional space and exists only in time.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, continuous voice, discrete voice, music theory, acoustics, liturgical reading, Albinus, harmony</keywords>
