<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical notation and the physics of sound, transitioning from general theory to the practical use of lines and clefs.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

# Second Treatise

to the right and to the left. For Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works became the foundation for medieval music theory.</note> attempts to represent the voice through a stone thrown into standing water; with waves excited by the throw of the stone fleeing outward in circles, so that it may be understood that the air is propelled by the voice just as the waters are by the stone. Therefore, the voice is not depicted on a flat surface, but the elevation <note>original: *elevatio*, referring to a rise in pitch</note> or tension <note>original: *intensio*</note> of the voice and its depression <note>original: *depressio*</note> or relaxation <note>original: *remissio*, referring to a lower pitch</note> is represented in a certain way. For just as in a geometric demonstration, a drawn line placed in the position of a geometric line does not lack width (though a geometric line is understood to lack it), so in our music, we show that very elevation or depression—which consists of an invisible point—by certain perceptible intervals of notes <note>original: *notularum*</note>. Therefore, when we see a following note above the first, starting from a low pitch <note>original: *gravitate*</note>, we are taught to raise the voice to a high pitch <note>original: *acumen*</note>. But if the second note is lower, we shall descend from the high to the low. And this method is to be observed in all the subsequent notes of the entire chant.

But since it is not easy to know how much elevation or depression of a note should be held in the voice, our contemporaries have devised the best method for this. For they decreed that four lines, or five, should be extended; and the progression from a line to a space and from a space to a line should be such as in a crafted instrument <note>likely referring to a monochord or a harp</note>, where the pitches follow one another continuously and succeed each other. And so that its distinct locations may be recognized on account of the difference of intervals and the inequality of the gaps, one of those lines is marked with this sign <note>original: *hº sigº*, an abbreviation for "this sign"</note> or this one <insert>𝄡</insert> <note>an early form of a C-clef</note>, the first of which demonstrates a low F, and the second a high C. And modern thinkers <note>original: *neotherici*</note> are accustomed to call these signs "keys" <term>clefs<gloss>from the Latin *clavis*, meaning key; these symbols "unlock" the pitch of the lines</gloss></term>, because they clearly demonstrate the locations. 

We shall therefore arrange five lines with the first sign marking the middle one as F; the C which we called "sol" <note>The author is using the Guidonian hexachord system: ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la</note> will fall in the space contained between the lower line and the following one; and on the first and second lines, D, which we called "la"; and in the space contained under the marked line, E, which we named "fa." On this line, therefore, is placed F, and in the space contained above it is G, "ut"; and it follows in the same way for the rest, namely: ut, re, mi, fa; so that "sol" falls on a line higher up, which is another C, where the other clef is located. But if we wish to do the same with this [clef] placed higher up, the same will occur. But if this clef is placed not in the higher but in the following position, we will place "sol" below the first line, and thus "ut" and "re" will fall on the marked line, which will also happen with the first, if it is placed on the fourth line from the top. Thus, neither the lines nor the spaces contained between the lines will always hold the same pitch, but rather according to whether the clef is placed higher or lower.

We shall therefore now mark a double <term>diapason<note>original: "diapason"; an octave, the interval spanning eight notes of a scale.</note></term>, one from the letter c to the acute (high) c, and another from that same note to the super-acute (very high) c, so that those beginning through various positions may also note the voices.

If, however, the reader cannot so easily run through the musical notes with the voices by raising or lowering them, let him return to the <term>monochord<note>A single-stringed instrument used by medieval theorists to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note></term>; and beginning from the third voice, let him ascend to its octave and climb to the third voice, as was said in the chapter "Many wishing" <note>original: "Multi uolentes"; likely a reference to a preceding chapter title in this manuscript.</note>. Therefore, everything that was said to be done with an instrument, he will know how to do in these notes without it just by looking at the notation.

->Explanation of Ficta Music, First Chapter<-

**In order**, **however**, that a fuller knowledge of these signs and notes may be had, we shall investigate some things regarding this matter more subtly. For other signs are usually placed in songs through which an unequal distance of the intervals <note>original: "intercapedinū"; the "spaces" or distances between notes.</note> will be recognized. One is written as a "round ♭," while the other is figured as a "square ♮." The first sign is called **soft b** or **round b**; the second, however, is **square ♮** or **hard ♮**. They are called "square" and "round" because of the quality of their shape, but they are called "soft" or "hard" because when singers using the <term>Letters of Gregory<note>The system of naming notes by letters (A, B, C, D, E, F, G), attributed to Pope Gregory the Great.</note></term> make a <term>semitone<note>A half-step, the smallest interval in traditional Western music.</note></term> between A and B, they call that B "soft." This is because in the <term>arsis and thesis<note>The "upbeat" and "downbeat," or more generally the rising and falling movement of a melody.</note></term>, when the leap is made through a semitone, the voice becomes "softer" than when it moves through a whole tone. Just as soft B is to square B, so also a <term>semiditonus<note>A minor third.</note></term> is "softer" than a <term>ditonus<note>A major third.</note></term>. Similarly, a <term>diatessaron<note>A perfect fourth.</note></term> is softer than a <term>tritonus<note>A tritone or augmented fourth, often avoided in medieval music due to its perceived harshness.</note></term>. As such, soft B is soft, square B is hard, and square B with a tritone is the hardest of all. 

From these things, the error of certain singers is evident when they speak of "soft B" or "square B." They err in two ways: first, because they sing using the <term>syllables of Guido<note>Referring to Guido of Arezzo’s solfège system (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la).</note></term> and not the letters of Gregory; therefore, they should not pronounce it as "soft B" or "hard B," but as *fa* or *mi*. Second, they do not make the correct comparison, for when they say "square B," they ought to correspondingly say "round B" <note>The author is arguing for consistency: if you name the shape (square), use the other shape name (round); if you name the sound (hard), use the other sound name (soft).</note>.

<summary>This page explains the use of the monochord for practice and introduces the concepts of "soft" and "hard" B (the origins of the flat and natural signs), clarifying the terminology used for musical intervals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the medieval gamut (the range of notes) and the rules for singing semitones, moving from the basic structure of the "Guidonian Hand" into the more complex territory of accidentals and "feigned music."</meta>

# Part One

And when they say "soft B" <note>original: *b molle*, the origin of the modern flat sign ♭</note>, they ought to say "hard B" <note>original: *b durum*, the origin of the modern natural ♮ and sharp ♯ signs</note> <insert>♮</insert> and thus the correct relationship will be established. This was the custom among the ancients who sang using Gregorian letters, which have their own specific terms, just as for the Greeks the terms *synemmenon* <term>synemmenon: Greek for "conjunct," a tetrachord that shares its first note with the previous one</term> or *diazeugmenon* <term>diazeugmenon: Greek for "disjunct," where a whole tone separates two tetrachords</term> are specific. But for those of us singing by our own expressions, the specific names will be "b" in the conjunct and "b" <insert>♮</insert> in the disjunct. However, the terms for creating a whole tone or a semitone are common to all.

But singers mark tones or semitones with these signs not only in the *paramese* <note>the "middle" note in the Greek system, equivalent to our B</note>; for they say that wherever *fa* is found without *mi*, there *mi* must be created, such as in *b-fa-♮-mi* <insert>♮</insert>. Likewise, where *mi* is without *fa*, many call this "feigned music" <term>musica ficta: the practice of using notes outside the standard medieval hexachord system to avoid dissonances</term>. Regarding this, Philipetus <note>likely an earlier music theorist, possibly Philip of Vitry or an obscure contemporary</note>, speaking rashly, says: "There is only one type of feigned music." Truly, however, he was ignorant of the fact that it must occur in at least two ways: for making *fa* out of *mi* is a different method from making *mi* out of *fa*, as will be said shortly thereafter. This is because the voices must correspond in the same way they are naturally placed. 

Therefore, when *fa* must be made from *mi*, they write it with this sign, namely the "round b" [♭]; but when they make *mi* out of *fa*, they note it with this sign, namely the "square b" <insert>♮</insert>. Therefore, according to them, this "soft b" <insert>♭</insert> will be located in five places: namely in *b-mi*, in *e-la-mi*, in the first *a-la-mi-re*, in high *e-la-mi*, and in the second *a-la-mi-re*. In these places, indeed, we shall say *fa*, lowered by a semitone from its proper place. But this [♭] or this <insert>♮</insert> [is used] in *c-fa-ut*, *f-fa-ut*, *c-sol-fa-ut*, high *f-fa-ut*, and *c-sol-fa*; in which places we shall say *mi*, raised by a semitone from its proper place. They also call these "conjuncts" <term>coniuncta: the transposition of a hexachord to a pitch other than G, C, or F</term> because of this: just as when the *trite synemmenon* is placed after the *mese*, the tone between the *mese* and *paramese* must be divided into two semitones; so too must any other tone located elsewhere be divided. 

They further add that any of these conjuncts is a hexachord <term>hexachord: a six-note scale (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la) used as the basis for medieval music theory</term>, just like the others previously established. And therefore, just as after *f-fa-ut* (in which, as has been said, *ut* is followed by *g-sol-re-ut*) where *ut* is placed again because of the stated rules, so it is in each of the locations. They define it in this way: A "conjunct" is making a tone out of a semitone and a semitone out of a tone; likewise, making a major third <note>original: *ditonus*</note> out of a minor third <note>original: *semiditonus*</note>, and a minor third out of a major third, and similarly for other intervals. And they speak correctly here, because these conjunct hexachords behave in the manner of the *diazeugmenon* and *synemmenon* tetrachords. 

Distant from true knowledge, Johannes Tinctoris <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), one of the most famous and influential music theorists of the Renaissance</note> says: "A conjunct is the positioning of 'b' [♭] or 'natural' <insert>♮</insert> in an irregular place." For if the sign of "soft b" <note>b-flat</note> were placed in *c-sol-fa-ut* or in another place where *fa* would be irregular, it would be positioned irregularly.

<summary>This page explains the medieval concept of "conjuncts" and "musica ficta," detailing how signs for flat and natural (soft and hard B) are used to alter tones and semitones across different hexachords. It highlights a theoretical disagreement between the author and the famous theorist Johannes Tinctoris regarding the definition of irregular note placements.</summary>
<keywords>musica ficta, conjuncta, hexachord, b molle, b durum, Johannes Tinctoris, Philipetus, semitone, ditonus, solmization</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory, specifically focusing on the structure of the Guidonian Hand and the introduction of "conjuncts" or chromatic alterations within the hexachord system. It follows a previous discussion on the basic intervals.</meta>

# Part One

...and yet it would not be a conjunct <term>conjuncta: a musical alteration or "accidental" that shifts the standard hexachord to start on an irregular note</term>. Thus, if a square B <note>original: "𝇩 quadrũ"; the symbol for B-natural or a sharp</note> is placed where *mi* is: but if a round B <note>original: "b rotundũ"; the symbol for B-flat</note> is placed in *B-mi*, a conjunct is made. And yet, the location of B itself is irregular, because it is an octave <note>original: "octaua"; also called a diapason</note> away from the round B. Therefore, when *fa* is placed in the location of *B-mi*, it is in the eighth position from the *fa* placed in the "trite synemenon" <term>trite synemenon: a specific note in the Greek Greater Perfect System, equivalent to B-flat</term>, sounding an octave consonance. Just as it will have the seven remaining individual voices corresponding to each other in octave consonance with the others: just as the *ut* of the *synemenon* <term>synemenon: the "soft" or "connected" tetrachord in Greek theory</term> is surpassed by a whole tone by the *ut* of the *diazeugmenon* <term>diazeugmenon: the "disjunct" or "separated" tetrachord</term>.

In the same way, the *ut* of this [system] will be a tone below *Gamma-ut* <term>Gamaut: the lowest note of the medieval scale, located at the tip of the thumb</term>, which is called "Retropolis" <note>literally "behind the city," referring to a position outside the standard hand chart, likely the back or base of the thumb</note> by these authors. Because while *Gamma-ut* is placed at the head of the thumb, "Retropolis" is placed behind it, namely in the first exterior joint of the finger, and it is followed in *Gamma-ut* by *re*. In *A-re*, *mi*; in *B-mi*, *fa*. But in *C-faut*, *sol*; and in *D-solre*, *la*. So too, for the *mi* that is placed in *C-fa-ut* to be raised, an *ut* is placed in *A-re*, and that hexachord <note>original: "exachordũ"; a six-note scale</note> is completed in *F-faut*. 

Furthermore, those conjuncts that lower a voice from its proper place by a semitone are called "conjuncts of soft B" <note>original: "coiuncte b mollis"; B-flat alterations</note> by these men. But those that are raised by the same semitone are called conjuncts of "square B" <note>original: "𝇩 quadrati"; B-natural or sharp alterations</note>. However, for a brief understanding of any conjunct, the following is important: once the location of the conjunct is known, we should step backward from it through the finger joints, saying *fa, mi, re, ut* for a "soft B," or *mi, re, ut, si* <note>the syllable 'si' was beginning to be used for the seventh note, though often the author sticks to the six-note hexachord</note> for a "square B." Thus, anyone who looks closely at everything that has been said will see the "Hand" composed in such a way: 

In the *Retropolis*, namely *ut*. In *Gamma-ut*, *ut-re*. In *A-re*, *ut-re-mi*. In *B-mi*, *ut-re-mi-fa*. In *C-fa-ut*, truly *ut-re-mi-fa-sol*. In *D-sol-re*, *ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la*. From which, all the way to the second *A-la-mi-re*, we will find these six voices in all signs or locations. After *A-la-mi-re*, it is resolved, for that which began first, ends first. And so in the second *B-fa-𝇩-mi*, there will be *re-mi-fa-sol-la*. In *C-sol-fa-mi*, *fa-sol-la*. In *D-la-sol*, *fa-sol-la*. And in *E-la*, *sol-la*. After this, they place another voice, namely *la*, which is a tone distant from that one, since it depends on the last of the joined notes. 

And so there were twenty-two locations. Therefore, after the addition of the conjuncts, the Hand is said to be "perfect," because the whole is correctly divided by semitones. But they themselves call it "perfect" because it contains three octaves <note>original: "triuz diapason"</note>; for the greatest perfection is denoted in the number three. This is because the whole is equal to its parts—both the quotients and the remainders—taken together, and no other number claims this dignity for itself <note>A reference to "perfect numbers" in medieval mathematics, which are equal to the sum of their divisors</note>. But they say this rightly only in part—namely, that the number three is perfect—but in this they err: because the "Hand" is not actually three octaves, as we shall show shortly hereafter. From these things that have been said, however, readers will be able to consider how confusion arose from the teaching of Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo, the 11th-century monk credited with inventing the staff and the Guidonian Hand</note>.

It follows from a discussion of how notes are mapped onto the musical scale.</meta>

### Second Treatise

...is. For he himself considered that wherever a <term>semitone|The smallest interval in medieval music, like the step between E and F</term> was to be pronounced, singers should utter *mi* and *fa*. Because of this, he placed <term>hexachords|Six-note scales (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la)</term> more frequently within <term>tetrachords|Four-note segments of the musical scale</term>, both conjunct and disjunct. Wherefore, he necessarily placed two or three <term>voices|In this context, 'voices' refers to the solmization syllables like ut, re, mi</term> in one and the same location, as was shown above. But we, having taken his foundation, have demonstrated mathematically that these same six syllables are necessarily composed in the predicted places. But enough has been said regarding the confusion of voices in one place arising from Guido's art; next, however, let us more subtly discuss <note>original: "discutiamus" (implied by catchword 'tiamus')</note> which of these should be uttered and which should be retained—what his followers call **mutations**.

### Chapter Four: Concerning the Permutation of Voices

Having recognized such variation of voices, it remains for us to learn precisely why they are placed in one location: whether we ought to pronounce all of them at once, or whether we should choose one while dismissing another in the same place. Regarding this, it must be noted that three of these syllables are called **ascending**—namely *ut, re, mi*—and three are **descending**—namely *fa, sol, la*. Whence Guido's followers say:

->*ut, re, mi* climb; *fa, sol,* and *la* descend.<-

And when the song rises high, they teach us to take an ascending voice in place of a descending one. For example, if we are at the <term>mese|The 'middle' note in the ancient Greek system, which medieval theorists equated with the pitch A</term> with the syllable *la*, and the song seeks higher places, according to their doctrine we ought to dismiss *la* and take up *re* or *mi*; then with *re* or *mi*, we can more easily reach the higher pitches. Likewise, when we are at the *paranete synemenon* or *trite diazeugmenon* <note>Technical Greek names for the pitches D and C in the medieval gamut</note> with the syllable *ut*, and the song glides toward the depths, they command us to dismiss *ut* and take up *sol* or *fa*; thus by singing with *fa* or *sol*, we can descend. And this is what they call **mutation**, saying: 

> *Mutation is the variation of one voice into another.*

Others define it thus: 

> *Mutation is the variation of two voices, equal to each other in pitch, through different properties at one sign and one note.*

**Permutation** occurs in two ways: either by necessity of climbing or descending, or for the sake of placing a semitone before or after. These, we know, always occur among voices that are of the same kind—that is, both syllables being ascending or both being descending. Other mutations are not like that, but are composed of one ascending and another descending voice. Therefore, there will be a triple mutation: one consisting of two ascending voices, another of two descending voices, and a third which uses both. 

Specifically, the mutation that occurs in ascending is that which is composed of ascending voices: as in **g sol re ut** <note>The pitch G, which could be sung as 'sol', 're', or 'ut' depending on the hexachord</note>, changing from *re* to *ut* or *ut* to *re*; and in **a la mi re**...

<summary>This page explains the practical musical theory of "mutation," the method by which medieval singers switched between overlapping six-note scales (hexachords) to navigate higher or lower melodies. It defines which syllables are used for climbing (ut, re, mi) versus descending (fa, sol, la) and introduces the formal definitions of these vocal shifts.</summary>

<keywords>mutation, hexachord, Guido d'Arezzo, solmization, ut re mi fa sol la, medieval music theory, gamut, ascending and descending voices</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical mutation (changing between hexachords) within the Guidonian system. It follows a likely introductory section defining the six solmization syllables.</meta>

<meta>Institution: Biblioteca Nazionale Centrale di Firenze; Shelfmark: Magl. A.7.35</meta>

# Part One

*mi re re mi* <note>original: "mi re re mi"; these are solmization syllables used to name notes in a hexachord, a six-note scale.</note> and in its octaves. That which indeed happens just as much in descending is certainly this: it consists of descending voices, such as in 
**c sol fa ut**: *sol fa fa sol*. In **d la sol re**: *la sol sol la*, and in its octaves.
Therefore, it always occurs between voices distant by a whole tone according to the order.
I say "according to the order" because, as was said above, just as *ut* is distant from *re* by a 
tone, so is *re* from *mi*, and also *fa* from *sol*, and *sol* from *la*. But when a mutation occurs, 
they are placed in an equal location. The third mutation, however, occurs in two ways: 
either in voices which, placed in order, are distant by a fourth <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a musical fourth.</note> or by a 
fifth <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a musical fifth.</note>. By a fourth in three ways: namely, *fa ut*, *ut fa*; *sol 
re*, *re sol*; *la mi*, *mi la*. By a fifth, however, in two ways, namely *la re*, *re la*; *sol 
ut*, *ut sol*. Therefore, when the "hand" <note>Referring to the Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic tool where different parts of the hand represent different musical notes.</note> is incomplete, such as in *Gamma-ut* <note>The lowest note of the medieval system.</note> or in *A-re*, in *B-mi*, or in *E-la*, a permutation cannot occur, because it does not belong to one voice but 
to two equal ones. For this reason, it also does not occur in either **b-flat** <term>soft-b</term> or **b-natural** <term>square-b</term>. <note>The text refers to the different forms of the letter 'b' used to indicate b-flat and b-natural.</note>

In the first art inspected, where there will be only two equal voices <note>Here "voices" refers to the syllable names (ut, re, mi, etc.) assigned to a single pitch.</note>, 
two mutations will occur: one from the first voice into the second, and 
the other conversely. But when there are three, they will hold this manner: from the first 
into the second and conversely makes two; and from the first into the third and 
conversely another two; and from the second to the third and conversely another two, and 
thus six will be had. But when we name an ascending voice second, 
they say the mutation is caused in ascending. But further, they add the property 
of that voice: namely, of the <term>square-b</term>, or <term>soft-b</term>, or <term>natural</term>. 
They call those hexachords "natural" <note>A hexachord is a six-note scale: ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la.</note> which are begun on either **C**. 
The <term>square-b</term> starts from **G**, while the <term>soft-b</term> starts from **F**. So that we may 
conclude with one example through which knowledge of the others may easily be reached, 
we say in **g sol re ut**: *sol re*, *re sol*, *sol ut*, *ut sol*, *re ut*, *ut re*. *sol re* in 
ascending from "natural" into "soft-b." *re sol* in descending from "soft-b" 
into "natural." *sol ut* in ascending from "natural" into "square-b." *ut sol* 
by the contrary. *re ut* in ascending from "soft-b" into "hard-b" <note>original: "♮ durū"; refers to the square-b hexachord.</note>; *ut re* in 
ascending from "square-b" into "round-b" <note>original: "b. rotundū"; another term for the soft-b (b-flat) hexachord.</note>. Indeed, by observing this manner in other 
places, anyone will be able to investigate these permutations for themselves through what has been said. 
But even with this foundation held in the "perfect hand" <note>A mnemonic device that is fully mapped out with all syllables.</note>, one will easily be 
able to make all mutations of all six voices placed in the same location. 
Wherefore, for the sake of example, let us take **d sol re**, where six voices have been 
located. Let us join *la* in the aforesaid manner, namely with voices which in order 
are distant by a tone, or a fourth, or by a fifth. And we shall say *la sol*, 
*sol la*, and we shall have two. Then with *mi*, namely *la mi*, *mi la*, and there will be 
four. And with *re*, which by joining in a fifth, we again make two more...

<summary>This page explains the rules of "mutation," the process of transitioning between different hexachords (natural, soft, and hard) in the medieval musical system. It details how many mutations occur at a single pitch depending on how many syllable names (voices) that pitch possesses.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of solmization (the system of assigning syllables to notes) and the "Guidonian Hand." It marks a transition to the Second Treatise, focusing on "mutations"—the method singers use to shift between different hexachords (six-note scales) within the larger musical gamut.</meta>

## Second Treatise

<note>original: "Tractatus secundus"</note>

...the **mutations**, namely: *la-re*, *re-la*; therefore we do not doubt that they arise when one syllable is dismissed and **sol** is taken. In the same way, six will be formed, namely: *sol-fa*, *fa-sol*; *sol-re*, *re-sol*; *sol-ut*, *ut-sol*; and *la-sol*, provided that *fa* is taken when the first is dismissed. This can only be joined with the syllable **ut**, and there will be fourteen. However, because **mi** combined with **re**, and **re** combined with **ut**, they produce four. Therefore, wherever six voices <note>The syllables ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la</note> are found, there will be twelve mutations; in **B-mi** there are only six; but four in **A-re**; in **Gamma-ut** you will have only two. In the same way, **B-fa / B-natural-mi** <note>original: "b fa ♮ mi"</note> is the same as the second **C-fa-ut**, and **E-la** is the same as **Gamma-ut**. So follow the other signs or locations throughout this extent.

### Chapter Five, refuting some preceding points and demonstrating the correct method of the joined notes.

<note>original: "Capitulum quintum reprobans aliqua precedentia: & rectum modum coniunctarum demonstrans." The term "coniuncta" refers to accidental hexachords used to navigate notes outside the standard set.</note>

Now that the <term>Hand</term> <note>The Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic device where different parts of the palm and fingers represent different notes.</note> has been perfectly arranged and the form of those things required for its perfection has been prescribed above, there remain for us some things to be investigated more subtly. For although it has been said that from **D-sol-re** up to the second **A-la-mi-re** six voices are found in any given location, and from those six voices eighteen mutations are caused—this is indeed true regarding the occurrences, but not at all regarding the mutations themselves.

For the sake of clarity, let a figure be arranged with the <term>voices of Guido</term> <note>ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la</note> from **Gamma-ut** up to **E-la**; this is called the <term>natural order</term>, because the voices are arranged naturally, just as they arise from the division of a regular <term>monochord</term> <note>A scientific instrument with a single string used to measure musical intervals.</note>. But let this same figure be arranged on its left side, raised by a whole tone, and likewise on its right side, lowered by a whole tone. From this arrangement we will find that any tone of the natural order is divided from another accidental one; and by this division, every perfect instrument ought to be divided.

For instance, between **A-re** and **B-mi** there is naturally a whole tone, because it is *re-mi*. But when, from the <term>accidental order</term>, a voice equal to the natural *re* is lowered by a tone as a *mi*, it follows that the voice *fa*—which follows that *mi*—does not reach the natural **B-mi**, since that voice makes a <term>semitone</term> <note>A half-step, the smallest interval in this system.</note>, whereas the other intends a whole tone. Furthermore, since there is naturally a semitone from **B-mi** to **C-fa-ut** (because it is *mi-fa*), and from the *re* of the left accidental order (which is equal to that *mi*) there follows a *mi* raised by a tone, it follows that it will be higher by a semitone than the natural voice *fa*. Thus, the natural whole tone sung from **C-fa-ut** to **D-sol-re** remains divided into two semitones.

Again, since there is naturally a whole tone from **D-sol-re** to **E-la-mi** (because it is *re-mi* or *sol-la*), and the natural *re* or *sol* is equal to the *mi* of the right accidental order, the voice *fa* follows. This *fa* creates a semitone by dividing that whole tone between **D-sol-re** and **E-la-mi**, and it will not reach the *mi* of the natural order. As the subtle reader says regarding these letters in this <term>semitetrachord</term> <note>A grouping of four notes, or here, a partial grouping within the scale.</note>: who shall do this, having inspected the figure placed more prominently above?

<summary>This page introduces the second treatise, focusing on the complex mechanics of solmization mutations and the "natural" versus "accidental" (or false) orders of the musical scale. It explains how shifting hexachords by a whole tone allows for the division of natural whole tones into semitones, effectively creating what we now call sharps and flats.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals and the division of the monochord, specifically focusing on how to mathematically determine the positions of chromatic notes (conjunctae) beyond the basic diatonic scale.</meta>

->Part One<-

# Part One

Someone might say that although these **conjunctae** <term>conjunctae: chromatic or non-diatonic notes added to the standard medieval hexachord system to allow for transposition or "accidental" notes</term> can be made at will, they cannot be measured. To this we briefly reply that they can be made as clearly and effortlessly as the natural order itself was made in the first figure. To examine this, let the first measured figure be arranged, then we will make the **soft b** <note>original: "b mollis"; refers to the flat sign (♭)</note> *conjunctae* in this manner:

Having doubled the quantity of Q <note>A reference point or the full length of the string on the diagram</note>, we will mark the first **soft b** [flat] in the joining; thus the first flat will be between A and B. Then let the quantity of the string between I and the first flat be divided in the middle, and let the second flat be marked, which will be between D and E. If you divide the middle quantity of the second flat and Q, we will mark the fourth flat between L and M; but with the quantity between the fourth and second flats divided in the middle, we will mark the third flat. If the quantity between the third flat and Q is divided, the fifth flat will be marked. 

We will have, therefore, from this division five **soft b** *conjunctae* weighed from a straight division. 

But if we seek to have the **square b** <note>original: "b quadratum"; refers to the natural sign (♮) or sharp sign (♯)</note> *conjunctae*, we will divide by three from B to Q. And coming from the letter Q toward B, at the end of the third part, we will place the fourth **natural**, namely between N and O. And at the **two-thirds point** <term>bes: a classical Latin term for a ratio of eight-twelfths or two-thirds</term>, the second **natural**, which falls between F and G. 

But if the quantity of the second natural is divided by three, coming from letter Q toward the second natural, at the two-thirds point we will place the third natural; it will be between K and L. If the quantity of this and Q is doubled, the first natural will result, marked at F. 

We will have, therefore, from this division four **square b** joinings resulting from a straight division, as is clear in the figure. If we wish to have a fifth, we divide the quantity of the third natural and Q in the middle, and it will be above P by two tones.

Truly, because we do not wish to add anything below the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos: the "acquired note," the lowest note in the Ancient Greek musical system (equivalent to A2)</term> nor above the **nete hyperbolaeon** <term>nete hyperbolaeon: the "highest of the highest," the top note of the Ancient Greek system (equivalent to A4)</term> in the measured figure, we do not place it there—not because it cannot be done, but because the ancients held this, and we find the teaching handed down thus by **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages</note>. 

When we wish to add something below or above, we can do it not on the same string but on different ones, tuning those strings with these divisions made correctly on one, corresponding in the **octave** <term>diapason: the Greek term for the interval of an octave</term>. For instance, if we wish to add one string below the lowest note, we will arrange it so that it sounds an octave with the **lichanos meson** <term>lichanos meson: the "index finger" note of the middle tetrachord, equivalent to G</term>, and it will be **Gamma-Ut** <note>The lowest note of the medieval gamut, one tone below the Greek Proslambanomenos</note>. 

And if another note is added below that, it will sound an octave with the **parhypate meson** <term>parhypate meson: the note F</term>; a **fifth** <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> with the **hypate hypaton** <term>hypate hypaton: the note B</term>; and a **fourth** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> with the first *conjuncta*. This note is the one that moderns call **retropolis** <note>literally "behind the city," a term used for notes added below the traditional Guidonian hand or gamut</note> as we have said above, with which almost all modern multi-stringed instruments found in Italy begin, including organs and other complete instruments divided by **temperament** <term>sinthonia: likely referring to syntonic tuning or a specific method of tempering the scale</term>. 

In our Spain, however, we find that ancient monochords and even organs used to begin on low C; but the modern multi-stringed instruments and organs place eight notes below low C in the natural order, though not yet...

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the musical scale and the system of hexachords, specifically focusing on the extension of the lower range and the rules for transitioning between note-names (mutations).</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>Second Treatise</header>

## Second Treatise

They have <term>conjunct voices</term> <note>original: "voces coniunctas"; these are notes outside the standard Guidonian hand, similar to accidentals</note> of B-natural or B-flat; not below the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>the lowest note in the ancient Greek musical system</note>, but only a true <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapent"; the interval of a fifth</note> below <term>Gamma ut</term> <note>the lowest note of the medieval gamut, G</note>, so that Gamma ut is the octave of G sol re ut. There is an octave, or <term>diapason</term> <note>the interval of an eighth</note>, for F fa ut, and another diapason for E la mi, another for D sol re, and another for C fa ut. We have now found a <term>polychord</term> <note>a multi-stringed instrument, possibly a harpsichord or large lute</note> here in Bologna that has an octave below D sol re, but one below C fa ut is found only in Spain. However, it does not matter where one begins, as long as the proportions of the strings and the divisions of semitones and tones are observed.

These <term>tetrachords</term> <note>four-note scales</note> are related to one another just like the <term>synemmenon</term> <note>the "conjunct" or joined tetrachord in Greek theory</note> and the <term>diazeugmenon</term> <note>the "disjunct" or separated tetrachord</note>. This is why these contemporaries call them "conjunct" voices, but they also improperly call them "disjunct" when they transfer from one <term>property</term> <note>a specific hexachord type: natural, hard, or soft</note> to another without a formal mutation. For example, if you were at C sol fa ut and sang "fa," and were immediately forced to descend to F fa ut and then to even lower notes, that descent is called "disjunct" because "fa" is pronounced in a higher voice and "fa" in a lower one.

Similarly, whenever a leap occurs across an octave, it will necessarily be "disjunct." I say "necessarily" for the octave because a leap of a fifth is not always necessary, except when the fifth is "diminished" <note>likely referring to a leap involving a tritone or a specific hexachord change</note>, such as from F fa ut to B-natural. But if the leap is made from A la mi re, starting on "re," and descends by the interval of a fifth, that "re" is immediately mutated into "la." It is then called "re la re" because that "re" follows well from that "la."

Likewise, in <unclear>geristes</unclear> with "ut," when a leap is made by an immediate fifth, it is not disjunct but is mutated into "sol," and is called "ut sol ut," because "la ut" follows well from that "sol." An immediate <term>tritone</term> <note>the "augmented fourth" interval, traditionally avoided in medieval music</note> always causes disjunction; for instance, if a leap is made from F to C sol fa ut, passing through a <term>nota ficta</term> <note>a note outside the standard "hand," an accidental</note>, then it is called F fa and B-flat/B-natural, and C fa follows, and then it is called disjunct. This is because that "mi" does not follow nor depend on that lower "fa."

Other leaps that are larger than a fifth always create disjunctions, both in ascending and descending, except where "la" can be taken in the hexachord. For example, if at A la mi re, "re" or "mi" were moved by a leap to C fa ut, then "la" must be taken... otherwise, any leap above a fifth will always be disjunct.

But we must see what was to be proven: whether eighteen <term>mutations</term> <note>the technical process of changing the syllable name of a pitch to move between hexachords</note> can be made in each of the locations through conjunct voices. If one looks closely at the diagram, he will very easily recognize that only in D sol re, G sol re ut, and their octaves will eighteen mutations occur. This is because in these places only, those six voices <note>ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la</note> are equally placed, as appears in the diagram. In other locations, however, because not all voices meet on the same line (some being placed higher and some lower), all eighteen will not occur. In E la mi, therefore, where "fa" and "ut" are lower, there are not eighteen, but only two in this manner: from "la"... only two from "sol"... because it can be paired neither with "fa" nor with "ut." From "mi" paired with "re" <unclear>paired</unclear> another two, and so six. But "fa" with "ut," because among equals... we will have others.

<summary>This page discusses the theoretical expansion of the musical range (the gamut) and the technical rules for "mutations"—the way singers switch between hexachords. It references specific instruments found in Bologna and Spain to justify the existence of notes lower than the traditional "Gamma ut."</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical mutations within the Guidonian hexachord system, specifically focusing on how accidentals (musica ficta) alter the standard intervals between notes like E-la-mi and A-la-mi-re.</meta>

# Part One

...two; and so there will be 12. Thus also in F-fa-ut where *la* and *mi* are equal, we will nonetheless have 12. We have them in this way: *la-mi, mi-la*, though there are only two. However, we do not doubt that they arise from the soft *b* <note>original: "sol b"; referring to the hexachord starting on F which uses B-flat</note>. And it is certain that four arise from the combination of *fa* with *ut*, and *re* with *ut*. Therefore, there are twelve in E-la-mi. But in A-la-mi-re, where only *fa* is unequal, there will be fourteen; this is because four must be made with it: namely, *sol-fa, fa-sol, fa-ut, ut-fa*. And similarly in C-sol-fa-ut. But in B-fa-natural-mi, twelve; and likewise in their octaves <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term>, as anyone can correctly see for themselves. 

It appears, therefore, from these things, that mutations <term>mutation: the process of changing from one hexachord to another to reach different notes</term> do not occur in all eighteen places. Thus the falsity of the Hand <note>The "Guidonian Hand" was a visual and mnemonic tool used by medieval singers to navigate the gamut of notes</note> is perfectly revealed, since it is not perfect across three octaves, because it exceeds by a semitone. For it is certain: it would be perfect if that last note of the left accidental order <note>likely referring to a specific column in a musical chart or the layout of the hand diagram</note> were distant by only a semitone from its neighbor. And then it would act against Guido himself and his followers, who say that between *sol* and *la* there is not a tone, but only a semitone distance. 

It should be noted from this that singing through an "accidental" order <note>singing using musica ficta or non-standard hexachords</note> is sometimes the same as singing through the natural one. The only difference is in the shape and the letters: that the semitone does not correspond to the same place as in the natural order. For if in the natural order the semitone was from E to F, by placing the soft sign *b* <note>the flat sign</note> in that same E, it is lowered, and the semitone happens from the letter D to E. Likewise, if the natural sign <note>original: $\natural$ (b-quadratum)</note> is placed in F, it is raised and the semitone happens from F to G. 

Truly, when we wish to sing through any of the accidental orders, a semitone follows after two tones. This is true for both the conjunct <term>conjunct: a system of two tetrachords where the last note of one is the first of the next</term> and the disjunct tetrachord <term>tetrachord: a scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth</term>. This was called *musica ficta* <term>musica ficta: "feigned" or "false" music, referring to notes outside the standard Guidonian gamut, often requiring accidentals</term>. And then one must be as careful with the natural as with the accidental, since the true followers of Guido, our contemporaries, do not do so. Instead, they frequently transfer themselves from the accidental to the natural and vice versa. 

Some perhaps do this by chance because they do not understand the proposition of the song; some do it by industry and shrewdness. But those who do this by industry mark the line or space with this sign *b* <note>flat</note>, or this $\natural$ <note>natural/hard b</note>, or this # <note>sharp</note>. And then, they follow that sign while singing. They say, however, that if the sign is placed at the beginning, that order must be observed throughout the whole song. But if it is not at the beginning but placed in the middle, they say that only that note to which the sign is applied is subject to the sign. 

Whence they make various considerations in raising and lowering the notes from their proper place. For if a note is in B-mi, and this sign *b* is shaped there, while an A follows it, or in C with this sign #; although in the natural order there is a semitone gap, because of the lowering of the first and the raising of the second, it transitions into the interval of a semitone. And so in all places where semitone distances occur. But if one melody in E-la-mi was preceded by this sign *b*, and A was in C with this sign #, the interval of a major third <term>ditonus: a major third consisting of two whole tones</term> is converted into a distance of a tone. 

They dogmatize that the same should be done in whatever places the aforementioned distances occur, and for other larger species of intervals. They say such an order should be kept so that the sign is always placed in the position of the "square" <note>original: $\square$ quadrati; the hard B sign</note> conjuncts, and the *b* in those where the round *b* <note>the soft B or flat sign</note> [is used]...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical and practical application of musica ficta (accidentals) within the hexachord system, critiquing the limitations of the traditional Guidonian Hand. It describes how singers use specific signs (flats, sharps, and naturals) to alter pitch and navigate between natural and accidental musical orders.</summary>
<keywords>musica ficta, hexachords, solmization, Guido d'Arezzo, tetrachord, semitone, mutation, Guidonian Hand, accidentals, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues a technical discussion on musical notation and the placement of semitones, specifically critiquing the theories of John of London and Guido d'Arezzo regarding the hexachord system.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>Treatises</header>

# Treatises

It is placed there: however, **John of London** <note>original: "Joāes vero de lodoïs"; likely the 13th-century music theorist</note> and others of lesser authority say: just as in the notes **B-flat** and **B-natural** <term>b fa ♮ mi: the dual name for the pitch B, which could be sung as 'fa' (soft/flat) or 'mi' (hard/natural)</term> both signs can be placed, so too it can be done in other places. Where neither *fa* nor *mi* <note>B-natural</note> can be made in this way, I do not think it should be attempted. Therefore, if by the aforementioned reasoning it is divided into two semitones, the remaining connections are in vain. For this is permitted, as has been said: so that we can have a tone and a semitone on any note, just as we say with **B-flat** and **B-natural**. For if there is a marked note in the notation with a **flat** <note>original: "b"; the soft-B or b-molle</note> preceding it, and in the same place there is a **natural** <note>original: "□"; the square-B or b-quadratum</note> preceding it, although the sound appears to be the same, nevertheless it is because of the proper lowering and the following raising of the semitone, just like *mi* and *fa*. 

They speak wrongly for two reasons. First, because even if it did not have the preceding sign, the second note would still be higher than the first. Second, they err because if the first note is lowered by another semitone from that place, and the second is raised by another semitone from that place while they are being held, it results in the size of a full tone and not a semitone. They say the same regarding the note **D** <term>dsolre: the note D in the medieval gamut</term> and its octaves. These things that have been said can be found in the <insert>treatises</insert> of many musicians, such that one may extend four or five lines <note>an early reference to the evolution of the four-line staff to the modern five-line staff</note> and mark some of those lines with either of the two aforementioned signs and arrange the notes. 

From this arises a dispute <note>original: "dīnīaz" for "differentiam"</note> proceeding from the teaching of **Guido** <note>Guido d'Arezzo, the father of modern musical notation</note> regarding the falling of notes. Some fall into a stubbornness of following him and hold it as certain that no semitone can be made between *fa* and *mi*. They say this because *mi*, closing the mouth, denotes obscurity, while *fa*, relaxing the mouth, signifies brightness. <note>Medieval theorists often linked the vowel 'i' in 'mi' with a closed, higher sound and 'a' in 'fa' with an open, lower sound.</note> But this is nothing; I will care to show the difference through the reasons of the masters of mathematics.

---

The difference in music consists in quantity or relationship. For the properties of the three voices <term>the three hexachords: natural, soft (b-molle), and hard (b-durum)</term> in the art of **Guido** are placed so that they differ among themselves. It is not necessary to place a difference between equal voices. For in the note **G** <term>gsolrut: the note G</term>, it is necessary that it creates a difference whether it is the 'sol' of the natural hexachord, the 'fa' of the soft-B hexachord, or the 'ut' of the hard-B hexachord. Thus, in the note **A**, they are not equal, and a mutation <term>the process of changing from one hexachord to another</term> could not happen in them. And yet they themselves do this through their own teaching already set forth above, which they must necessarily contradict. 

Certain people, like **Master Osma the Spaniard** <note>original: "ut mī: osmen\^b hyspā\^o"; likely referring to a scholar from the Cathedral of Osma</note>, who published a certain treatise on this faculty, think they are leading themselves to a discovery. If they did not see *fa* and *sol* in the note **C** <term>csolfaut</term>, they would not place them there, for it would be in vain to use many things where few suffice. Thus they place them there because they differ. So too the other notes located elsewhere; and this discovery is of no value because they place them there to make the semitone longer or closer, from which the musical difference arises. For if all intervals were equal, there would be no musical difference in tones or semitones. If I wished to solve this by calculation, I could do so with many prayers. They say the voices of the **square-B** <note>natural</note> differ from the voices of the **soft-B** <note>flat</note>, but not knowing how to differ from themselves, they agree with what we have proved by some examples. For in **C-flat** they clearly place *fa* and **B-natural**...

The author critiques the teachings of Guido d'Arezzo and other theorists regarding how different hexachords (natural, soft, and hard) overlap on the same pitch.</summary>

# Part One

<note>original: "n. ponūr. qz differētīā inter fa et mi magnā cogōscūt."</note> Indeed, they place it there because they recognize a great difference between *fa* and *mi* <note>In the medieval system of solmization, the syllables *fa* and *mi* always represent the semitone, or the smallest standard step in a scale.</note>. And yet, both voices are marked with a <term>square B</term> <note>original: "▢ qua." This refers to B-natural, the "hard" B, written as a square shape which eventually became our natural (♮) and sharp (♯) signs.</note>. Therefore, the same thing would differ from itself, which is what they were trying to prove. In the same way, in the scale where the "mi" of <term>soft B</term> <note>original: "b mollis." This refers to B-flat, written as a rounded 'b', which became our modern flat (♭) sign.</note> is placed, a "fa" is joined to that soft B; therefore, one soft B would differ from another soft B. 

It is certainly an absurdity in music to place a difference between voices as being both "hard" <note>square B</note> and "soft" <note>rounded B</note> by nature. If John of San Vicente <note>Johannes de Sancto Vincentio, a 15th-century theorist</note> attempted to affirm this in the context of theology, nothing more need be said of him, as he composed no book. However, if John Hothby <note>Johannes Otbi / Hothby, an English Carmelite monk and influential music theorist who worked in Italy</note>, who posits a "hard," a "soft," and a "natural" semitone, has done this, he has understood the matter far worse. Although he correctly adopted numbers for his <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate mathematical musical ratios</note>—since they are the same ones Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music textbook of the Middle Ages</note> uses—he nevertheless seems to have received that distinction of the semitone not from Boethius, but from some unlearned source. 

Regarding him, I must say what John the Carthusian <note>Johannes de Muris, a famous 14th-century mathematician and astronomer</note> used to say of Marchetto <note>Marchetto da Padova, a theorist known for his innovative but controversial division of the whole tone</note>: "Since the beginning of time, it has never been heard that there is a threefold semitone—<term>Chromatic</term>, <term>Enharmonic</term>, and <term>Diatonic</term>." For as he says, who ever heard from any true teacher that a semitone is divided threefold, except from that beginner Marchetto? I believe John Hothby took some of his foundation from him. But I am not surprised, for he is a follower of Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo, the 11th-century monk credited with inventing the musical staff and the "do-re-mi" system</note>. I intend to "crush the head" <note>A biblical allusion to crushing the head of the serpent</note> of this error, so that this body of mistakes may become a corpse and live no longer.

Furthermore, this man, bound to the dregs of music <note>An insult to Hothby’s traditionalist views</note>, says that these three properties—square B, soft B, and natural—relate to one another just like the three genera of melodies described by Boethius: the Diatonic, the Chromatic, and the Enharmonic. He says that the Diatonic, which is somewhat "hard," holds the likeness of the <term>square B</term>. The Enharmonic, being more closely adapted to the "soft," he compares to the <term>soft B</term>. And the Chromatic, which holds a middle ground between the two, preserves the image of the "natural."

<margin> = </margin>

Indeed, when I was recently lecturing at the University of Salamanca, we argued against this in his presence. In the treatise I composed there in the <term>mother tongue</term> <note>referring to Spanish; Ramos published his "Musica practica" in Latin but wrote earlier works in his native Spanish</note> regarding this discipline, I contradicted him in all things. So much so, that after he had seen and examined my treatise, he said: "I am not as familiar with Boethius as this man." 

For I have proved that "hard" and "soft" exist in the other two genera just as they do in this one. I do not doubt that the <term>synemmenon tetrachord</term> <note>A Greek term for a specific four-note grouping that allowed for the "soft" B-flat</note> is found there. Their system is one thing, and mine is another. Let us divide the <term>tetrachords</term> <note>The four-note scales that were the building blocks of the ancient Greek musical system</note> of the Chromatic genus by a semitone, a semitone, and a <term>trihemitone</term> <note>A "three-half-tone" interval, equivalent to a minor third</note>. That is, the first interval is a semitone, and the second is a semitone, but the third is a trihemitone (or <term>semiditone</term>). In the Enharmonic genus, the semitone is divided into two parts called <term>dieses</term> <note>Microtonal intervals smaller than a semitone</note>, and thus two spaces hold individual *dieses*. But the third interval encompasses a <term>ditone</term> <note>A major third</note>. We will not treat these genera further at present, because at the end of this first part, we shall put them into practice using musical notation. Now, however, as to how not...

## Second Treatise

Let us declare how the semitone is formed between <term>mi and fa</term> <note>In the medieval system, the semitone (half-step) occurred naturally only between the syllables *mi* and *fa*.</note> and in what way the mutations of the **conjuncts** <note>original: "coniunctarum"; these are notes outside the standard gamut, often requiring what we now call sharps or flats.</note> are necessary. In this way, tonal voices are made into semitonal ones, and major thirds <note>original: "ditonales"</note> into minor thirds <note>original: "semiditonales"</note>, and vice-versa. According to what must be correctly understood, let us diligently examine what follows.

### Chapter Seven: Refuting the followers of Guido and subtly demonstrating the truth of the matter <note>Guido of Arezzo was the 11th-century monk who pioneered the solmization system. Here, the author critiques those who follow Guido's rules too rigidly without considering natural melodic tendencies.</note>

Once the differences in music have been examined and perceived, it remains to be proven how tonal steps become semitones and vice versa. Regarding this, it should be known that a melody in its ascent—as **Johannes de Villanova** says—requires the voice to be fortified, and in its descent, to be made soft. Wherefore, he says that if a melody is chanted *a–c–d* and does not return to *c*, although it ought to be called <term>re fa sol</term> as the order demonstrates, it should nevertheless be called <term>ut mi fa</term>. This is because the distance between *a* and *c* is not a semitone, but a "ditone" <note>a major third</note>; or rather, if it is pronounced with those very syllables, namely <term>re fa si</term>, an implied ditone is said to be understood.

Again, if a melody is made thus: *g–f–g*, and again does not touch [another note], the *f* is an implied semitone, even though it may be called <term>sol fa sol</term> or <term>re ut re</term>. He judges the same thing: that a **synemenon** <note>original: "synemenon"; the Greek term for the "connected" tetrachord, used here to refer to the use of a B-flat.</note> should always be made when, after a note placed on *b-fa / b-natural-mi*, another follows on the **mese** <note>the central note, here referring to the pitch A</note>; this applies whether one arrives at it from the low notes or touches it while descending from the high notes, especially if the melody strikes that same place multiple times.

Likewise, if a melody makes this progression: *d–b–c–d–c–d–d*, the interval to the eighth is *b–c–e* <note>original: "risi us octavis"</note> as a tone, and *c–d* as a semitone twice made; and thus either the tonal voices will hold themselves as implied, or a mutation will be made from <term>mi into re</term>, which is a voice of the conjuncts. And this same Johannes says that a minor third is made from a major third in this manner: if the melody says <term>la fa sol sol</term> without returning again to *fa*, it will either be an implied minor third, or a mutation should be made from <term>la into sol</term> so that it is said <term>la sol mi fa fa</term>. In this manner, the diligent reader may judge other instances arranged in this way.

Such notes ought to be marked with this sign, namely **#**, or this one [b], for greater evidence. Therefore, whoever wishes to compose a melody must pay great attention to these things, about which we shall speak more subtly in the second part. For now, let these few things suffice to declare the confusion resulting from Guido’s syllables.

Indeed, **Saint Johannes Carthusinus** <note>John of the Carthusians, a 15th-century music theorist.</note> spoke well concerning these mutations. I do not mean a mutation of voice into voice, but a variation from one winding path to another, merely noting the tones and semitones and singing by the letters of **Gregory** <note>referring to letter notation (A-B-C-D-E-F-G) attributed to Pope Gregory.</note>. This I say truly regarding my own syllables: for whoever wishes to sing through our syllables will be bound to make only a single mutation in one **diapason** <note>an octave</note>. This is because when the song ascends beyond high *c*, we will change <term>tas</term> into <term>psal</term>, and thus the mutation <term>sas sal</term> will occur. But when it wishes to go downward, they will change <term>psal</term> into <term>tas</term> in this manner:

# Part One

One must remain silent on the 7th <note>original: "filenduz," likely *silendum*</note> in the low register. And also, let them not compel students to do this, for sometimes we permit one to be said for another, but only to attend to the rules mentioned above—that is, they should become accustomed to observing the species of <term>tones or semitones</term> <note>The specific patterns of whole steps and half steps that define musical scales.</note> so that they do not perform one for another. This occurs among those who use <term>solmization</term> <note>The system of singing music using syllables like ut, re, mi.</note> as they say, through the "voices" of Guido. Indeed, the "Catholic" <note>Here meaning "universal" or "standard."</note> faith of the new theorists regarding the voices of Guido has grown so much due to the long duration of the custom that Ugolino of Orvieto <note>Ugolino Urbeveterano (c. 1380–1452), a famous music theorist and archpriest at the Ferrara Cathedral.</note>, while secluded in the cathedral church of Ferrara, when he begins to treat the voices, calls them Greek expressions and says: "The Greeks had only five, namely *re, mi, fa, sol, la*, lacking *ut*." He proves this because their art begins from the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The lowest note of the ancient Greek musical system, corresponding to the note A.</note>, which is <term>A-re</term>; from there he concludes his intention. Truly, if this were to be brought to light and into public view, it should be held as a joke and a mockery.

We, therefore, wonderfully delighted in this our new form of the most excellent art, have decided to begin singing with <term>ut</term>, because that voice holds a more noble tone, as will be said later, and also because, as Guido himself says, we find it to be the first by the nature of numbers. We shall therefore make for it one <term>diapason</term> <note>An octave.</note> in the lower part and others in the higher part, so that our music contains three octaves, that it may be useful not only for ecclesiastical song but also for the more elaborate secular song.

It will, therefore, be the music of Gregory, Ambrose, Augustine, Bernard, Isidore, Odo, and the *Enchiridion* <note>A handbook or manual; specifically referring to Odo of Cluny’s or Guido’s manuals.</note> of Guido, who took almost everything from these men and their successors—just as the law of Scripture was not given to everyone, for some sing today without it. But our music will be "Catholic" or universal, like the Law of Grace, which contains the written law and the law of nature within itself. Thus, our system will also contain everything that those most wise ecclesiastical men and ancient musicians said and discovered. 

Therefore, when we place the octave syllable under the letter C, let the Italians not wonder, for it is established that this is not new but very frequently used. Almost all the <term>polychords</term> <note>Instruments with many strings, such as early keyboards or harps.</note> of the new theorists have it. Indeed, it is found through instruments that the <term>monochords</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate mathematical proportions of musical intervals.</note> of the ancients made their beginning on that same low C. Therefore, we have established the beginning of singing on that same letter. On the monochord, however, following the Greek Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the fundamental music theory text of the Middle Ages.</note>, it begins on A. 

Now, however, we place the mnemonic art—that is, the letters to be sung with the "voices" through the joints of the fingers—in the palm in this way. Not without reason, as Guido did at his pleasure, but we shall arrange the voices with the greatest likeness to the reality in this manner: Let the hollow of the instrument be in the hollow of the hand where silence shall be placed, for there no voice ought to be made, and it shall be like the earth. Then the first voice shall be placed in the restricted part <note>Likely referring to the base of the thumb or the first joint.</note>, below which no lower voice should be placed because there will be silence; for the hollow of the hand...

# Second Treatise

<note>The text begins with a physical rationale for the mnemonic system: joints of the fingers represent musical intervals because they allow for distinct, measurable movement.</note>
Anything lacking a joint lacks distinct motion. But even where movement is restricted, it possesses a first position from the application of the thumb's motion to the fleshy part of the hand. Therefore, since motion exists there, we will locate the sound. Then, the second voice <note>In this context, 'voice' refers to a specific musical note or pitch.</note> is placed at the base of the thumb on the outer side, where there is another motion distinct from the first. The third is placed at the other outer joint of the thumb, where the motion is also distinct. The fourth is placed at the next-to-last outer joint for the same reason. The fifth indeed—because it has a greater harmony with the first than the fourth itself does—we shall place at the tip of the thumb, turned around, that is, looking back toward the first. This is the very reason why that voice is called a fourth <note>Latin: *diatessaron*</note>. And so we will place the sixth voice in the following inner joint, looking downward; and so also the seventh. In this way, seven different notes will be placed in different ways.

But the eighth note, which is similar to the first though higher in pitch, shall be located at the base of the index finger. Because that finger is more slender than the thumb, it clearly demonstrates that more slender strings <note>Latin: *chordas*</note> produce more slender <note>meaning higher-pitched</note> voices. Another octave <note>Latin: *diapason*</note> we shall arrange in this manner: so that there is an octave with the eighth of this first set—namely a triad—we place the first note of the following set at the base of the index finger itself. Then in the second joint of this finger, then in the third, and finally at the top. Across the very summit, even to the summit of the middle finger, we shall draw two lines in such a way that those two fingers appear tied together at the top. At the tip of the middle finger, we will place a note "turned around" for the same reason we mentioned before. Thus we will fit the following voices to the following joints, so that the eighth voice is placed at the base of the middle finger itself, and is positioned in unison with the base of the aforementioned index finger. 

The same should be done for a third octave <note>Latin: *diapason*</note> using the little finger <note>Latin: *auricularis*</note>, at the base of which the sequence shall reach its end. We do not say this because one cannot proceed further toward higher notes, nor do we deny that "wings" <note>likely referring to the range of the scale</note> can be extended below the lower notes. Rather, we say this because the teaching is sufficiently expanded within these three octaves. For in low strings, there is a limit to the voice so that the depth does not descend all the way into silence; and in high notes, that same limit of sharpness is observed: lest it happen, as Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works on music were the standard textbooks of the Middle Ages.</note> says, that the thinness of an overly strained voice should break. Instead, let the whole be consistent and suitable to itself. Therefore, the figure placed below—the diagram of the following hand—will make all of this clear.

We imagine this figure, arranged in this way, as if the thumb were cut through the middle, as if toward the hollow of the hand, and the inner part is joined together with the index finger, which is separated from the middle. Likewise, let the middle finger be raised above the binding which ties both fingers by their tips, so that the voices marked with inverted letters may be represented as upright. The ring finger <note>Latin: *medicus*; literally 'the doctor's finger,' used in medicine for pulses and ointments</note>, however, from the side...

## Second Treatise

standing upright in the middle of the ears: and by connecting them through the head by raising it up: so that its root is at the peak: and thus we demonstrate the likeness of the ancients with the first fifteen chords <note>original: "chordis"; in this context, "chords" refers to the individual strings of a lyre or specific musical pitches</note> or voices. Therefore, we place them in a single upright structure: but those eight which we join for perfection, we join there to be marked out as appears in the figure <note>The author is likely referring to a diagram that was present in the original manuscript or a preceding page</note>. But enough of these matters for now; let us now turn our discourse to the earlier species <term>species|In medieval music theory, 'species' refers to the different possible arrangements of whole tones and semitones within a specific interval like a fourth or fifth</term> which were mentioned before, since the entire power of harmony depends upon them.

### Chapter Eight, in which it is subtly explained in how many ways
### each species is formed

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Take note, reader: that each species is formed in as many ways as the intervals it contains. This is because the semitone <term>semitone|The smallest interval in the scale; a 'half-step'</term>, which possesses an unequal interval, rotates through the other intervals. Hence, it is sometimes placed in the first, sometimes in the second, and occasionally in the third interval. However, a species that does not contain a semitone is formed in only one way, even if it contains several intervals; for example, the ditone <term>ditone|A major third, consisting of two whole tones</term> and the tritone <term>tritone|An interval of three whole tones</term> are always formed in only one way. 

The semiditone <term>semiditone|A minor third, consisting of one whole tone and one semitone</term>, because it has two intervals—namely one tone and one semitone—can be formed in two ways: one when the semitone is in the higher interval, such as A to C, or D to F, or G to B-flat, or B to D in the disjunct system, and in their octaves. The other way is when the semitone is in the lower interval, such as B to D, or C to E, or H to B <note>The letter 'H' was often used in Germanic traditions to signify B-natural, while 'B' signified B-flat</note> in the conjunct system, and in their octaves. 

The diatessaron <term>diatessaron|A perfect fourth, containing two tones and one semitone</term>, because it contains three intervals—namely two tones and one semitone—can be formed in three ways. The first mode is when the semitone is in the middle, such as A to D, and D to G, as well as G to B in the conjunct system and H to L in the disjunct, and in their octaves. This is called the first mode because the "Art of Boethius" <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose work 'De institutione musica' was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages</note> begins there. The second mode is when the semitone is in the lower place, such as B to E and E to B, as well as H to L in the conjunct system and in their octaves. The third mode is when the semitone is in the higher place, such as C to F, F to B, and G to K, and in their octaves. 

The diapente <term>diapente|A perfect fifth, containing three tones and one semitone</term> is formed in four ways because it contains four intervals—namely three tones and one semitone. The first mode, therefore, will have the semitone in the second interval, such as A to E, H to D, and likewise G to I in the conjunct and B to M in the disjunct, and in their octaves. The second mode will have the semitone in the first interval, such as E to <note>natural sign indicated in original</note> B and B to M in the conjunct, and in their octaves. The third mode will have the semitone in the fourth interval, such as F to K in the disjunct and B to N, and in their octaves. The fourth mode will have the semitone in the third interval, such as C to G, or F to K in the conjunct, or G to K in the disjunct, and in their octaves. This is understood in the natural order: because in the accidental order, by moving from any one chord to another, we can make four different versions of the diapente in this way.

<summary>This page explains the "species" of musical intervals, detailing how the position of the semitone determines the different modes of the minor third, perfect fourth, and perfect fifth. It references the authority of Boethius and distinguishes between natural and accidental arrangements of these tones.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, species of intervals, semitone, ditone, tritone, semiditone, diatessaron, diapente, Boethius, medieval scale construction</keywords>

<meta>The transcription continues from a discussion of musical classification, moving into the second treatise which defines the various intervals or 'species' of music.</meta>

# Second Treatise

...and seven other species. But we set these down so that the teaching does not become confused and ecclesiastical song <note>original: "cantus ecclesiasticus"; referring to Gregorian chant</note> may be understood. Chant is regularly ordered; however, other songs composed more out of lewdness than beauty never or rarely follow the rule of which we will speak shortly. First, however, let us speak of all the species, which many think to be thirteen.

A species, according to <term>Boethius</term> <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the Roman philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages.</note>, is a certain position having its own proper form according to each genus of every proportion that creates a consonance. These are established by terms; that is, descending from equality <note>unison, or a 1:1 ratio</note> up to the <term>diapason</term> <note>the octave</note>, they are created through the addition of a semitone.

Therefore, the first [species], being equal, is called the **unison**. After this, the smaller quantity is the **semitone** <note>a half-step</note>; then the **tone**, which is worth two semitones. After this is the **semiditone**, which contains three semitones—that is, a tone with a semitone—and so on: the **ditone**, the **diatessaron**, the **tritone**, and the **diapente**. 

Next is the diapente with a semitone, which is called by the Greek name **minor hexas** <note>a minor sixth</note>, since it is capable of six voices and has only a semitone beyond the diapente. The diapente with a tone is called the **major hexas** or **hexachord** <note>a major sixth</note>; it also has a tone above the diapente. Likewise the **minor and major heptas** or **heptachord** <note>minor and major sevenths</note>, because it is capable of seven strings or voices. These are formed by the diapente with a semiditone and the diapente with a ditone. Finally, the diapente with the diatessaron, which as we said, joined together make the **diapason** <note>the octave</note>.

To speak in summary, these are thought to be the thirteen species by which all song is governed: namely, the **unison**, **semitone**, **tone**, **semiditone**, **ditone**, **diatessaron**, **tritone**, and the **diapente**—with which the previous intervals are repeated up to the diatessaron, and in this way the **diapason** itself is completed.

Someone might ask, however, why we say that the diapente <note>perfect fifth</note> and diatessaron <note>perfect fourth</note> joined together fulfill the diapason <note>octave</note>. For would not a ditone with a minor hexas do the same, or a semiditone with a major one? Likewise, a tone with a minor heptas and a semitone with a major one? It must be said that this is true. 

But because those are the principal parts—namely the diapente and the diatessaron—into which the diapason is first divided, the diapason is said to be integrated from them. For those who examine well the quantity of the string in the first figure (B A): in the middle, V is equally distant from both; and let the diatessaron sound with A, and the diapente sound with B. Therefore, the quantity of string for the diatessaron (V to A) is the same as for the diapente (V to B). Therefore, it is no wonder if the diapason is always said to be perfected by those quantities into which it is first divided. Thus, in its progression, song proceeds through those voices which form these species rather than others, as will be said shortly. However, some should not judge from this that there is such a...
