# Second Treatise

...the fourth <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> in sound is as great as the fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note> when it is exceeded by a whole tone, as has been said. However, there is another quantity that differs almost not at all in sound, into which the octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> can be divided: namely, the tritone <term>tritonerange of three whole tones</term> and the imperfect fifth, which is called the semi-fifth <note>original: "semidiapente"</note>. This occurs between B and F, or F and B-natural <note>The text uses the "square b" (♮) for B-natural and "round b" (♭) for B-flat.</note>, because the distance between F and B-flat is the same as between F and B-natural. This difference does not matter much to practical musicians, but it does to theorists <note>original: "theoricorum"</note> who study the specific difference of a semitone. In sound, however, it is no less discordant than the tritone when considered directly. Therefore, we have not made special mention of the semi-fifth, because in actual sound it is taken in place of the tritone. We specifically said "directly" because if one proceeds through the intermediate notes—whether ascending or descending—the sound is sweet and playful, such as F-E-D-C-B or conversely B-C-D-E-F. However, the melody should not rest on F when ascending, but should turn back to E; likewise, in descent, it should turn toward C. To create a tritone, as Brother Johannes Carthusinus <note>Johannes Carthusinus (John of Mantua) was a 15th-century music theorist and Carthusian monk.</note> says, is not a **mortal sin** as many believe; rather, it is necessary. For otherwise, one could not provide the third type of the fifth, which would be clearly false and against the truth. Indeed, it is seen as necessary in its proper place; for if a melody ascends in this manner, F-G-A-B♭-C, the ascent is sweeter than F-G-A-B-C. In descent, however, it is the opposite. But enough on these matters. Now we must speak of the species of the octave itself: how many there are, what they are, and how they are arranged. Within this lies the whole power of harmony, and they are called modes, tropes, or even tones.

## Third Treatise, in which a full knowledge of tones, modes, or tropes is set forth.

->Let us proceed, then, to that species of music which is said to<-
->contain the whole of harmony. Since the octave is made of<-
->eight notes containing seven intervals—five whole tones and<-
->two semitones—it results in seven modes. For if the octave<-
is correctly constructed from a fifth and a fourth, and the fifth is made of four types <note>The "four types" refer to the different positions of the semitone within the five-note span.</note> and the fourth of three, it follows that the octave itself must be varied in seven modes. To begin from the *proslambanomenos* <term>proslambanomenosthe lowest note in the Greek Greater Perfect System, equivalent to a low A</term> up to the *mese* <term>mesethe middle note, equivalent to A an octave higher</term> is the first species. This is divided into a fourth and a fifth by the *lichanos hypaton* <term>lichanos hypatonthe third string/note of the lowest tetrachord</term>, which divides the string in the middle. Therefore, this species of octave will have the first fourth, A to D <note>The text uses "a o" and "d b", likely referring to string positions or specific diagram labels.</note>, with the first fifth, because the semitone of both is located in the second interval. The second species of octave is from the *hypate hypaton* <term>hypate hypatonthe second lowest note, equivalent to B</term> to the *paramese* <term>paramesethe note B above the middle</term>. This is divided by a string equally halved at the *hypate meson* <term>hypate mesonthe "principal of the middle" notes, equivalent to E</term>. It will therefore have the second fourth, namely B to E, and the second fifth, namely E to B-natural, since the semitone...

<header>Part One</header>

# Part One

...of each is placed in the first interval, that is, in the lower position. Therefore, the third species of the entire <term>harmony<gloss>original: concentus; refers to the concordant sounding of notes</gloss></term> is formed from the <term>hypate hypaton</term> <note>The lowest note of the Greek "Greater Perfect System," corresponding to a low B</note> to the <term>trite diezeugmenon</term> <note>The "third string of the disjoined tetrachord," corresponding to C</note> which is divided, like the others, at the <term>parhypate meson</term> <note>The "next-to-lowest of the middle," corresponding to F</note>. It will therefore have the third species of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: diatessaron</note> and the third of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: diapente</note>, since the semitone of the fourth is placed in the third interval, and the fifth in the <unclear>fourth</unclear> <note>original: a/tato; likely a scribal error or shorthand for "quarto" (fourth)</note>.

Indeed, you make the fourth species of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: diapason</note> from the <term>lichanos hypaton</term> <note>The "index-finger" note of the lowest tetrachord, corresponding to D</note> to the <term>paranete diezeugmenon</term> <note>The "next-to-highest" of the disjoined tetrachord, corresponding to D an octave higher</note> by dividing it through the <term>lichanos meson</term> <note>The "index-finger" note of the middle tetrachord, corresponding to G</note>. This fourth species of the octave will therefore have the first species of the fourth, but the fourth species of the fifth. In this way, it is easily known to differ from the first species, because the semitone is placed in the third interval. 

Truly, if a fifth were made from the <term>lichanos meson</term> to the <term>nete synemmenon</term> <note>The "highest string of the conjoined tetrachord," which introduces the B-flat</note>, it would be the same as the first species, and there would be no difference; for the semitone would be in the second interval just as in the first. Since the fourth is entirely similar, it follows that there is no difference between these species so arranged, unless one were to say they differ because the fifth of the first species is made from line to line (such as from B to F), and the fifth of the fourth species is from space to space (such as from G to D). 

However, that distinction is of no value, because a line or a space does not prove a difference in music. <term>Brother Johannes Carthusiensis</term> <note>Johannes Gallicus or John of Mantua (c. 1415–1473), a Carthusian monk and music theorist</note> attempted to prove that only fifteen strings were placed by the ancients for this reason: that <term>A-re</term> and <term>A-la-mi-re</term> <note>Pitches in the medieval Gamut; A-re is the low A, A-la-mi-re is the A in the middle of the range</note> are both located in the second space. So too the remaining strings that come after A-la-mi-re correspond in a similar way. For if this were the case, the third species of the fifth would be similar to the first, and the fourth similar to the second, since they now occupy lines or spaces; therefore, the fourth species of the fifth is not dissimilar to the first, but is the same, if one assumes the <term>conjoined tetrachord</term> <note>original: thetrachordum synemenon; a four-note grouping that includes the B-flat</note>. 

In this way, by dividing the string in the middle, we make four different species of the octave, and there cannot be more. But if we divide them not by the middle, but into three parts, we shall make another four different species—not because they are entirely different, but because the fourth will be above the fifth and not below it. For example, the fifth species from the <term>lichanos hypaton</term> to the <term>paranete diezeugmenon</term>: since the fifth will be the same as that held by the first species, let the fourth be extended above it; in this, it will be seen to differ sufficiently from the first. 

The sixth species of the octave, however, is from the <term>meson</term> <note>The middle notes</note> to the <term>nete diezeugmenon</term> <note>The highest note of the disjoined tetrachord, corresponding to E</note>, which will have the same fifth as the second species, and the fourth similarly, but extended above the fifth; and in this, it differs from that same second species. The seventh species of the octave will be made from the <term>parhypate meson</term> to the <term>trite hyperbolaion</term> <note>The "third string of the extreme tetrachord," corresponding to F</note>, taking the fifth which the third species held and extending the fourth above it. We make the eighth species in this way: from the <term>lichanos meson</term> we extend the same fifth which the fourth species held...

# Third Treatise

...the *paranete diezeugmenon* <note>original: "ranete diazeugmeno"; a specific note in the ancient Greek scale, literally the "next-to-last note of the disjunct tetrachord"</note> above which we intend the first <term>diathessaron: an interval of a fourth</term> in the *paranete hyperboleon* <note>original: "pazanete hypboleon"; the second-highest note of the entire Greek system</note>. But if the *synemmenon* <note>original: "synemenō"; the "conjunct" tetrachord used for modulation</note> is taken, it will be like the fifth: because of this difference, then—namely the conjunct and the disjunct—an eighth species of the <term>diapason: an interval of an octave</term> is added. We shall now discuss how the modes or tones arise from these species.

### Chapter Two: On the Origin of Tones

From the species of the octave, as Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages</note> says, there exist what are called modes, which some also name "tropes" or "tones." For tropes are frameworks <note>original: "constitutiones"</note> in the whole ordering of voices, differing in either depth or height. A "framework" is like the body of a melody, consisting of a joining together of consonances. Boethius calls these modes by distinct proper names; for he himself says that whatever name a certain people delighted in, that name was bestowed upon the mode.

For instance, the **Dorian** <note>original: "dorius"</note> is so called because the Dorians <note>An ancient Greek tribe</note> delighted in it. It consists of the first species of the <term>diapente: an interval of a fifth</term> and the first species of the fourth pitched upward above the fifth: **f . c b ᚦ b l** <note>These letters represent specific notes in the medieval gamut</note> which we said was the octave located in the fourth place. When, however, this fifth has a fourth below it pitched downward—that is, **d . a**—it is called the **Hypodorian** <note>The "sub-Dorian"</note> because it is situated below the Dorian.

The second species of the fifth with the second fourth pitched upward, such as **e □ □ m**, he calls the **Phrygian**, because it was in use among the Phrygians. If, however, the second species of the fifth has the second species of the fourth pitched downward below it, it is called **Hypophrygian**, as in **e □ e b** with the fourth downward.

The third species of the fifth with the third species of the fourth pitched upward he calls the **Lydian**, because it was frequently used with great joy in Lydia (from where the Tuscans trace their origin), as in **f k** fifth and **k n** fourth upward. But if the fourth is held not above but below, it will be named **Hypolydian** for the reason mentioned before, as in **f k** fifth and **f c** fourth.

The fourth species of the fifth with the first species of the fourth pitched upward lacks its own name, but because it is placed next to the Lydian, it is called the **Mixolydian**, as in **g l** fifth upward and **l o** fourth upward. If the fourth is not pitched above but downward below it, it is called **Hypomixolydian**, as in **g l** fifth and **g c** fourth downward. 

Because of this conformity in both the thing itself and the name, the Greeks and even our own ancients say there are only four modes <note>referring to the four pairs of "authentic" and "plagal" modes</note>, because the species of the fifth is fourfold. Thus, the first species is called *Protus* in Greek, which is interpreted as *First* in Latin; the second is *Deuterus* in Greek, *Second* in Latin; the third is *Tritus* in Greek, *Third* in Latin; and the fourth is *Tetrartus* in Greek, which is *Fourth* in Latin.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical and cultural origins of musical modes, showing how the Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, and Mixolydian modes (and their 'Hypo' counterparts) are built from specific combinations of fourths and fifths.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, musical modes, Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, Mixolydian, interval theory, species of the octave, Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrartus</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page begins the "First Part" of the treatise, establishing the definitions of the musical modes that govern medieval and Renaissance chant.</meta>

First Part

# First Part

When, therefore, the <term>protus</term> <note>original: "protus"; the first category of modes, typically based on the note D</note> has a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> above it, it is called the **authentic protus**, that is, the "first authoritative" mode or the "master." But if it receives the fourth below it, lacking that authentic height, it is named the **plagal protus**, that is, "collateral" or the "disciple," as the modern writers say. If it should have both, it will be called "mixed"; understand the same for the other three modes. However, we speak of this mixture only superficially for now, since we are dealing only with the species of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> from which those eight tones arise. When these tones each fill their full octave, they are each "perfect." If they fall short, they are "imperfect"; if they exceed it, they are "superfluous." They are not, however, "more than perfect," as <term>Ugolino</term> <note>Ugolino of Forlì (c. 1380–1452), a famous music theorist</note> asserts and <term>Johannes de Muris</term> <note>A 14th-century mathematician and music theorist</note> (whom Ugolino praises excessively), or as <term>Marchetto</term> <note>Marchetto of Padua, whose 14th-century works were foundational but often debated</note> says, who was refuted by Brother Johannes. For I say that a tone which fills its octave, neither more nor less, is perfect; but one which exceeds or falls short is imperfect through either superfluity or diminution.

The moderns call these eight modes thus: the **authentic protus** is called Mode 1, and its **plagal** is Mode 2; the **authentic deuterus** <note>The second category of modes, typically based on the note E</note> is Mode 3, and its **plagal** is Mode 4; the **authentic tritus** <note>The third category of modes, typically based on the note F</note> is Mode 5, and its **plagal** is Mode 6; the **authentic tetrartus** <note>The fourth category of modes, typically based on the note G</note> is Mode 7, and its **plagal** is Mode 8. 

But we must see how a trope or mode is understood: whether simply by extending a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, by extending or relaxing a fourth, or from the first note—that is, from the lowest to the highest through all the intermediate notes—or if one must proceed in some other way in song. For just as we have set down four different classes, it is necessary that they maintain their difference in song. For the quality of one mode is carried along in a straight and tranquil course like a narrative; another is composed of twists and leaps; another is chattering; yet another is severe, lifting the voice on high to elevate the souls of the listeners; while another is placid, indicating a joyful character. From this it should be noted that musical movement draws its quality and distinction from the mode itself—whether first, second, or another. Therefore, we must speak of these individually.

<summary>This page defines the eight musical modes, categorizing them as authentic (master) or plagal (disciple) based on their range and internal intervals. The author also describes the distinct emotional and narrative qualities associated with different modes, while critiquing the terminology used by earlier theorists like Johannes de Muris and Ugolino of Forlì.</summary>
<keywords>musical modes, authentic, plagal, protus, deuterus, tritus, tetrartus, octave, fourth, fifth, Ugolino of Forlì, Johannes de Muris, Marchetto of Padua, music theory, emotional character of music</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Third Treatise," focusing on the philosophical and physiological connections between musical modes and the human body, specifically the four humors. It references the Boethian concept of the three types of music (mundane, human, and instrumental).</meta>

# Third Treatise

## Chapter three, in which the correspondence between mundane, human, and instrumental music is demonstrated through the modes:

Truly, this instrumental music has the greatest correspondence and similarity with human and mundane music <note>The author refers to the Boethian division of music: *Musica Mundana* (the harmony of the universe and planets), *Musica Humana* (the internal harmony of the human body and soul), and *Musica Instrumentalis* (the audible music we hear and play).</note>. Regarding the human realm, it works in this way: those four modes move the four temperaments <note>The "complexions" or humors—phlegm, choler, blood, and melancholy—which medieval medicine believed governed human health and personality.</note> of man. Specifically, the **Protus** <term>Protus: The first category of medieval musical modes, encompassing the 1st (Dorian) and 2nd (Hypodorian) tones.</term> rules over phlegm; the **Deuterus** rules over choler; the **Tritus** over blood; and the **Tetrartus**, being more sluggish and slow, over melancholy. 

The **Protus** mode itself moves phlegm by waking one from sleep; thus, its figure is depicted in a crystalline color, since the crystalline heaven is said to be made of water—the element that creates phlegm. But we assign it the color of crystal and not of other waters because not all phlegmatic men are able to perceive a sweet sound. Rather, it is for ingenious men—those as clear as crystal—who, when they are somewhat burdened by food (without gluttony) or some other external "accident" of phlegm that induces sleepiness, sluggishness, or sadness, are relieved by the sounding of the **Protus** mode. 

Yet its plagal <note>A "plagal" mode is a variation of a principal mode that uses a lower melodic range.</note> mode behaves in the opposite way. For the first tone, as <insert>Ludovicus Sanctus</insert> <note>original: "Iodociuicus sanciꝫ." This likely refers to Ludovicus Sanctus of Beringen, a 14th-century music theorist and friend of the poet Petrarch.</note> says, is mobile and suited to all emotions—namely, those that are desirable, as found in songs. But the second tone is grave and mournful, and most suitable for the wretched and the sluggish, as in the *Threni* <note>Threni: Songs of mourning or dirges.</note> and the Lamentations of Jeremiah. 

For we do not doubt that a sound arises from sadness due to the movement of phlegm, hence the saying: *their eyes were heavy with sadness* <note>original: "erant oculi eoruʒ grauati pre tristicia." A reference to the biblical description of the disciples in the Garden of Gethsemane.</note>. It was also the custom among the Pythagoreans <note>original: "perhagoricis"</note> that when they wished to dissolve the day's cares in sleep, they would use the **Hypodorian** mode so that a gentle and quiet sleep might creep over them; yet upon waking, they used the **Dorian** mode to purge the stupor and confusion of sleep. They knew surely, as Boethius says, that the entire composition of our soul and body is joined together by musical adaptation, and just as the state of the body is affected, so too [is the soul]...

<margin>Institutional stamp: NATIONAL CENTRAL LIBRARY FLORENCE</margin>

<summary>This page explains the "Musica Humana" theory, linking the four primary musical modes to the four human humors and describing how specific tones, like the Dorian and Hypodorian, can manipulate physical states like sleep and sadness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory, moving from the general definitions of music into a specific analysis of the "modes" (the scales used in medieval and Renaissance music). It connects the emotional character of these modes to the four humors of the human body and the physical arrangement of the cosmos.</meta>

# First Part

## Section One

The pulses of the heart are incited by movements. The **Deuterus** <note>The 'second' category of modes, comprising the 3rd (Phrygian) and 4th (Hypophrygian) modes.</note> mode truly stirs the **choler** <note>Yellow bile, the humor associated with fire, anger, and energy.</note> by inciting it and provoking one to anger; therefore, it is depicted with a **fiery color**. This is because it is severe and, being incited in its course, it possesses stronger leaps <note>Intervals between notes.</note>, as we shall discuss in the proper place. This tone is most suited to men who are proud, wrathful, and haughty because of its harshness and great severity; and they rejoice in it. As **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music textbook for a millennium.</note> says: "Those who are harsher are delighted by the harsher modes, while those who are gentle are delighted by the moderate ones."

Boethius said that by this tone a **Tauromenian youth** <note>A famous anecdote where a young man, driven to a frenzy by music, was stopped from burning down a house only when the music was changed.</note> was so incited that he prepared to break down a harlot's doors, but was calmed by the **Hypodorian** mode, which is its **plagal** <note>A 'plagal' mode is a companion scale that sits a fourth lower than its 'authentic' counterpart.</note> version. 

The **fourth tone** is said to be smooth and talkative, especially suitable for flatterers; because in one's presence they soothe men with charming words, but in one's absence they sting. Thus this tone is seen to be rather playful, yet without grace, and sometimes inciting depending on how it is mixed. 

The **Tritus** <note>The 'third' category, comprising the 5th (Lydian) and 6th (Hypolydian) modes.</note> authentic trope, however, has dominion over the **blood** <note>The sanguine humor, associated with cheerfulness and vitality.</note>. Therefore, this tone is called delightful, modest, and cheerful by the blessed **Augustine**, making the sad and anxious joyful, and recalling those who are fallen or desperate. For this reason, it is depicted with a **blood-red color**. Regarding this, Boethius says that the **Lydians**, who are especially pleasant and joyful, are delighted by this—especially their women, from whom the **Russians** <note>original: "russi." The author may be making a folk-etymological connection between the Lydians and Eastern peoples or referring to the color red.</note> are said to have arisen, who take great pleasure in dances and leaps. Its plagal version [the sixth tone] is mournful, suiting those who are easily moved to tears, because it has very unified voices, as will be discussed in the same section.

The **Tetrartus** <note>The 'fourth' category, comprising the 7th (Mixolydian) and 8th (Hypomixolydian) modes.</note> authentic mode has a part of playfulness and pleasantness, besides various leaps of excitement, and it represents the manners of youth. Therefore, it holds dominion over **melancholy** <note>The humor associated with earth and sadness; the text suggests this mode can either fight or increase it.</note>, sometimes resisting it and sometimes increasing it, depending on the mixture it makes with others, as will be said shortly. For this reason, it is depicted with a **yellow, semi-crystalline color**. 

Its plagal [the eighth tone] is sweet, well-mannered, and deliberate according to the style of discerning men, as **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose, credited with introducing antiphonal chanting to the West.</note> reports. Therefore, the seventh and eighth modes move melancholy by their melody, bringing sad and sluggish men to a middle ground: namely, the authentic mode by inciting them, and the plagal by gladdening them.

From these things, therefore, the agreement between **instrumental music** and **human music** <note>Boethius divided music into three types: Mundana (of the universe), Humana (of the human body/soul), and Instrumentalis (audible music).</note> is clear. But that **world music** <term>Musica Mundana; the "Music of the Spheres" created by the movement of planets.</term> has the greatest conformity with instrumental music is clear by making a comparison. For from the *proslambanomenos* to the *mese* <note>The names of specific notes in the Ancient Greek "Greater Perfect System."</note>, the order of the planets is arranged with the firmament: 

*   The **Moon** is the *proslambanomenos* <gloss>the lowest note</gloss>
*   **Mercury** is the *hypate hypaton*
*   **Venus** is the *parhypate*
*   The **Sun** is the *lichanos hypaton*
*   **Mars** is the *hypate meson*
*   **Jupiter** is the *parhypate meson*

<summary>This page explains the psychological and physiological effects of musical modes, linking them to the four humors (choler, blood, melancholy) and specific colors. It concludes by beginning a comparison between musical scales and the celestial arrangement of the planets.</summary>

<keywords>musical modes, four humors, Boethius, Augustine, music of the spheres, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrartus, Lydian music, planetary harmony</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "musica mundana" (the music of the spheres), specifically linking the positions of the planets to the intervals and modes of medieval music theory.</meta>

# Third Treatise

Saturn is linked to the <term>lichanos meson</term><note>original: "licanos meso"; the "index-finger" note of the middle tetrachord in the Greek musical system</note>; the starry sky corresponds to the <term>mese</term><note>the central pitch of the Greek system</note>. If, therefore, the Moon is placed at the <term>proslambanomenos</term><note>the lowest note of the scale</note> and the Sun at the <term>lichanos hypaton</term><note>the third note from the bottom</note>, it is clear that these two planets are arranged in the musical interval of a <term>diatessaron</term><note>an interval of a fourth</note>. Consequently, the Moon holds the **Hypodorian** mode, while the Sun holds the **Dorian** mode. From this, it is clearly established that the Moon increases the phlegmatic and humid qualities in a person, whereas the Sun dries out those same humid and phlegmatic qualities. Therefore, because these two planets are the primary rulers and luminaries, they govern the "first mode" along with its companion—that is, the <term>authentic</term> and <term>plagal</term><note>The two versions of a musical mode: "authentic" is higher-reaching, "plagal" is lower</note> forms of the *protus*. For the Dorian, the first of the authentic modes, is rightly compared to the Sun because it holds primacy among all the modes just as the Sun does among all the planets. Indeed, all earthly exhalations and great vapors are raised up by the Sun's rays, from which meteorological phenomena are created. The agreement between the Sun and Moon is thus clear: she shines at night, while he puts the night to flight. The **Hypodorian** brings on sleep, while the **Dorian** expels it. They agree, therefore, both in their placement and in their harmony within the consonance of the fourth.

Mercury, however, rules the **Hypodorian** <margin>Mercury</margin>. For this mode is flattering; it praises the wicked and the wise and upright in equal measure, and is easily turned toward either side—that is, toward lamentation or toward joy, toward incitement or toward calming. Such is the nature of Mercury, who is good with the good and worst with the evil.

Mars holds the **Phrygian** mode, which is entirely choleric and angry; for he strives to destroy all the good things of the world with his wrath. Thus, when Mercury is joined with him, or in a certain aspect, he is as evil in his anger as Mars himself; for Mars wounds with the sword, but Mercury wounds with the tongue.

The **Hypolydian** mode is attributed to Venus herself, who is a "fortune" <note>a "benefic" or lucky planet in astrology</note>, though a feminine one, because she sometimes provokes "pious tears." The **Lydian** mode is attributed to the "Greater Fortune" <note>Jupiter</note>, who creates sanguine, benevolent, mild, and joyful men. They harmonize in the fourth and agree in their "fortunate" goodness; they do not differ except in the pitch of their voices. For a lower voice is not as sweet or pleasant as a high, sharp one.

The **Mixolydian** is attributed to Saturn because it deals with melancholy. The **Hypomixolydian**, however, is placed entirely as "Castalian" <note>pure or heavenly</note> because it is attributed to the starry sky or the firmament. For this mode possesses a certain wonderful sweetness and beauty above all others; it is immune to all earthly qualities and is suitable for every business. **Guido** and **Odo** <note>Guido of Arezzo and Odo of Cluny, famous medieval music theorists</note> say that this mode represents glory. They do not descend much [in pitch], as they say that we labor while running through the "seven ages" of the world, but in the "eighth age" <note>eternity or the afterlife</note> we expect rest from all our labors. From these thirteen move—

<summary>This page maps the planets and the starry firmament to specific musical modes and intervals, explaining how celestial movements influence human temperament and musical character.</summary>
<keywords>music of the spheres, Dorian mode, Phrygian mode, Lydian mode, planetary influence, Sun, Moon, Mercury, Mars, Venus, Jupiter, Saturn, Guido of Arezzo, Odo of Cluny, diatessaron</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the "First Part" of the treatise, transitioning from a general introduction to a specific discussion on the harmony of the spheres and the Greek musical system.</meta>

-># Part One<-
<note>original: "Prima pars"</note>

Thus, it has been sufficiently stated concerning the harmony of human and mundane music <note>The author refers to Boethius's famous three-fold division: *musica mundana* (the music of the universe/planets), *musica humana* (the internal harmony of the human soul and body), and *musica instrumentalis* (audible music).</note> with instrumental music. For in the second and third books, we shall speak more fully on these matters. But I have placed these things here so that I might accustom the reader's mind and remove any ignorance of the musical signs. It is clear, therefore, from the said comparisons and authorities, that each **mode** <note>original: "tonum." In this context, "tone" refers to a musical mode or scale type.</note> has a different quality from the others.

If one wishes to prove this even more certainly, one may do so by authority and by comparison with that from which it draws its origin. As it pleased **Hesiod**, the nine Muses—the daughters of Jupiter and Memory—are arranged in such a way that she who narrates wars we hand over to Mars, and likewise to the **Phrygian mode**. So too, she who commemorates tragedies or slaughters <note>original: "trogoedias siue cedes"</note> is associated with <unclear>safno</unclear> and for this reason the **Mixolydian mode**. She who indicates joy, however, is given to Venus. And thus each of the Muses is placed in her due position according to the authorities of **Marcianus Capella** <note>A 5th-century writer whose work on the Seven Liberal Arts was a standard textbook.</note> and **Macrobius**.

So too, we shall assign a power to each planet through which they harmonize. Since we have noted the Muses, let us arrange them thus: **Thalia** holds silence, just like the Earth <note>In the geocentric model, the Earth is the stationary, silent center of the universe.</note>. Then **Clio** <gloss>the Moon</gloss> is attributed to the Moon. But **Calliope** shall be dedicated to Mercury; **Terpsichore** shall be joined to Venus; **Melpomene** shall be adorned by the Sun; **Erato** shall stand with Mars; **Euterpe** shall know Jupiter and the lyre; **Polyhymnia** is made somber by Saturn; and finally, **Urania** shall give beauty and rule to the starry sky.

When, therefore, we move from the silence of the earth to the ultimate sphere, and returning to the center we run through the whole range, we produce the **Hypodorian mode**. Just as we have done with these, we think it should be done with the rest, so that we do not cease to make spirals until we reach the last Muse. If anything were added beyond her, it would be superfluous, because it is merely a repetition of what came before. As the authority <unclear>Rogenerus Capero</unclear> asserted that there was a "crisis" <note>likely meaning a critical point or division</note> regarding that note added above the *nete hyperbolaion* <note>The highest note of the Greek "Greater Perfect System."</note> and the note <insert>coruph</insert> which is below the *proslambanomenos* <note>The lowest note of the Greek scale.</note>. He himself, I believe, set forth the whole art in the Cretan style, and when he reached that boundary, he and the wheel fell. For it is proven that one Muse holds silence while another holds a higher voice. Let us, however, be careful about transferring things from the ancient authors.

Therefore, the first voice shall be the **proslambanomenos** and the last shall be in the **nete hyperbolaion**, for all of which we have provided the figure written below:

<note>The manuscript likely contained a diagram or table here illustrating the scale.</note>

From this arrangement of the figure, it is clear what the **modes** <note>original: "tropi"</note> are called, and how each one is created. For their order proceeds such that if one stretches the first species of the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> from the *proslambanomenos* to the *mese* <note>The central note of the Greek scale.</note>, and including those middle voices that close the extremes, one increases the tension toward the high pitch, making the *hypate hypaton* <note>A low note in the Greek system.</note> sharper by a whole tone and all the others sharper by a tone, the entire order will become higher than it was before it received the tension of the tone. Thus the whole constitution is made sharper, becoming the **Hypophrygian mode**, and it is similar for the others in the process of sharpening and tension.

Therefore, the modes are not so called because they begin in low notes, cross into high notes, and return at the end to finish in low notes, as it pleased some. Rather, there are those that begin not in the low, but in the high notes, as will be said shortly when we treat each one individually. First, let us discuss certain general signs for all, through which an unknown song may be recognized—whether it is corrupted or correctly set forth—and so that we may know how to compose a new one correctly.

# Third Treatise

->The Second Part (that is, counterpoint)<-

**Chapter One, in which knowledge of certain voices and dissonances is set forth.**

We have followed another path up to this point, speaking only of voices produced in succession <note>original: "successive prolatis"; this refers to melody, where notes are played one after another</note>. Now, however, let it be briefly explained which of those voices, when two or more are struck or sung together, sound in harmony or in discord. As for how <term>consonance</term> <note>a "sweet" or stable combination of sounds</note> or <term>dissonance</term> <note>a "harsh" or unstable combination of sounds</note> enters the ear, and whether the opinion of Plato or Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa was an ancient mathematician who wrote on the harmony of the spheres</note> is true, we shall attempt to declare and define it in the second book according to the strength of our intellect, since it is a matter of theory. 

For now, because we seek to draw students of practice toward this teaching little by little, let us explain briefly—and almost by way of a summary—those things which pertain to practice. It has been said that the whole body of music is a single <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the Greek term for an octave</note>, which consists of eight voices. Therefore, if we describe these eight voices by relating them to one another, it will be sufficient. 

By way of instruction, you should know that equal voices <term>accord</term> <note>the unison</note>. The second is dissonant with the first. The third is consonant with the first. The fourth alone is discordant with the first <note>In this period of music theory, the perfect fourth was often treated as a dissonance in two-part counterpoint</note>. However, the fifth and sixth are consonant. The seventh is discrepant <note>dissonant</note>. The eighth is most perfectly <term>equisonant</term> <note>sounding the same, as an octave does</note>. 

Just as we have done by relating all the others to the first, so it is with the second, the third, and the rest: so that the third is dissonant with the second, but the fourth is consonant <note>a 4th above the 2nd is a 5th from the original root</note>, and so on for the others in this manner; the <unclear>established</unclear> order will demonstrate this. 

Furthermore, so that we may know how to avoid the unharmonious and choose the harmonious, we say that there are three discrepant voices: namely, the second, the fourth, and the seventh. The second may be major or minor, being either a whole tone or a semitone. The fourth similarly, because it is a <term>diatessaron</term> <note>a perfect fourth</note> or a <term>tritone</term> <note>an augmented fourth, famously known as the "devil in music" for its harshness</note>. The seventh is the same way, being a major or minor <term>heptas</term> <note>seventh</note>. 

But the concordant ones are the <term>unison</term>, the third, the fifth, the sixth, and the octave. Regarding the unison, however, there is no doubt, because the same thing does not differ from itself; for that reason, it is not strictly counted among the consonances. For a consonance is not the agreement of things that are the same, but the agreement of dissimilar things brought into one, or the blended sound of dissimilar things falling sweetly upon the ears. 

A <term>dissonance</term>, however, is a harsh and unpleasant collision, as Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works were the foundation of medieval music theory</note> says, because each sound strives to reach the ear intact and neither gives way to the other, as we shall demonstrate more fully in our theoretical works. Nevertheless, the unison in music is like "unity" <note>the number one</note> in arithmetic: the beginning of numbers, the fountain and origin of consonances. 

Therefore, setting the unison aside, we say that there are four species of concord: namely the third, the fifth, the sixth, and the octave. Of these, two are **perfect** (the fifth and the octave) and two are **imperfect** (the third and the sixth). They are called "imperfect" because...

<summary>This page introduces the principles of counterpoint, moving from melody to harmony. It classifies musical intervals into consonances (unison, 3rd, 5th, 6th, 8th) and dissonances (2nd, 4th, 7th) based on classical theory.</summary>
<keywords>counterpoint, consonance, dissonance, Boethius, music theory, octave, unison, interval classification</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the second part of the treatise. The author transitions from basic definitions to the specific behavior of musical intervals (consonances), particularly how they change or remain stable when shifted by an octave. There is a clear emphasis on making the text accessible to "practical" musicians rather than just "theorists."</meta>

## Part Two

They are variable because, by the addition or subtraction of a semitone <note>A half-step, the smallest interval in this musical system.</note>, they do not change their nature as a consonance <note>A "consonance" is a combination of notes that sounds pleasant or stable together.</note>, but always sound good. This is the case with the third, whether it is a ditone <note>original: "ditonalis"; a major third consisting of two whole tones.</note> or a semiditone <note>original: "semiditonalis"; a minor third.</note>; they differ only in that the former is called "major" and the latter "minor." The same must be said of the sixth. A fifth <note>original: "diapente".</note> combined with a whole tone or a semitone is either major or minor.

The octave <note>original: "Octaua".</note>, however, accepts neither increase nor decrease without sounding harsh and discordant, because it must always contain five whole tones and two semitones—neither more nor less. Therefore, it is called the "most perfect" and "equal-sounding" <note>original: "equisona".</note> interval, because it seems to sound exactly like the first note, just as a unison <note>Two voices singing the exact same pitch.</note> does. Hence, if a man and a boy sing together, they may seem to be in unison, yet they are actually an octave apart.

The fifth, however, if it is increased or decreased by a semitone, either takes on the characteristics of a sixth or is turned into the harshness and discord of a tritone <note>An interval of three whole tones, famously difficult to sing and often avoided in medieval music.</note>. For this reason, the fifth is indeed "perfect," but not in the same way the octave is. 

We will discuss other mathematical reasons in our theoretical works, which practical musicians would not care much for if we included them here; nor would they even be able to grasp them, since they would first need to understand proportions and mathematical ratios. Therefore, let them be satisfied with the reasons given here, since they are entirely concerned with practical music. In this way, like those starting with milky foods <note>A common historical metaphor for teaching beginners simple concepts before moving to "tougher meat" or advanced subjects.</note>, they will eventually be led toward harder nourishment. 

Once these things are understood: if we extend or lower any type of consonance by an octave, we will undoubtedly produce the same type of sound. As has often been said, the entire harmony is contained within the octave <note>original: "diapason".</note>. Whatever is true of the first note and its octave will be similar for the notes that follow; the only difference is that they sound higher or lower. Therefore, the octave is like the source. The ninth is like the second; the tenth is like the third; the eleventh like the fourth; the twelfth like the fifth; and the thirteenth like the sixth. However, the fourteenth <note>The OCR text says "decimaquinta" (15th) here, but the musical logic and the following sentence suggest the author meant the 14th.</note> is discordant like the seventh. The fifteenth is equal-sounding just like the octave. The same pattern holds from the fifteenth up to the twenty-second. 

Thus, there are only four different types of consonant intervals, which are frequently repeated through the addition of octaves. The first group is called "simple," the others "composite," and the third "doubly composite," as is shown in the diagram. 

So, if the original interval that creates the sound is dissonant, perfect, or imperfect, the resulting interval will be the same. This creation of secondary consonances happens whenever the melody is higher than the plainchant <note>original: "cantu plano"; the traditional, monophonic church music used as a foundation for harmony.</note> by an octave, or below it when the interval is lowered. 

But what if the opposite happens? That is, if an octave is added to a lower interval or removed from a higher one? We must say that from a third, a sixth is produced; from a sixth, a third; and from a fifth, a fourth <note>The author is describing "interval inversion," where flipping the notes of a third creates a sixth, and so on.</note>. Therefore, the third and the sixth are of the same condition because they are "imperfect" consonances. But the fifth and the fourth primarily come from... <note>The text ends abruptly here.</note>

<summary>This page explains the classification of musical intervals, distinguishing between variable intervals (thirds and sixths) and perfect ones (octaves and fifths). It also introduces the concept of octave equivalence and interval inversion for practical musicians.</summary>
<keywords>octave, consonance, dissonance, fifth, third, sixth, musical theory, interval inversion, practical music, species</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals, moving from the mathematical properties of fourths and fifths into practical rules for composing organum (early polyphony).</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

# First Treatise

I promise that I shall speak in the volume where we shall treat of several voices. For the present, however, let it suffice to know that as much perfection as the fifth possesses, so much does the fourth approach dissonance while remaining <term>among the consonances</term> <note>original: "intra consonantias"; the fourth was often considered a "borderline" consonance in medieval theory</note>. This is just as when a sixth is produced from a third and vice-versa. If the "creator" interval is major, the "created" interval comes out minor, and conversely. The same also applies to the dissonances that are formed from the seventh and vice-versa. But if the creator is major, the created will be minor and vice-versa. Now, however, since we care to create <term>organum</term> <note>Organum is an early form of polyphony where a second voice is added to a plainchant melody</note> over a given melody, we will first insert certain brief rules of the ancients.

The first of these is: one must begin and end in a perfect species <note>A "perfect species" refers to intervals like the unison, fifth, or octave</note> or in <term>unison</term> [original: *unisono*].

The second: it is not permitted to make one perfect interval after another of the same kind, nor a <term>unison</term> <note>This is an early rule against "parallel fifths" or "parallel octaves," which was thought to make the two voices sound too much like a single melody</note>.

3rd: two or more imperfect intervals can be given one after another.

4th: if the chant continues on the same pitch through two or more notes, the <term>organum</term> <note>The added voice</note> shall not rest on the same pitch but should move through different places.

The fifth rule: a major sixth connects to an octave; a minor sixth, however, separates to a fifth. Likewise, a major third moves outward to a fifth. A minor third, however, leads to a unison.

Sixth: if the <term>tenor</term> <note>The principal melody, usually a plainchant, held by the "tenor" (from "tenere," to hold)</note> ascends, the <insert>counter-voice</insert> <note>The manuscript says "Corruptus," which is likely a scribal error for "Contrapunctus" (counterpoint)</note> should strive to descend.

For the first rule is explained thus: when we begin to create organum, let us place the voice in a fifth or in an octave, or in some other interval composed from these according to the convenience of the voice, and also in unison; and when we finish, the same must be done. This is because other consonances are not of such great perfection as these are. Therefore, we ought to make the better intervals at the beginning and the end; in the middle, however, it is permissible to intersperse <term>imperfect ones</term> [original: *imperfectiores*].

The second rule is understood thus: we should not sound twice in perfect consonance with a <term>tenor</term> that is ascending or descending by the same perfect species, because then it would seem to be the same progression. For if the tenor moves from D to C and the organum moves from L to K, it would seem to be the same voice in the octave; so also regarding the unison, even if it moves from H to G, it is prohibited for the same reason—not because it is an identical likeness, but because the likeness is too great.

<term>Tristan de Silva</term> <note>An otherwise obscure musical theorist cited here as an authority</note>, however, does not totally prohibit this in the fifth, as he says, since one fifth can be made after another, provided that one be a <term>diminished fifth</term> <note>original: "semidiapente"</note> and the other a <term>perfect fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, just as we find in the song of Sois Enpzantis and in other older works; but this is not to be allowed in "straight" plainchant, though it is fine in "broken" notes—that is, in the <term>division of notes</term> [original: *diminutione notularum*], of which we shall speak shortly.

However, dissimilar perfect intervals may be permitted; that is, an octave after a fifth, or a fifth after an octave in this manner: as if the tenor moves D, E, C, D, and the organum moves L, M, K, H, and so in others.

The 3rd rule is to be understood thus: if the tenor ascends E, D, E, the organum can ascend in the same way E, F, G. The same in descent, and in the same way with the sixth and similarly in other places. However, the one creating the organum should not judge from this...

<summary>This page outlines six fundamental rules for early polyphony (organum), emphasizing the use of perfect consonances at the start and end of a piece and prohibiting parallel perfect intervals. It also cites the theorist Tristan de Silva regarding exceptions to these rules in "broken" or diminished musical passages.</summary>

<keywords>organum, counterpoint, consonances, dissonances, perfect intervals, Tristan de Silva, tenor, musical theory, parallel fifths, unison</keywords>

<meta>The transcription continues from the first part of the treatise, moving from general definitions of intervals to specific rules for how the "organum" (the accompanying voice) should move against the "tenor" (the base melody).</meta>

<header>Second Part</header>
<meta>image quality: excellent; catchwords: none; typeface: Gothic/Blackletter</meta>

## Second Part

If the <term>tenor|the foundational melody in a polyphonic work</term> repeats the same pitch through two or more notes, it is possible to create two or more imperfect intervals <note>original: "imperfectas"; likely referring to thirds or sixths</note> in the same position with the <term>organum|the voice added to the tenor to create harmony</term>. And thus the "right fourth" is explained, although this is not strictly forbidden by many, especially in the composition of three or four voices, because there it is permitted.

The **fifth** is explained thus: if the tenor descends from *d* to *c*, we ascend from *b* to *k* <note>The letters k and l in this manuscript likely represent specific pitches in the gamut, possibly corresponding to 'a' or 'c' depending on the local notation system</note>. For if it descends from *e* to *d*, or any similar place, it is not appropriate to make the organum *k* and *l*, because it results in a minor sixth. If we wish to do so, we must raise the *k* if we ascend from a lower part, or sustain it if we descend from a higher voice. If it were to be drawn, it should be shaped thus: <note>musical symbol</note>.

From this follows a notable instruction, which is: if the song descends *f, c, d*, it is not permitted to make the organum *k, k, l*, because one of two improprieties follows: namely, either being led from a minor sixth into an octave, or pronouncing unequal voices in the same place. Instead of this, one should make *l, k, l* or *b, k, l*. In the first method, it descends only by an implied <term>semitone|the smallest interval in this system, a half-step</term>; in the second, it ascends by a <term>ditone|an interval of two whole tones, a major third</term>. If this is drawn, it should be marked as above.

Regarding this, one must pay attention to what we said in the first part about the "implied semitone" <note>original: "semitonio subintellecto"; also known as 'musica ficta,' where singers sharpen or flatten notes even if not written</note>. Consider when a note is raised from its proper place or lowered, and beware of <term>perfect species|perfect intervals like the unison, fifth, or octave</term> if they are used incorrectly. For example, if the tenor sings *d, f, g* and has such conditions through which the *f* is raised from its proper place, the organum shall not create a unison or a perfect interval over it. In the same way, a tenor singing *d, c, d* and immediately returning above the *c* will not sound perfectly <note>i.e., will not form a perfect interval</note>; it is different if it pauses on the *c* or makes a distinction <note>a phrase ending or cadence</note> there.

However, a minor sixth resolving to a fifth is proven thus: if the tenor stays on the same pitch for two notes or more, such as *d, d, d*, the organum may make *b, b, b*. Similarly, if the melody descends by a semitone—whether real or implied—an organum staying on a fifth may remain there. For example, if the tenor is *f, e, f*, the organum will be *k, k, k*. Some, however, prohibit wandering through three notes on the same pitch, even if the species are different. They say this because the <term>counterpoint|the relationship between the voices</term> would seem to be a <term>cantus firmus|a fixed, unmoving melody</term>. But this is a minor obstacle because both voices can be correctly called organum and tenor when we place the notes fully in both. But because we do not wish to depart from common usage, we forbid making concords with them; namely, that the organum should not rest on the same pitch for more than two notes. Therefore, in the previous example, let it say *b, k, k*.

Furthermore, the movement of a **major third to a fifth** is exemplified thus: let the tenor be *b, g*; the organum [shall be] *k, ♮, k* <note>The symbol ▢ represents 'B-natural' (B durum)</note>. For this reason, one must be careful, as with the sixth: if the tenor sings *g, e, d*, the organum will not make *g, g, h*, but rather *e, g, b* or *♮, g, b*. And *e* to *g* is a ditone, and *♮* to *g* is a <term>semiditone|a minor third</term>, and thus it creates a major third with...

It emphasizes the use of implied semitones (musica ficta) to avoid dissonances or "improper" interval progressions.</summary>

# The First Treatise

Regarding the tenor, since whatever is raised in its own [mode], if it is depicted, it should be marked as shown above. Therefore, also if the tenor says **f d c**, the organum will not perform **f f g**; however, it could perform **b f g** or **d f g**. And yet, **b** to **f** is a <term>minor third</term> <note>original: "semiditonus"</note>, while **d** to **f** is a <term>major third</term> <note>original: "ditonus"</note>. If this is written down, it must be marked with a <term>□</term> <note>The "square B" symbol, indicating B-natural</note>, or even a minor interval might join the organum with the tenor. For example, if the tenor says **f e f**, **b g f** sounds best. Likewise, if the tenor is **f g b**, the organum should be **k b b**, but not **k □ b**. For this reason, one must take care that if the tenor is **f f g**, the organum does not perform **k b a**; or if the tenor is **g g h**, the organum does not perform **l □ b**. However, **l b b** works well. If, however, the tenor sings in this manner: **g f g**, the organum can perform **g b g** or **□ b g**: because that third, even if it appears major, is made minor by implication.

Most people, however, do not observe this rule regarding the major or minor third. Because of this, their compositions—even if they delight at first hearing because the song is new—displease more and more every day as they are brought into frequent use. Singers are often ignorant of these causes we have mentioned, as well as others concerning <term>diminution</term> <note>The breaking of long notes into shorter, decorative notes</note> which we will speak of shortly.

Thus, counterpoint ought to proceed to the nearer <term>species</term> <note>The types of musical intervals</note>: such as from a sixth into an octave or into a fifth; or from a third into a <term>unison</term> <note>original: "unisonum"</note> or into a fifth. This applies to both simple and compound intervals. But sometimes the organum sings by the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, or even the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note>; in such cases, it will come from a third directly into an octave. For example, if the tenor is **f e**, the organum is **b m**. Or if the tenor is **g f**, the organum is **g b**. Or if the tenor is **d d c d d e d**, this is a good arrangement for the organist: **f n m b l k l r**, and similarly in other <term>modes</term> <note>original: "tropis"</note>.

However, there is a best way of composing organum: when the organum imitates the tenor in its ascent or descent, not at the same time, but beginning after one or more notes to make the same or a similar song in the same voice, or at the <term>fourth</term>, the <term>fifth</term>, or even the <term>octave</term> (including their compounds above or below). Practitioners call this method <term>fuga</term> <note>A "flight" or fugue; the earliest form of melodic imitation</note>, because one voice follows another with a similar <term>rising</term> <note>original: "arsis"</note> or <term>falling</term> <note>original: "thesis"</note>. 

For instance, if the tenor should sing: **l n m l n m o**, the organum can follow it at the lower <term>fourth</term> after the first note and say: **b k □ b k □ l**. Likewise, at the <term>fifth</term> above, keeping the same rest, the organum will say: **p q p q s**. The same applies to their compound intervals, or to the <term>unison</term> and <term>octave</term>. If the tenor is **d e f g d c f e d**, the organum can sound the same at the octave after two notes: **l m n o l k n m l**. The same is true for the <term>unison</term> and its octaves above and below. 

But in these examples, we have placed those last notes in the organum without the tenor having corresponding notes for them, so that the similarity might be shown. We assume that the voices following in the tenor do not create discord with them; because when a <term>fuga</term> begins to create discord in its similarity, a dissimilarity should be made immediately, so as not to violate the rules mentioned above. Now, the sixth rule is declared thus: if the song in the ten...

# Second part

...given the voice: let the descant <term>discantus: the highest voice part in early polyphony</term> fall back to an appropriate species <note>musical interval</note> according to the assigned rules. And if the tenor <term>tenor: the fundamental voice part that "holds" the melody</term> descends, the counterpoint <term>counterpoint: the relationship between voices that are interdependent harmonically yet independent in rhythm and contour</term> should ascend, and this is what is most frequently observed in counterpoint; for it is from this that it is said to have taken its name: **Chapter Two, in which the error of some is rebuked and the truth is demonstrated.**

Now all the aforesaid things are understood [to apply] mostly when the tenor moves up or down by step. But what if it moves by leaps and turns? It must be said that when it moves in such a way, then it is more necessary for the voices of the organ <term>organum: an early form of polyphony</term> to join together; for instance, if the tenor sings [the notes] o b b, the organ will make l k l, or l m l, or l m n; likewise in other modes of the fifth <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term>. If it is a fourth <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> in this manner, d f e, the organ [plays] l k k, or if the tenor is c f d, the organ [plays] k k l. Ugolino <note>Likely referring to Ugolino of Forlì (c. 1380–1452), a famous music theorist</note> set down common rules for certain "barbaric" meters regarding all species, both simple and compound, of which some hold the truth, while others do not at all. But so that the truth may shine forth, and falsehood may blush and be confounded, I will briefly explain them here in this manner, speaking first of the ascending unison <term>unison: two voices sounding the same pitch</term>. Let there be a third <term>third: an interval spanning three staff positions</term> below if the unison moves up by one [step]. If it moves a third or a fourth, it shall hold a fifth below. If it ascends a fifth, it shall only end at the octave <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term>. **Second rule concerning the descending unison:** let the third be above if the unison falls by one. It moves toward the fifth if it falls by a third or fourth; it seeks the octave if [it falls] by a fifth or more. He explains: if it traverses more, the order of reason will teach [the way]. <margin>3rd rule: regarding the ascending 3rd <insert>ascending</insert></margin> Let a unison be made by one [step] if the third moves up. If it moves up more, let a unison finally be made. The third falls if it ascends by a third or fourth.

->**Fourth rule concerning the descending third**<-

A fifth will be made three times if it falls by a third alone; if there are more, a fifth will terminate them. If it oversees a third or fourth [above] an octave, a fifth will be made above. If it is joined with an octave: <margin>5th rule: regarding the ascending 5th</margin> The fifth seeks a third if there is an ascent of one [step]. You may call it a unison if you intend a third or a fifth. <margin>6th rule: regarding the descending 5th</margin> The fifth seeks an octave if it descends by only one. It will be an octave [and] sixth if it is associated with another. After a fifth, an octave will be made if it falls a third below, or if it allows a fourth or fifteenth <term>fifteenth: an interval of two octaves</term> to be reached. <margin>7th rule: regarding the ascending 6th</margin> The sixth desires a third if it moves up to the note above. <margin>8th rule: regarding the descending 6th</margin> The sixth wants an octave below if it moves toward one [note]; and let more be made if they precede the octave: a sixteenth <term>sixteenth: an interval of two octaves plus a second</term> [is wanted] if the third falls below.

->**Ninth rule concerning the ascending octave**<-

After the octave, a fifth [is made] if the song <note>original: "cantus"</note> moves up by one: if it sings a fourth or fifth...

# First Treatise

The tenth <note>original: "decima," abbreviated here as "lir"</note> by right demands a third. The tenth rule from the eighth descending.
The eighth becomes a tenth if it only drops by one step. If a third were there, it then becomes a twelfth: the eleventh rule from the tenth ascending.
The tenth wants an octave if it is raised <note>intensio: moving to a higher pitch</note> by only one step. If it transcends more, then the fifth will hold the place: the twelfth rule from the tenth descending.
The descending tenth desires to have a twelfth: the 13th rule from the 12th ascending.
Ascending by one, the twelfth seeks the tenth. The eighth, third, fourth, and fifth seek the following fifth: the 14th rule from the 12th descending.
A fifth with a tenth becomes a twelfth afterward: if a third is joined with a tenth, let it be given: a third with a tenth demands a fifth or an eleventh.

---

<margin>Note!</margin>
**T**he first rule is refuted in this way: if the <term>tenor (the foundational melody)</term> sings F G, the <term>organum (the accompanying voice)</term> can say F E just as well as F E because it ascends a third. Since he himself says the better organum stays in the third rather than going to the fifth. Because if it is a fourth, [for example] G K <note>In this notation system, letters like K, L, and M represent pitches in the higher octaves beyond the standard A-G</note>: tenor . organum and it will act correctly G C because if there is a <term>fifth (diapente)</term> such as F K, the organum will act correctly F F. 

The second rule: if the tenor descends F D, the organum will do just as well with F F as with F H. The 3rd [rule] is good enough. The 4th rule: if the tenor sings F E D C, the organum will act correctly H G □ K <note>The square symbol '□' denotes a B-natural (b-quadratum), whereas a rounded 'b' would denote B-flat</note>, just as B G F G. Similarly, if the tenor made F D, the organum will do as well with B B as with B L. 

The 5th rule is rejected similarly because if the tenor sings F B, the organum does as well—and even better—K F or K K than K B: and if the tenor [sings] D B: the organum likewise says B F just as B B: and sometimes B K, but rarely except for the sake of variation. 

The sixth rule passes well enough, but if the tenor sings F C: the organum likewise [does] well K L, just as L M; similarly if G E is the tenor, the organum likewise [does] well L L just as L M. The 7th rule is good: if the tenor is E F, the organum likewise [does] well K L just as L H, because from a minor sixth we move well into a fifth. 

The 8th rule is good; but when the tenor says F D, the organum can say L L just as L N. The ninth [rule] is refuted because if the tenor says E F, the organum can say K L just as L H. The 10th is rebuked because if the tenor sings E C, the organum will do better M M than M O. 

The eleventh [rule], however, departs greatly from the truth. For when the tenor makes leaps and turns <note>original: "saltus et anfractus," referring to wide intervals and winding melodic lines</note>, the organum ought to bring its voices together <note>coadunare: to move the voices closer together to maintain stability</note>. For if both voices run through leaps and turns, it would be called a contest rather than <term>harmony</term>. For example, if the tenor says C D, the organum will do better M B than M L: and then only as much as the tenor begins to make leaps and turns: the organum should begin to bring the voices together. When the tenor [sings] E E: the organum M □: tenor C F, organum M L and tenor C G, organum M L. In this example, if you pay attention, there is the greatest agreement, since the tenor begins to sing gradually from the second up to the fifth, the organum has also brought the voices together from the fifth down to the second...

<summary>This page lists technical rules for melodic intervals in early polyphonic music and then systematically critiques them. The author argues for harmonic "agreement" and stability, suggesting that the accompanying voice should move closer to the melody when the melody becomes complex or jumpy.</summary>

<header>Second Part</header>

# Second Part

In this mode, harmony generates in the souls of listeners a certain innate sweetness that cannot be explained in speech. <note>original: "dulcedinez"</note> However, our singers consider this very little; instead, they believe that whatever pleases their own imagination or fantasy happens to everyone by accidental disposition. It is for this reason that the common people do not turn so readily to music—or to speak more truly, to "new harmony"—as they used to do in ancient times. We shall speak of these reasons most clearly in the second book. We have said this much now so that we might draw certain singers away from erroneous opinions and lead them back to a true knowledge of music.

Other rules are consistent enough with the truth, but they are to be rejected in this regard: they are superfluous. For beyond the <term>diapason</term> <note>The Greek term for an octave, literally meaning "through all [the notes]."</note> there is always a repetition of the first note. And if you argue that we should distinguish them because of the fifth or the fourth, we reply that we have already given the method for composing the fifth from the fourth and vice versa. We leave the rest to the diligent investigator; for with these few rules, the whole art of <term>counterpoint</term> <note>The practice of writing or playing a melody to be played simultaneously with another.</note> or <term>organum</term> <note>An early form of polyphony (multi-voiced music) based on an existing plainsong.</note> can be defined.

As for other things that might occur regarding the <term>organization</term> <note>The act of composing or performing organum (polyphony).</note> of the voices, we leave those to the judgment of the singers, provided they take care not to do anything against the rules; for even if such things are not fully proven, they do not depart from the truth. Let the singer also observe the method in <term>arsis</term> and <term>thesis</term> <note>Technical terms for the "lift" (rise) and "fall" of a musical phrase.</note>. This means that from a lower voice to a higher one, there should be an ordered movement like a trope; and almost [imitated] pauses when giving perfect <term>species</term> <note>Specific types of musical intervals, such as fifths or octaves.</note>. 

For example, if the tone or mode is the first, let the perfect consonances frequently occur on D, B, or L. Sometimes, however, they may occur in F or in O, and rarely in G or in K. However, they should never occur in E or in B <note>Original: "in e uel in b". This likely refers to B-natural, which was often avoided in medieval cadences to prevent the "tritone" or "devil in music."</note>, though this is to be understood with discernment, since otherwise they can be made anywhere. 

The <term>organizer</term> <note>The singer performing the secondary, polyphonic part.</note> should also observe the <term>pneumata</term> <note>Original Greek: "pneumata," meaning breaths or melodic phrases.</note> of the tropes so that phrase corresponds to phrase. For if the phrases of the <term>tenor</term> <note>The "holding" voice that sings the principal melody, usually in longer notes.</note> are of the first mode, those of the organizer shall be as well. For instance, if the tenor sings:
f d e d g d c g e f d

The Organizer should **not** sing:
h □ m l □ □ k □ k : h □

But **rather**:
h l m l l n m l m k l

This is because the first polyphony belongs to the <term>Phrygian</term> mode, but the second to the <term>Dorian</term>. <note>Phrygian and Dorian are two of the "church modes" or scales used in medieval music, each having a distinct emotional character.</note> Lest anyone think these small examples are not sufficient to grasp the whole doctrine, we give them a more subtle way to arrange these through lines and spaces, moving below and above through the whole <term>hand</term> <note>Refers to the "Guidonian Hand," a visual mnemonic used by medieval singers to learn the notes of the scale.</note>. 

Then, for example, let the same thing that was Dorian be placed in Phrygian, Lydian, and Mixolydian, and let the same be done for their <term>plagal</term> <note>A "plagal" mode is a version of a musical scale that sits at a lower pitch range than its "authentic" counterpart.</note> modes. And when some <term>species</term> does not make good consonances, let the singer raise or lower it by a sign <note>An early reference to sharps or flats (accidentals).</note> so that the consonance may receive its full quantity, or give up what it has in excess. In order that all these things may be more easily remembered, let the following example be placed before your eyes:

**Tenor:** d d e d f e f d c d e d. f g f g h g b b d f e f g e d.
**Organum:** □ l b k l l m h l m l k l. b □ l l k b □ b k l b g b b □ l l l

<page-num>28</page-num>

# First Treatise

It is clear from these few preceding examples that the entire art of <term>counterpoint</term> <note>original: "contrapuncti" — the practice of composing or singing a melody "point against point" with another melody.</note> is concluded through the variation of examples across different positions, through both "ficta" and "recta" music <note>"Recta" (right/correct) refers to the standard medieval scale, while "ficta" (feigned/false) refers to notes outside that scale, similar to our modern accidentals.</note>, and through these variations themselves. Thus, through the same example in different <term>modes</term> <note>original: "tropis" — here referring to the melodic types or musical modes.</note>, a variety of patterns grows through a small, sacred change. For if one <term>pneuma</term> <note>A melodic phrase or breath-length of music.</note> can be varied in so many ways as was said regarding the modes, how much more will it be varied with different consonances? Therefore, the things already said in this section should be sufficient.

But it has been shown that the arts of <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the immensely influential monk who invented the four-line staff and the system of "ut-re-mi" syllables.</note> have bestowed so many superfluous, inconvenient, useless, long-winded, and excessive things in the first part of music. His followers—limping under a blind leadership and believing they have investigated subtle things—have remained stuck in worse, more shameful, and more useless things. There are some who, as they say, take care to practice counterpoint so that the <term>hexachord</term> <note>A six-note scale (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la).</note> does not exceed the plainchant when singing above or below, which they call the "Gamma-B." They say that their entire "hand" <note>The "Guidonian Hand," a mnemonic tool where each joint of the hand represents a specific note in the musical system.</note> is contained in Gamma and <term>Ela</term> <note>The highest note in the medieval system.</note>. They agree that one hexachord is assumed in the theme, and thus they make seven "gammas."

According to the seven hexachords, they call the first <term>B-natural</term> <note>original: "♮" or "square B".</note> low; the second <term>B-flat</term> <note>original: "b molle" or "soft B".</note> low; the third <term>Natural</term> low; the fourth B-natural middle; the fifth Natural high; the sixth B-flat high; and the seventh B-natural high. We shall demonstrate with the firmest reasons that this is both superfluous and insufficient. They set down "gammas" that can be investigated through others, yet they leave out other things that are necessary, namely <term>conjunctions</term> <note>original: "coniunctatum" — notes that fall between the standard steps of the hexachord system.</note>.

However, we who care to flee from ramblings, to bring the truth into the light, to confound falsehoods, and to avoid long-windedness, will provide a most easy method for finding all gammas, completing their diminutions, and avoiding falsehoods.

Let us therefore first take the hexachord—that is, the <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A four-note scale segment.</note>—which ends in <term>D-sol-re</term>. We are speaking, you see, through their own terms. Let us look through the whole "hand" to see which voices concord with it, and thus we shall compose it:

*   **Gamma-ut** <note>The lowest note of the scale.</note> from that hexachord will have *re* because it is a unison; *fa* because it is a third; and *la* because it is a fifth.
*   **A-re** will have *ut* as a third below; *mi* as a unison; and *sol* as a third above.
*   **B-mi** will have *re* as a third below; it will lack a unison; and *la* as a third above.
*   **C-fa-ut** will have *ut*, *mi*, and *sol*.
*   **D-sol-re** will have *ut*, *re*, *fa*, and *la*.
*   **E-la-mi** will have *re*, *mi*, and *sol*.
*   **F-fa-ut** will have *ut*, *mi*, *fa*, and *la*.
*   **G-sol-re-ut** will have *re*, *fa*, and *sol*.
*   **A-la-mi-re** will have *ut*, *mi*, *fa*, and *la*.
*   **B-fa-♮-mi** will have *fa* and *la* (for B-flat) and *re* and *la* (for B-natural).

Up to this point there was a difference in every place. Afterward, however, **C-sol-fa-ut** will behave just like **C-fa-ut** having *ut*, *mi*, and *sol*. Likewise, **D-la-sol-re** will behave just like **D-sol-re** with *ut*, *re*, *fa*, and *la*. They do not differ except that the former are simple notes and the latter are composite <note>This refers to the same pitch classes appearing in higher octaves.</note>. If the former were placed opposite, these would be exposed—about which opposition we have already spoken above. The same thing is said of other places in the series. Thus, if we wish to compose other gammas easily and without great labor, we shall do so with a practical arrangement. **C-fa-ut** in the fifth place from that conjunction... <note>The text here becomes highly technical and somewhat abbreviated as it describes the intervals between the notes.</note>... just as there is the same distance between *s* and *f* as there is between **Gamma-ut** and **D-sol-re**...

<summary>This page critiques the traditional music theory of Guido of Arezzo as being overly complex and provides a more practical, systematic breakdown of how musical notes and hexachords overlap on the "hand."</summary>

<keywords>counterpoint, music theory, Guido of Arezzo, Guidonian hand, hexachord, solmization, Gamma-ut, musical notation, medieval music, intervals</keywords>
