## Second Part

Thus **E-la-mi** with **A-re**, because in both the "ut-mi-sol" <note>These are solmization syllables used to identify notes within a six-note scale.</note> relationship is demonstrative, since **E-la-mi** is as distant from its **Gamma** <note>The starting note of the hexachord.</note> as **A-re** is from the one it is compared to. But when we come to **F-fa-ut** and **B-mi**, the distance is not the same because one is shown to be a semitone apart while the other is a whole tone. Nor are the signs themselves distant from each other by a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"; a musical interval encompassing five letter-names.</note> in the same way the <term>hexachords</term> <note>original: "exacorda"; six-note scales.</note> or the **Gamut** <note>original: "gama"; the full range of the musical system.</note> are. Therefore, the process does not proceed in the same way in the lower as in the higher notes. For this reason, caution must be taken in these and others when this happens, so that a perfect dissonance is not placed in either of those signs.

Thus, **B-mi** will only have "re-la" a third above and below; but in **B-fa** <insert>natural B</insert> **mi** <note>The text refers to the distinction between B-flat (soft B) and B-natural (square or "hard" B).</note>, where the location or sign is double, the same must always be done because the first **Gamut** is perfect with "fa" but with "mi" it is perfect in its octaves. We place the third **Gamut** in this way: the lower note is distant from this "fa" and comes to **Gamma-ut**. Just as one **Gamut** relates to another **Gamut**, so the signs placed in them will have their differences. Hence, as it is in **D-sol-re**, so it is thirdly in **A-re** and in others as well. Wherefore **F-fa-ut** and **B-fa-re-sol** hold so much, but <insert>natural B</insert> **mi** furthermore obtains the fifth. Thus, when it reaches high **E-la-mi**, both "mi" and "re" of **E-la-mi** hold "ut-mi-sol-la," but **B-fa-la** will lack the perfect fifth of "mi."

From this comes the common saying among practitioners: <term>mi against fa</term> <note>original: "mi contra fa"; a rule warning against the tritone, a harsh musical interval between B-natural and F.</note>. This should never stand in a perfect species <note>Intervals like octaves or fifths.</note>, but in imperfect ones it is fine, because they accept "major" and "minor" qualities. They were, however, at a loss in this defect, because that same thing can be said for other voices, as we shall show shortly. 

When you wish to arrange the fourth **Gamut** from these, the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> falls intensely on **D-sol-re**. Hence, the signs at the distance of a fifth will have the same properties; for as **A-re** is in the third, so **E-la-mi** is in the fourth, because of "re-fa-la." But since **B-mi** and **F-fa-ut** do not differ by a fifth, **F-fa-ut** will lack the perfect interval; but the fourth in <insert>natural B</insert> **mi** is like the third in **E-la-mi**, because of "ut-re-fa-la." **B-fa**, however, will lack the perfect intervals. 

If you attempt to distinguish this, let it be distant from the lower one which marks **A-re**. Therefore, signs that are distant will have the same properties. Thus, as in the 4th hexachord at **E-la-mi**, so it is in the 5th at **B-mi**, and in others similarly distant. But in <insert>natural B</insert> **mi**, the fifth will have what the fourth held in high **E-la-mi**. Yet **B-fa**, because it is not proportioned in these two <term>hexachords</term> or **Gamuts**, is therefore most distant from all, since it will only have the "mi-fa" sixth, both major and minor, and those composed of them in the high register.

And when you attempt to create the sixth <term>hexachord</term>, let it be distant from the first; a "conjunction" <note>original: "coniuncta"; a term for a transposed hexachord that requires an accidental note.</note> is located between **A-re** and **B-mi**, so that just as **Gamma-ut** is in the first, so **C-fa-ut** is in the sixth, and so with other places at various distances. And just as **F-fa-ut** and **B-fa** are like "mi-fa-la," but <insert>natural B</insert> **mi** will lack the perfect intervals. If you now consider completing the seventh **Gamut**, let it be a fourth from the sixth, which falls between **E-la-mi** and **D-sol-re**. Therefore, the signs that are higher than this will be related in such a way that just as...

It highlights the rule of "mi against fa" to avoid the dissonant tritone in perfect intervals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the musical "gamut" or scale system (the Guidonian hand), specifically focusing on how different hexachords (six-note scales) overlap and how to correctly identify intervals and solmization syllables (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la) when transitioning between them.</meta>

# First Treatise

Just as ***C-fa-ut*** is in the sixth, so is ***F-fa-ut*** in the seventh: just as the sixth is in ***F-fa-ut***, so the seventh is in ***B-fa***, because in both places ***ut-mi-sol*** is used instead of ***B-mi***. A case of perfect intervals will hold ***mi-sol*** alone in this way. Unless you look at these instructions, you will not be able to complete all the gamuts <note>The "gamut" (gamma-ut) refers to the entire range of musical notes used in medieval theory.</note> without labor; nor should you believe this is set down redundantly if you consider the teachings of Guido <note>Guido d'Arezzo, the 11th-century monk who developed the system of solmization and the musical staff.</note> to be necessary. For you who follow him, semitones <note>The smallest interval in this system, like the step between E and F.</note> often occur by implication rather than being explicitly written, and one must guard against perfect intervals in these cases unless they are supplied by these conjoined notes <term>conjuncta: a note that falls outside the standard "natural," "hard," or "soft" hexachords, often used to avoid dissonant intervals like the tritone.</term>. Therefore, you will see those who propose correctly marking them immediately. 

But indeed, if the gamut can be completed from the upper part in the manner mentioned, you will say: how is it done from the lower part? Let this be your general rule: that you look at each individual place to fulfill its octave, and having maintained the aforesaid formation, you will have it again. If they are simple, namely the unison, the fifth, sixth, and octave, they will correspond in this way: for the sixth, a third; for the octave, a unison; you will always have these in reality. But for the fourth and fifth, and beyond, you should pay attention; for if in a composite figure you have ***fa*** as a fifth, in proposing the following voice you should take ***sol***, because that ***fa*** is the fourth voice; and the same must be done with the other voices. 

For example, in gamut F, where ***C-fa-ut*** lacks the aspect of ***E-sol-fa-ut***, we will have ***ut-mi-fa-la***. For decoration we will have ***C-fa-ut ut-mi-sol-la*** to fill it out; but you should know that when ***fa*** is to be taken instead of ***mi***, that place will lack a fifth, as in the gamut where ***B-mi re-mi-sol*** is composed; but ***B-mi*** will obtain only ***re-sol***, because the ***fa*** which was taken in the place of ***mi*** is not a fifth <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth.</term>. But ***A-la-mi-re*** is like ***re-fa-la***; ***A-re*** is like ***mi-fa-la***; and so is the gamut ***ut-re-mi-sol***. Because they are accustomed to resolving as ***ut-mi-sol***, you thus have a completed gamut. Just as this gamut is completed, so I leave the others to be completed by you. 

Likewise, it should be noted that just as the place to be proposed lacks a fifth when ***fa*** is taken for ***mi***, so when the proposed case is a perfect interval, ***mi*** to be proposed will hold sway, as in the fifth gamut. Truly, the place ***C-sol-fa-ut*** lacked a ***mi*** as a fifth because a third ***fa*** could not be made; therefore it only obtained ***re-sol***. Thus ***C-fa-ut*** claims for itself to adhere to ***re-fa-sol***; but if it reaches ***la***, the proposed place will have a fifth. Beyond ***la***, there is no natural voice in this formal doctrine. When you wish to propose other gamuts with prepared signs, that will happen in the completion of all things contained in the following figure.

**P**ater <note>original: "Pater" - likely an address to a teacher or "Father" of the order.</note>, from this it is clear that neither ***fa*** nor ***ut*** can be made against ***re***, such as the third gamut and the seventh in ***A-re***; nor can ***re*** or ***la*** become a third ***fa***. This is clear from the operation of the third hexachord <term>hexachord: a six-note scale starting on C, F, or G.</term> in relation to ***F-fa-ut***. In the same way, other voices cannot be made against each other in different hexachords, which I leave to the diligent reader to investigate. Pay attention, therefore, reader, to how many things have been followed by the followers of Guido. This entire system is for those who understand the doctrine correctly; however, they who only know the doctrine of a single gamut think they know much, even when they do not know how to transition from one to another.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on musical notation, moving from the geometric shapes of notes (covered in the previous treatise) to the "harmonic numbers" or mathematical proportions that govern how long notes are held relative to one another.</meta>

# First Treatise

## Third part, in which harmonic numbers are treated extensively

We have already clearly demonstrated through geometric demonstration—in the third treatise of the first part, in the chapter on note shapes—that two **maxima** longs contain two **breves** and a **long**. <note>The author is referring to "mensural notation," the system used from the 13th to 16th centuries where the duration of a note was determined by its context and the "mode" or "time" of the piece.</note> In this part, however, which is entirely concerned with numbers, it remains to be said how that same note can be worth three or more units.

Once the consideration of <term>tempus<gloss>time</gloss></term> is accepted—which is recognized by the beating of the pulse—we must know whether it happens that notes are doubled, tripled, or quadrupled, or even halved, or divided into thirds or fourths. 

For the first consideration is called <term>modus<gloss>mode</gloss></term>. This is not named from *modulating* or *moving*, as was said above, but we believe it is named from the *modification* or computation of times. According to Master **Franco** <note>original: "magistrū frāchonez"; likely referring to Franco of Cologne (c. 1250), one of the most influential music theorists of the Middle Ages.</note>, it is the conjunction of sound and time measured by long notes. This is nothing other than saying that *modus* is the conjunction of the proportion that arises from long and short notes by measuring one measure—that is, the *tempus* itself. 

Then there is <term>prolatio<gloss>prolation</gloss></term>, named from "bringing forth" (*proferendo*). When time is divided into parts, it is "brought forth" better, just as happens in the scanning of verses in poetry. **Egidius de Murino** <note>A 14th-century theorist known for his work on motets and rhythmic notation.</note> says that it is called *prolatio* because it divides time into smaller parts so that it may be better pronounced; for, as he says, it would be absurd if something that can be pronounced could not also be written.

Therefore, in this third part on numbers, we have three species: **modus**, **tempus**, and **prolatio**. Just as *modus* can be doubled, so can *prolatio* be divided in half. When we join the *modi* together, we call it the "greater mode" (*modus maior*). Conversely, when *prolatio* is cut, it is called "greater prolation" (*maior prolatio*). 

If we place <term>tempus</term> as the unit on the tip of the middle finger, <term>modus</term> corresponds on the index finger by an increase, and "lesser prolation" (*minori prolationi*) is placed on the middle finger by division. Likewise, "greater mode" (*modus maior*) on the thumb and "greater prolation" (*prolatio maior*) on the little finger will be correctly situated. <note>The author is using the fingers of the hand as a mnemonic device, a common teaching tool in the Middle Ages (like the famous "Guidonian Hand" for pitches) to help students remember the hierarchy of rhythmic levels.</note> 

Following the similarity of the matter mentioned above, a simple note is called *tempus*. Therefore, **minor mode** will have the **longa**. **Major mode** will have the **maxima**, which is called a "double long" by many. **Lesser prolation** will have the **semibreve** (which is also called "minor"), and **greater prolation** will have the **minim**. After these notes, a **dot** is placed for augmenting, dividing, or reducing. After these are the diminished notes, namely the *scitonia*, the *cursee*, the *minaria*, and the *fusea*. <note>These are likely regional or archaic terms for very fast notes like the semiminim or fusa.</note> We have spoken more extensively about their names and the many ways each note is shaped in the chapter on notes. Here, we will briefly show their knowledge strictly and through their correlations. Knowledge of them proceeds from the **breve**, which note...

<summary>This page introduces the three levels of rhythmic measurement in medieval music: Modus, Tempus, and Prolatio. It explains these concepts by citing authorities like Franco of Cologne and using a mnemonic system based on the human hand and pulse.</summary>
<keywords>mensural notation, Modus, Tempus, Prolatio, Franco of Cologne, Egidius de Murino, rhythmic theory, Longa, Maxima, Breve, Minim, Semibreve</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>Continuity check: The previous page concluded with the catchword "la," which begins the first word of this page, "la [quadrata]" (the square note).</meta>

### Part Three

The **square note** <note>original: "la [notula] est quadrata"</note> is written like this [symbol]. But if it has a stroke on the right side, either reaching upwards or downwards in this manner [symbol], it becomes a **Long**. 

If the body of the Long is doubled like this [symbol], it is called a **Maxima** <note>meaning "the largest"</note>. It is called this by the moderns, though by the ancients it was more correctly called the "Double Long." This occurs by increasing the value of the **Breve** itself. 

If a note is cut diametrically from corner to corner in this way [symbol], two **Semibreves** are created, which were called "Minors" by the ancients. However, if a Semibreve has a stroke pointing upwards or downwards [symbol], it becomes a **Minim** <note>meaning "the least"</note>. 

If the Minim is blackened [symbol], it is a **Semiminim**. If it has a hooked tail at the top [symbol], it is called a *cursea* or *cursuta* <note>original: "cursea siue cursuta," likely referring to a "running" note</note>, or a *crocea* <note>meaning "saffron-colored" or a "crotchet"</note>. If it is formed like this [symbol], it is called a *minaria*. If in this manner [symbol], it is called a **Fusa**. 

In this way, all the individual notes are recognized. However, because Semibreves, Breves, and Longs are identified differently in other contexts, we will now provide the method for recognizing the Long and the Semibreve within ligatures, and thus the Breve will also be known.

Notes are figured in diverse and varied ways when "ligated" <term>ligature: a symbol combining two or more notes into a single shape, common in medieval and Renaissance music</term>. 

A ligature of two or more notes where the first note is higher than the following one, and lacks a stroke on the left side (either upwards or downwards), is called a **Long**. But if any of these conditions are missing, it will not be a Long. Furthermore, if a note is not square, whether it is positioned higher or lower, if it lacks any stroke, it is always a Long. The final note of a ligature, if it possesses the opposite conditions, is also called a Long. 

[Example]. 

In a ligature of two or more notes in a rising <term>arsi</term> or falling <term>thesi</term> melody—whether they are square or not, or if the first is square and the others are not—if the first note has an ascending stroke on the left side, the first two notes are always called **Semibreves**, even if they appear alone. 

We will mark the following example, designating **Longs** with the letter ***l*** and **Semibreves** with the sign ***s***, so that when we later find similar ligatures in musical compositions, we may easily recognize which are Longs and which are Semibreves. The remaining notes <note>original: "odas," referring here to the musical sounds or notes</note> which are neither Semibreves nor Longs are understood to be **Breves**.

One might wonder, and not without merit, why these notes are named as they are. We say that because the duration is short, the note is called a **Breve** <note>meaning "brief" or "short"</note>. This brings to mind the scripture: "For a thousand years in your sight are but as yesterday when it is past" <note>A reference to Psalm 90:4 (Vulgate 89:4), comparing musical time to divine time</note>. 

The **Long** is so named because it is greater than the Breve itself. The **Semibreve** <note>literally "half-brief"</note> takes its name from reality: for a Breve may be divided into two Semibreves. When it is divided into three, they are called "Minors." 

The **Maxima** <note>Greatest</note> and **Minima** <note>Smallest</note> are named by comparison. If the Breve is placed as the "positive" degree in terms of its divisions, it is called "great"; if it is great, then the Long is necessarily "greater" <note>the comparative degree</note>; and thus the Maxima follows as the "greatest" <note>the superlative degree</note>. Yet that same Breve is also called "small" in respect to the Long; and it is "most small" <note>Minima</note> if the "small" note is made "smaller"...

<summary>This page explains the visual forms and names of musical notes in mensural notation, detailing how shapes like the "Long" and "Breve" change when joined in ligatures. It also provides a philosophical and grammatical justification for the names of the notes based on their relative durations.</summary>
<keywords>mensural notation, Maxima, Longa, Brevis, Semibrevis, Minima, Semiminima, Fusa, ligatures, music theory, Latin translation, musical terminology</keywords>

<page-num>32</page-num>

# First Treatise

is the <term>semibreve</term> <note>original: "semibrevis"; a note value that was originally half of a "breve," now the equivalent of a whole note</note> and the <term>minim</term> <note>original: "minima"; the "smallest" note value at the time of its invention, ancestor of the modern half note</note> follows in the superlative degree. However, the names of these fractions <note>shorter note values</note> were invented by modern writers. Therefore, they do not possess such great authority: such as the one called the <term>semiminim</term> <note>original: "semiminima"; half of a minim, ancestor of the quarter note</note>, because the half-minim is also called "cursive" [running] from the word "course" [cursu] being derived in a similar way; otherwise, in former times, it was called the "triarea minima" because it is smaller in value than the preceding ones. Indeed, some call the <term>fusa</term> <note>original: "fusa"; equivalent to an eighth note</note> by that name from the resemblance of its shape, because it is made in the manner of a spindle [fusus]. Others, however, [say it is so named] because of the song and those spinning around with spindles. But enough regarding these things; we have already spoken of them in the chapter on note-forms. 

Now, however, so that we may be able to understand the values of these small notes, we must know that there are three numbers which we use in this section: namely, the perfect, the imperfect, and the diminished. It is "perfect" when a note is equal to the value of three of the following notes <note>Medieval music theory regarded the number three as "perfect," reflecting the Holy Trinity</note>. It is "imperfect" when it contains two. It is "diminished" when it is placed for only one of the following. 

We call the number three [the ternary] perfect, not merely by persuasion nor by comparison as <term>Johannes de Muris</term> <note>c. 1290–1350, an influential French philosopher and music theorist</note> says: that the Trinity exists in divine matters and in the intellectual soul. For it is a defect in the discipline of <term>mathematics</term> <note>original: "mathesis"</note> to prove something by means of a mere comparison. As for the idea that the third dimension of a body or lines is somewhat natural—we nevertheless also reject these arguments. For in the same way, the number six [the <term>senary</term>] could be said to be prior and more perfect, because it is bounded by six points, and a point is primary in mathematical abstraction. 

But the reasoning which we provide is mathematical: namely, that the number three is called a perfect number because it is equal to its "aliquot" and "quota" parts <note>mathematical terms for divisors; a perfect number is a positive integer that is equal to the sum of its proper divisors</note> taken together. Therefore, it exceeds the number six in perfection. For a "quota part" is said to be any part contained within the number itself. Thus, every aliquot part can be called a "quota" part, but not conversely. Therefore, when certain things in other sciences must be proven, it is necessary that they recur to mathematical demonstrations; for here [in math] it is demonstrated, while there [in music or rhetoric] a comparison will suffice. Those, therefore, who speak of "perfection" in music through comparison [to the Trinity] take away the actual perfection from it. 

This much regarding the perfect number. However, the number two [the <term>binary</term>] is called an "imperfect" number because it is distant from perfection by one unit. But it is called "diminished" because of the number two, which should scarcely be called a number except because in this faculty [music] note-forms are broken down, and it will hold true in respect to two halves. Furthermore, in <term>arithmetic</term>, number is categorized in a three-fold way: namely, perfect, superfluous, and diminished, by comparison to its aliquot parts. Whence the number six, whose aliquot parts are 1, 2, and 3, which joined together complete the number six and do not exceed it, is called "perfect." [Take for example] 12, whose parts 1, 2, 3, 4 [and 6] together...

The author argues that music theory should be based on Euclidean mathematical proofs rather than the theological analogies favored by earlier theorists like Johannes de Muris.</summary>

# Part Three

<note>original: "tertia pars"</note>

...are taken by reason: [if the parts] exceed the surplus, it is considered a <term>deficient number</term> <note>original: "diminutus numerus"; a number whose proper divisors sum to less than the number itself</note>. 10 is such a number, because its parts—namely 1, 2, and 5—do not reach the sum of the whole, which is 8. Similarly [8], whose parts—namely 1, 2, and 4—sum to seven and do not exceed it. Therefore, every inequality is established in either greater or lesser terms. For the former, by a certain immoderate fullness, exceed the quantity of their own body by the large number of their parts <note>referring to abundant numbers, where divisors sum to more than the number</note>. The latter, as if oppressed by poverty and a certain lack in their nature, are composed of a sum of parts smaller than the numbers themselves. But enough of these matters for now; let us now gather the differences of numbers:

## Chapter Two: In which the figures by which they distinguish numbers are shown

**To** reach a true understanding of these numbers, let me declare certain signs that were invented by the ancients from geometric figures. Then we will add others which the moderns <note>original: "neoterici"; likely referring to 15th or 16th-century theorists</note> use. A rectangle with three strokes was placed in this manner <insert>rectangle with 3 vertical lines</insert> for <term>perfect mode</term> <note>original: "modus perfectus"; where a Long note equals three Breves</note>, and with two thus <insert>rectangle with 2 vertical lines</insert> for imperfect. For <term>tempus</term> <note>division of the Breve into Semibreves</note> with <term>prolation</term> <note>division of the Semibreve into Minims</note>, a circle (O) was placed with three dots in the center thus <insert>O with 3 dots</insert>. For the imperfect, a C with two dots in the middle was used thus <insert>C with 2 dots</insert>. If a C was used with three dots thus <insert>C with 3 dots</insert>, the *tempus* was imperfect and the *prolation* was perfect. But if the sign $\Phi$ is placed with two dots thus <insert>$\Phi$ with 2 dots</insert>, it was believed to be perfect *tempus* and imperfect *prolation*. Others indeed used "Indian figures" <note>Arabic numerals</note> in this way:

->3 3 2 2<-
->3 2 3 2<-

The lower number denotes the *tempus*, while the upper denotes the *prolation*. But now, contemporary writers use a mixture of geometric parts and Indian figures. For they use one for the <term>mode</term> and *tempus*, and another for the *tempus* with *prolation*. Each of these is done in four ways. For mode with *tempus* like so: O3 C3 O2 C2. But for *tempus* with *prolation* in this way: ʘ O Ꞓ C. <note>Signs representing a circle with a dot, a plain circle, a semicircle with a dot, and a plain semicircle</note>. This method of signing was discovered by this reasoning: because the circle is a perfect figure, it denotes a perfect species. But when only two parts of that circle are placed <note>the semicircle or "C" shape</note>, they denote two parts of that species, with the third part being lost. Regarding this sign $\Phi$ or this one $\unicode{x1D10A}$ <note>musical symbol for cut C</note>, there is no ambiguity: the first is perfect and the second is imperfect. In the first four-part sign, the Circle or Semicircle shows the *mode*, while the 3 or 2 shows the *tempus*. Therefore, the subtle reader, through what we have set down, will be able to find the perfection of either the *mode* or the *tempus*. In the second sign, which designates the *tempus* in this way, if a dot is in the center...

# First Treatise

...let it be placed to demonstrate the perfection of <term>prolation</term> <note>the division of the semibreve into minims</note>, which if it is not present, indicates imperfection. The same applies to the semicircle, as is clear in the figures.

->Figure of <term>mode</term> <note>the division of the long into breves</note> with <term>time</term> <note>the division of the breve into semibreves</note>  |  Figure of time with prolation<-

**W**e find these figures arranged in this manner because we recognize that they maintain the contrary relationships <note>literally "passions"</note> of numbers among themselves. For in the first four-part sign, the upper sign on the reader's left affirms the perfection of both time and mode; however, in its contrary, it denies the perfection of mode (which is the more universal category). The same logic is found in the <term>subcontraries</term> <note>a term from Aristotelian logic describing the relationship between specific statements</note>. The lower sign on the reader's right receives entirely contrary qualities: and so also the lower left is against the upper right. For that which is denied in one is affirmed in its contradictory other, and vice-versa. If both are denied in one, both are affirmed in its contradictory. They can also be called <term>subalterns</term> because we see that the mode, being more universal, remains the same; the same must be said of the other four-part sign, as was shown in the figures.

**B**ut because in this art, whatever is sung through various fractions and different diminutions must be reduced to a certain complete integrity and determined measure, we ought to know <note>original: "oportet"</note> through the different signs which notes we must hold as whole measures. For <term>measure</term> <note>mensura</note>, as we have said, is that duration of time, without interval, comprised between the <term>diastole</term> <note>expansion</note> and <term>systole</term> <note>contraction</note> of a well-tempered <note>healthy/balanced</note> body. From the unequal alteration of this pulse arise the unequal proportions of music, which we shall speak of shortly. Therefore, when a singer desires to sing correctly and in proportion, he should move his foot or hand or fingers in imitation of that pulse, striking against some place while singing.

**A**nd when he sings according to the first four-part sign, he should place this measure in the <term>breves</term>. For in these signs ○ ⊙ <note>Circle and Dotted Circle</note>, a <term>long</term> will be measured by three units of time, but in these C 𝇌 <note>Semicircle and Dotted Semicircle</note>, it is measured by two. A double-long in the first two ○ ⊙ is worth six units, but in these C 𝇌 it would be worth four. The measure itself in these first two ○ ⊙ cuts through the middle into two parts, namely into two breves or four <term>minims</term>. But in these C 𝇌 it divides equally into three, namely three breves and six minims—unless an unequal comparison is made with the <term>tenor</term> <note>the structural base voice of a composition</note>, because then a certain state of inequality arises, which we shall discuss under "proportions." If, however, he sings by the second four-part sign, he should place the little delay <note>morulam</note> in the <term>semibreves</term> <note>original: "semibrevibus"</note>, and then a breve will be worth three measures in these ○, but only two in these C. And just as it was divided in the others, e... <note>The text breaks off here at the catchword for the next page.</note>

# Part Three

Just as [the breve] is divided into two or three <term>semibreves</term><note>The semibreve was the ancestor of our whole note, but in the Renaissance, it was a much faster unit of time.</note>, so in these [semibreves] are two or three <term>minims</term> contained, depending on whether the sign denotes <term>perfection</term><note>Triple meter</note> or <term>imperfection</term><note>Duple meter</note>. Likewise, they may be divided into four or six <term>semiminims</term>, and this is what is most frequently observed.

Others, however, because of an excessive speeding up of the song, take the <term>mensura</term><note>The 'mensura' or 'tactus' was the physical beat, often a slow down-and-up motion of the hand.</note> that ought to be observed in the breve and place it in the semibreve. And if it was meant to be held in the semibreve, they transfer it to the minim. This is done so that now, for the most part, everyone holds and writes in their compositions so that a full beat of the measure is held in the minim. 

Furthermore, if a sign is placed in the <term>tenor</term><note>The foundational voice part of a polyphonic composition.</note> that is different from the other voices—for instance, if the tenor has [one sign] and the others have $\mathbb{C}$ <note>A 'broken circle' sign, the ancestor of our common time symbol.</note>—the minim of the tenor is worth as much as the semibreve of the others, because it represents a full beat. And if the sign $O2$ is used in the other voices, it is worth as much as a breve. This practice is observed by Ockeghem, Busnois, Dufay, and Johannes de Monte, and other famous men in this art. 

Tinctoris <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), one of the most influential music theorists of the Renaissance.</note>, however, being ignorant of that truth, proposes certain things that should not be brought to light. Indeed, I shall say something about these things in the section on proportions, lest the correct order be disturbed by an ignorant opinion. For Busnois and these other great men are founded in antiquity; just as a quantity is increased on one side by growing, so on the other it is diminished by dividing. For if the ancients placed the measure in the breve, the long, and sometimes the <term>maxima</term><note>The largest/longest note value in medieval music.</note>, so we place it in the breve, the semibreve, and sometimes the minim. But so much for measures; now I will speak of the perfection of other species.

### Chapter Three, in which the signs of the other species are discussed:

It remains for us to declare the figures by which we distinguish numbers in all species, moving from <term>imperfection</term> in the <term>greater mode</term> and <term>prolation</term><note>These terms refer to the complex hierarchy of how long notes were divided into shorter ones.</note>. We will achieve this better if we first briefly recall to memory the knowledge of <term>pauses</term><note>Musical rests.</note> which we set down in the first part. 

The knowledge of rests, just like notes, begins with the rest for the <term>tempus</term><note>The relationship between the breve and the semibreve.</note> itself. This is figured as a line filling the entire space between two staff lines in this manner: [illustrated here]. If it occupies two or three spaces between lines, it is called a "long rest." If it embraces four spaces, it is a "maxima rest," beyond which no rest is called greater. 

There is also a "general rest," which is when everyone is silent together while the song is not yet finished. It is also called a "final rest" because it is always placed at the end of a song. If a breve rest is divided, the half that hangs down from the upper staff line is a semibreve rest. If the part instead rises from the lower line, it is named a minim rest. If the rest is twisted at the top in this way, it is named a semiminim rest. Of the other fractions...

# Treatise the First

On account of its extreme brevity, a rest <note>A silence of a specific rhythmic duration.</note> for this note is not found. However, certain men, such as Johannes de Muris <note>A famous 14th-century French mathematician and music theorist whose works were fundamental to the *Ars Nova* style.</note>—our musician and master to the King of Castile and Spain—have established rests for the *cursea* <note>An early term for a hooked or flagged note, similar to a modern quaver/eighth note.</note> written in this manner: <insert> [music symbol] </insert>. This is based on what occurs with the notes themselves. For if a *semiminima* <note>A "half-minim," equivalent to a modern crotchet or quarter note.</note> has a turned-back head <note>meaning a "flag" or "hook" on the stem.</note>, it becomes a *cursea*, which is half of a *semiminima*. If a *cursea* has a double turn, it creates a *minarea*, which is half of a *cursea*.

Thus, regarding the formation of the rest: if the rest of a minim is turned back at the top, it creates a semiminim rest; if it is turned back twice, it creates a *cursea* rest. We do not deny that this *can* be done, for we recognize it is grounded in the logic of the art; however, we conclude that it *should* not be done. Our reasoning is that since that note is so small that its value can scarcely be grasped in song, how could a singer's breath find rest within such a pause? Therefore, we do not concede that this should be practiced, but rather propose that it be avoided.

Once these rests are understood, we can easily recognize **perfection** and **imperfection** <note>In medieval music, "Perfection" refers to triple meter (groups of three), while "Imperfection" refers to duple meter (groups of two).</note> in other categories. For when we see three *longa* rests placed together—either one preceding two others, or all three separate—we understand without a doubt through the rules of the art that the **Greater Mode** is present, and therefore a *maxima* is worth three *longae*. But if they are placed two by two, we judge it to be **imperfect**.

However, if a single *longa* rest occupies three spaces [on the staff], we recognize the **Lesser Perfection** <note>Also known as the Lesser Mode.</note>, and through this, we know that one *longa* is worth three *breves*. But it is **imperfect** if it occupies only two spaces. Thus, even if the "lesser" is imperfect, the "greater" can be perfect, and vice versa, as is the case with other signs.

The **perfection of Time** (*tempus*) is also denoted by *brevis* rests, according to both us and the ancients. For as Master Franco <note>Referring to Franco of Cologne, the 13th-century theorist who pioneered the system of "Franconian notation" where note shapes represent specific durations.</note> says: if a rest of one *tempus* <note>The relationship between the *brevis* and the *semibrevis*.</note> occupies an entire space [of the staff], it denotes a whole **perfect time**. If it occupies two parts of a space, it demonstrates two parts of a *tempus*. If it occupies only one, it indicates only one minor part.

The "moderns" <note>original: "neoterici"; referring to the practitioners of the *Ars Nova* style.</note>, however, do not do this. Instead, when they wish to indicate two-thirds of a *tempus*, they arrange two semibreve rests next to each other in this manner: <insert> [symbols] </insert>. Then, a **perfect time** can be correctly recognized, because we understand that the whole *brevis* rest is worth these smaller units; since two-thirds are placed there, the whole is implied. For when the *tempus* is **imperfect**, a rest of one *tempus* is worth only as much as two semibreve rests. Why then should two be placed thus <insert> [symbol] </insert> if one were sufficient? It is done in vain to use many things for what can be accomplished by few.

In the same way, concerning **perfect prolation** <note>The relationship between the *semibrevis* and the *minim*.</note>: if two minim rests are found, they denote **perfect prolation**, unless another sign indicates otherwise. For when the prolation is imperfect, a brevis rest is worth only two minims...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical notation, specifically focusing on the internal division of note values (prolation). It maintains the previous page's focus on defining "perfection" (groups of three) and "imperfection" (groups of two) within the mensural system of the late Middle Ages and Renaissance.</meta>

### Part Three

The way in which perfection or imperfection is distinguished in **major prolation** <term>major prolation: a rhythmic system where the semibreve is divided into three minims</term> is not clear to all singers, nor even to certain musicians like our friend Tristan de Silva. He follows the opinion of **Johannes de Muris** <note>original: "io. de muris"; a 14th-century mathematician and music theorist</note> and asserts that major prolation is "perfect" and minor is "imperfect." We intend to disprove this through the authority of the ancients, the examples of modern masters, and mathematical demonstration.

First: **Egidius de Murino** <note>a 14th-century theorist known for his rules on motet composition</note>, in treating the **minim** <note>the basic unit of time in this system</note>, says it "rightly ought to lose a third part," while the "dot" <note>the dot of addition or perfection</note> loses only a half. Therefore, if a minim can lose a third part, I ask: what else is that third part if not a **semiminim**? Thus, three of them correctly fill the whole. It follows then that a minim can be worth three semiminims.

This is evident in the example of **Johannes Ockeghem** <note>original: "io. olregan"; refers to the famous Franco-Flemish composer (c. 1410–1497)</note> in his *Mass on L'homme armé* <note>original: "missa alo me armet"; one of the most famous musical melodies of the 15th century</note>. When he needed to write two semiminims for one minim—as singers like the musician Tristan de Silva would have it—he instead uses "voided minims with curved heads" in this manner <unclear>which</unclear> we have called **curseas** <term>curseas: a term for fast-moving notes, similar to modern eighth or sixteenth notes</term>.

From this, musicians observe that a minim is worth three semiminims. Furthermore, the authority of Egidius and the ancients clearly shows the opposite [of Tristan's view] when he says: "There are also other figures called **imperfect minims**, made in this way, and they have a greater effect than a semiminim because they are full (solid), and a lesser effect than if they had an upright head." From these words it is clear that we are doing the opposite, putting black notes for white. When we put two white *curseas* for a minim, as Ockeghem does, it is because this white note is of greater value than that black one. If two *curseas* fill a minim, then three semiminims would be necessary [for a perfect minim].

It is proven mathematically by the very thing they themselves do: placing the **measure** <term>mensura: the steady pulse or beat</term> in the minim. If the full duration of time is in the minim, and "time" admits perfection and imperfection, it follows that the minim itself can sometimes be divided into two and sometimes into three equal parts. If this were not so, neither would the **breve** <note>a longer note value</note> be so when the measure is placed in it, nor would the **semibreve** for the same reason.

In other ways, those two signs were drawn in four parts by certain ancients as **mood** <term>modus: the relationship between the "long" and the "breve"</term> along with **time** <term>tempus: the relationship between the "breve" and the "semibreve"</term> like this: **oo . co . oc . ce**. This method does not differ from the other, for that which is denoted by the third part makes the **O** <note>a circle, the sign for perfection (three)</note>, and that which is understood by the second part is recognized as **C** <note>a half-circle, the sign for imperfection (two)</note>. If, therefore, these signs are arranged in the present figure, it will be the same as it was in that one.

Others, however, like my teacher **Johannes de Monte**, who was the first who [introduced] me to the study of music...

<summary>The author argues that a minim in "major prolation" is naturally divisible into three parts (semiminims), citing the composer Ockeghem and the theorist Egidius de Murino to refute the views of Tristan de Silva. He uses mathematical logic to show that if the beat falls on the minim, it must be capable of both triple and duple division.</summary>

## First Treatise

<note>original: "Tractat' prim'"</note>

...imbued with these basic principles, he denied that one sign should be placed at the side as we have arranged, and allowed one to be placed under the other in this way:
-> o : c . o . c . <-
as if geometric figures were mixed with the characters of waves.
-> o . o . c : c : o . o . c . o . c . <-
However, so that the higher sign of these holds the place of **3 . 3 . 2 . 2 .**, it is based on the reasoning of other ancients. For if they themselves arrange <term>tempus</term><note>The division of the breve note into semibreves.</note> with <term>prolatio</term><note>The division of the semibreve note into minims.</note> in this manner:
**3 3 3 2**
Since 3 is nothing other than **o** <note>The circle, representing perfection or triple meter.</note> and 2 is nothing other than **c** <note>The semicircle, representing imperfection or duple meter.</note>, it seems reasonable that I should do similarly regarding <term>modus</term> <note>The division of the long into breves.</note> with <term>tempus</term>.
But concerning <term>tempus</term> with <term>prolatio</term>, which we said should be signed as **o c**, he said they should be placed thus because then it is correctly shown that the prolation is enclosed within the time. In this way, the <term>perfection</term> <note>Triple grouping.</note> and <term>imperfection</term> <note>Duple grouping.</note> of both are recognized in a clearer manner. 

However, we told him that the first way is more subtle. For, being grounded in this, we have proven by geometric demonstration that every single note holds the value of the two following it by its very nature. Therefore, when no contrary sign is found, the song must be sung according to its nature: namely, by the binary number. But when, by way of <term>art</term> <note>Human-made rules or craft.</note>, we make it ternary, we write it down with some sign; for if a note was worth only two by nature, through art we make it three. 

Therefore, when we set out either of these signs—**o** or **c**—since there is no sign for prolation, it is judged to be imperfect. But when a sign, that is a point, is placed in the middle of the circle or semicircle, the perfection of the circle is designated, denoting the perfection of the prolation. 

**Master Robert the Englishman** <note>Robertus Anglicus, a 14th-century music theorist.</note>, being ignorant of the properties of notes in geometry, said the opposite: that is, when a sign of <term>tempus</term> was not found, he judged the time to be perfect. He said that almost all songs lacking signs were badly composed. For he, being unskilled in the doctrine, placed <term>art</term> before <term>nature</term>. The opposite of this is manifest, because **art imitates nature** as much as it can; it is not said, however, that nature imitates art, since we often see art go astray, but nature rarely or never.

Furthermore, we discern the perfect from the imperfect by other white signs; for example, if black notes appear among white ones in some song without a "near association," it is a sign that the black note is placed so that it loses a whole part; therefore it does not have a third part unless it is worth three. When we take black notes without a near association, we say they are strictly <term>perfect</term>, yet we say they are "without near association."

<summary>This page discusses the geometric logic behind musical notation signs (circles and semicircles) for time and prolation, while critiquing Robert the Englishman for misunderstanding the relationship between "art" and "nature" in music theory.</summary>
<keywords>Robertus Anglicus, mensural notation, prolation, tempus, perfection, imperfection, art imitates nature, geometry in music</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical notation, moving from the physical appearance of notes (coloration) to the use of signs and written instructions (canons) to indicate rhythm.</meta>

->**Part Three**<-

We say they are close together because if certain black notes follow them such that three are placed where there should be two, the distinction is not clearly seen; for this can happen in both <term>perfect</term><note>In medieval music, "perfect" refers to triple time (groupings of three), reflecting the Holy Trinity.</note> and <term>imperfect</term><note>"Imperfect" refers to duple time (groupings of two).</note> numbers. Early musicians and singers used to draw <term>black notes</term><note>original: "notulas nigras"</note> as we now draw white ones, and they used red ones where we now use <term>filled-in</term><note>original: "integras"</note> notes. They also used to place white notes—that is, those that are hollow in the middle—just as we do now when they did not have red color ready at hand (as Egidius de Murino <note>A 14th-century theorist known for his work on the "ars subtilior" style.</note> prefers). However, Ugolino <note>Ugolino of Orvieto (c. 1380–1452), a famous music theorist.</note> did not examine these matters well: he placed black, red, and hollow notes in the same melody; for it seemed to him that black notes should be perfect, red ones imperfect, and hollow ones diminished. But since we never find this done by anyone else, we judge that it should be done by no one.

In another way, following Master Franco <note>Franco of Cologne, the 13th-century inventor of "mensural" notation where the shape of the note indicates its length.</note>, the older musicians distinguished the perfect from the imperfect by placing a **b** <note>representing "binary" or two-fold</note> above groupings of two, and a **2** <note>likely representing "ternary" or three-fold in this context</note> above groupings of three; thus the difference in the value of the notes was clearly shown. But since notes of imperfect <term>mode</term><note>The relationship between the largest note values (Longs and Breves).</note> were sometimes sung in perfect <term>time</term><note>The relationship between Breves and Semibreves.</note>, and notes of perfect mode in imperfect time, the former were marked with a **b** (having found the binary division), while the following were shaped as a **Ɑ** <note>A reversed 'C' or similar sign</note>, denoting the opposite of the preceding ones.

Therefore, just as those err by omission who judge the perfect species of notes poorly without any sign, so too do those err by excess who add a second sign when one is enough to distinguish the perfection. For example, if a <term>long rest</term> occupies three spaces, those who add the sign **o** <note>The circle indicating triple/perfect time.</note> are in error. Likewise, if binary <term>semibreve rests</term> are found in this way, it is superfluous to place this **o** or this **Ɑ** if the rests are already so arranged—especially if both are found, since otherwise someone might say the sign was placed to denote what was missing. So much for the shapes of notes.

# Chapter Four, in which canons and subscriptions are subtly explained

I do not think it should be passed over in silence if a composer wishes to write a <term>subscription</term><note>original: "subscriptio"; a short written instruction placed under the music.</note> beneath the song, through which the perfection, imperfection, or diminution can be recognized without any other sign. Or, if the music is marked in one way, the composer might issue a contrary instruction through a **canon** <note>A "rule" or instruction that explains how to perform a piece of music, often as a riddle or logic puzzle.</note> or subscription. Indeed, it is called a "subscription" because it is always written under the **Tenor** <note>The foundational voice-part of a medieval composition.</note>. A **Canon**, however, is so called because it is a rule...

<summary>The author discusses the history and proper use of note coloration and time signatures, critiquing Ugolino of Orvieto's methods before introducing the use of written instructions (canons) to guide performers.</summary>
<keywords>coloration, perfect time, imperfect time, Egidius de Murino, Ugolino of Orvieto, Franco of Cologne, canons, subscriptions, musical rests, mensural notation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical "canons"—riddles or instructions that tell singers how to perform a piece of music in a way that is not immediately obvious from the written notes. The author, likely Johannes Tinctoris, references both famous works by Antoine Busnois and his own compositions.</meta>

# First Treatise

The composer suggests his will through a certain ambiguity, cloaking it obscurely and in riddles. For example, in the Mass *Se la face ay pale* <note>original: "si la face appale"; a famous work by Guillaume Du Fay based on a secular song "If my face is pale."</note>, where the "sea" <note>The Latin *pontus* might refer to a specific part of the music or a pun on the composer's name or a bridge/tenor part.</note> increases in triple and double [proportions], and where it remains silent. Sometimes a **canon** <note>In this context, a "canon" is a rule or verbal instruction, not just a round like "Row, Row, Row Your Boat."</note> instructs the performers to sing by the contrary: those beginning at the end finish at the beginning, as Busnois <note>Antoine Busnois (c. 1430–1492), a highly influential Franco-Flemish composer known for complex musical puzzles.</note> did, writing: "Where Alpha is, let O [Omega] be; and where O is, let it be the end." <note>original: "ubi alpha ibi o ⁊ ubi o finis esto"; this describes a retrograde canon where the music is played backward.</note> Indeed, we ourselves placed something similar using secret words in a certain song, saying in the voice called the *contratenor*: "thus it is sung by the canon."

The location of the notes can also be changed. For example, Busnois wrote: "Lest you be ignorant, take the *Lichanos Hypaton*." <note>original: "nescires cacefatõ sume. licãos ypaton"; technical Greek names for specific notes in the ancient musical system.</note> For the first little note is written on G, which is the *Lichanos Meson*, yet the canon instructs the singer to place it on D, which is the location of the *Lichanos Hypaton*.

The manner of the canon’s progression is also changed, so that the voice is lowered by as much as it was supposed to be raised. Busnois did this with the phrase: "the leader of the opposite-sound quenches the leader of the rising-tone." <note>original: "antiphrasis sbenorizar ipos dux epitrõzizar"; Tinctoris is likely quoting a mix of Greek-sounding jargon used by Busnois to describe musical inversion.</note> The meaning of this is: "let that which was to be done above be done below, and vice versa." Similarly, in another place: "let the responses be joined," where there is an **arsis** <note>a "rise" or upward motion</note> and vice versa, so that the voice is raised in the same amount it seemed it should be lowered.

Sometimes, multiple voices arise from a single written voice in a "fugue" <note>An early term for a canon, where one voice "flees" and the other "follows."</note>—either in unison, or at the fourth (*diatessaron*), the fifth (*diapente*), or even the octave (*diapason*). We did this ourselves in a certain verse of the *Magnificat*: a fugue of two voices in unison, with the "perfect number" <note>a reference to rhythmic groupings of three</note> preserved. For there is only one voice written, and after six small delays <note>rests</note>, a second voice follows it on the same pitch.

We also spoke of this in the Mass we composed on the song *The Armed Man* <note>original: "missa quaz lhõme arme"; the famous "L'homme armé" melody used by almost every great Renaissance composer.</note>: "While we were reading Boethius <note>The most authoritative Roman writer on music theory, whose works were studied for over a millennium.</note> as our guide in music, let there be a harmonic mean, and let each voice preserve its own number." For the first voice leads; then another follows it in unison after eight rests, though it has no proportion to any other voice to which a relation might be made. Another follows after six rests, beginning a fourth below; another, while waiting for four rests, begins a fifth below that one; and finally, one begins an octave below the first. Thus, "four rivers flowed from a single fountain." <note>A poetic way of saying four distinct parts are derived from one single line of music.</note>

But in the motet *You, the Light* <note>original: "Tu lumen"</note>, where we placed it in the "perfection of minims," it is sung through the three **genera** of melodies <note>The Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic scales of ancient Greece.</note>, which we composed while we were lecturing publicly in Bologna. We indicated that each vowel, denoted by syllables on the lines and spaces, is worth six measures, just as if "C" were the time-sign, since a rest of one *tempus* <note>a unit of musical time</note> is placed at the beginning; therefore, each individual syllable denotes one *tempus*.

What these three genera of melodies are, we discussed in the first part [of this work]. For it is sung three times: the first time, the second note is raised from the first by a minor third; the second time by a whole tone; and the third time by a semitone. We have proposed many other canons with musical terms; our elders used to do well in their teaching...

<summary>This page discusses "canons"—riddles or rules used by composers like Busnois and the author to derive complex multi-voice music from a single written line. It highlights techniques like inversion, retrograde motion, and the use of ancient Greek musical theory to create intellectual puzzles for performers.</summary>
<keywords>canon, Antoine Busnois, musical riddles, inversion, retrograde, L'homme armé, Boethius, musical proportions, Bologna, Greek genera</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical theory, specifically focusing on the construction and interpretation of "canons"—riddles or verbal rules that instruct a singer on how to perform a musical part. It links these rules to liturgical and scriptural phrases.</meta>

# Part Three

To demonstrate their intelligence, those who are unlearned—wishing to imitate the masters—establish <term>canons</term> <note>In this context, a "canon" is a rule or a verbal riddle that tells a singer how to manipulate the written notes (e.g., "sing this backward" or "omit certain notes").</note> supported only by their own imagination. I will place none of those here, so that they may be forgotten, as they are not steeped in true doctrine. Others, however, apply the methods of Holy Scripture, such as "descending into the deep like a stone." In music, "the deep" refers to the <term>octave</term> <note>The interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency.</note> below any given voice. But on the contrary, when it says, *we hung up our instruments* <note>original: "suspendimus organa nostra"; a reference to Psalm 137:2.</note>, we also attribute canons to Holy Scripture, many of which we have established. For instance, in a <term>canon</term> on the note *Re*, using the phrase *that they may rest from their labors* <note>original: "ut requiescant a laboribus suis"; from Revelation 14:13.</note>, we suggest that the note *Re* should remain silent while the others sing; however, one must still count the value of the notes during these rests.

But when it is said secondly, *if you hold with the Lord Agamemnon of the head, you will lose no hairs* <note>This appears to be a mnemonic or a cryptic humanist riddle used to indicate which notes to keep or discard.</note>, it suggests that in the <term>paranete</term> and <term>nete</term> of the <term>synemmenon</term> <note>Ancient Greek names for specific pitches in the medieval musical system: Paranete Synemmenon is usually the note D above middle C; Nete Synemmenon is the F above that.</note>, those notes are not lost, but rather their works follow them—meaning all those small notes previously omitted in the canon must be resumed. Thus, let the note that was omitted at *Ut* <note>The note C.</note> follow the small note placed at *Ut*; and those that rested at *Re* <note>The note D.</note> should "cry out" again by working after the note *Re*, so that the words harmonize with the actions. 

And since we know from what was said above that the <term>paranete synemmenon</term> is the pitch *K* and in the <term>conjunct</term> [tetrachord] the <term>nete</term> is the pitch *L*, it follows that their "works" follow them in a <term>diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note> "reclamation." And so, when the small notes in the second part are arranged thus: *G, H*, after the addition of the "works" is made, they should be sung as *F, K, G, L, L, H*, so that the value of the preceding notes is fully resumed.

So too in the high part where we said, *let it rest until the desired thing comes*, we wish to show that the note which was on *F* (which is called *Ut* in common speech) should rest—that is, be silent—until the end is reached. But when we said in the following part, *and like the days of a hired hand* <note>original: "et sicut mercenarii dies eius"; from Job 7:1.</note>, we wish to show (as above) that whatever remained unfinished in the first part, they should resume in the second at the <term>paranete synemmenon</term>. We did this in the high canon in the manner of a "hired hand," whose days in singing are calculated toward the fulfillment of the others.

Furthermore, when it is said, *do not remember* <note>original: "ne recordetis"; a play on the musical syllable 'Re'.</note>, it is clearly shown that the note *Re* should not be placed on the string; we understand the same for *Re* and *Mi*. When we place *nor remember* <note>original: "neque reminiscaris"; another play on the syllable 'Re'.</note>, the values must still be sung. But when we said at the penultimate point, *may they rest in peace* <note>original: "requiescant in pace".</note>, we clearly demonstrate that the note *Re* should be placed neither in the singing nor in the rest, but having left that touch, we transfer ourselves to another voice. Finally, when it says *Amen*, we understand it is to be done in the same way.

We have seen the canons of others through many examples; we ourselves have also established very many. However, because scientific knowledge of every specific detail cannot always be grasped—or if it is, some tiny part always remains confused—these things said regarding canons shall suffice for the sharpening and refining of wits.

<keywords>canon, music theory, solmization, Ut, Re, Mi, diapente, octave, paranete synemmenon, nete, scriptural interpretation in music</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion on musical ratios from the previous volume, transitioning from simple proportions between two numbers to "proportionalities," which compare two or more ratios.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Third Treatise</header>

# Third Treatise

## Chapter One: In which the threefold kind of proportionalities is subtly discussed

Just as the various kinds of proportions are derived from the multiplication of numbers, so too is <term>proportionality</term><note>The author distinguishes between a "proportion" (a single ratio like 2:1) and "proportionality" (a comparison of two ratios, like 2:1 = 4:2).</note> created from the perceived mixture of proportions. For a proportion is the relationship of two numbers to one each other, while proportionality is the relationship of two proportions to each other. Because we have investigated certain things regarding proportions up to this point, it remains for us to discuss certain practical necessities regarding proportionalities to complete this first volume.

This proportionality is divided into "continuous" and "separated." It is continuous when the middle number is taken twice when compared to the outer numbers, such as 4, 6, and 9. For we say that just as four is related to six, so six is related to nine; because a <term>sesquialtera</term><note>original: "sesqualtera." A ratio of 3:2, where the larger number contains the smaller plus its half (e.g., 6 is 4 + 2).</note> ratio exists in both cases. However, when there are not one but two middle numbers, it is called separated or discontinuous, as in these numbers: 4, 6, 8, 12. The progression works in this way: just as six relates to four, so twelve relates to eight. If we then permute the terms, we conclude thus: therefore, just as eight relates to four, so twelve relates to six. In the first progression, there is a <term>sesquialtera</term> ratio on both sides, but in the conclusion, there is a <term>dupla</term><note>A 2:1 ratio (an octave in music).</note> ratio.

If we wish to argue this way using musical voices, we ought to take four voices from the <term>monochord</term><note>A single-stringed instrument used by theorists to measure musical intervals by dividing the string length.</note> that relate to each other in this manner. Let these be **a, c, d, f**, in which we will make the progression as follows: just as **a** is to **c**, so **d** is to **f**. By permuting the letters, we conclude that just as **a** is to **d**, so **c** is to **f**. In the first part, the span is an interval of a <term>tritone</term><note>original: "tritoni." An interval of three whole tones, known for its instability.</note>. But in the resulting part of both proportions, the interval is a <term>diatessaron</term><note>original: "diatessaron." The musical interval of a perfect fourth (ratio of 4:3).</note>. 

In this disjointed proportionality, several proportions can also be joined together, as in these numbers: 2, 3; 4, 6; 8, 12. Then the progression happens in this way: just as 2 is to 3, so 4 is to 6, and 8 is to 12; because the <term>sesquialtera</term> proportion is preserved in all of them. By joining the smaller numbers separated from the larger ones, we conclude in this way: therefore, just as 2, 3, and 4 relate among themselves, so 6, 8, and 12 relate among themselves. In musical voices, it works in this fashion: if we have **c, d, e** and **f, g, h**, we argue that just as **c** is to **f**, so **d** is to **g**, and likewise **e** is to **h**; for in all of them, the consonance is a <term>diatessaron</term>. Furthermore, by joining the first three voices separated from the higher ones, we conclude: therefore, just as **c, d, e** relate among themselves, so **f, g, h** relate among themselves; for in all of them, a <term>ditone</term><note>original: "bis tonus." Literally "two tones," which is a major third.</note> creates the difference. This proportionality is varied in many and various other ways, of which we shall speak more broadly a little later—that is, in the second volume.

<summary>This page defines and distinguishes between proportions and proportionalities (ratios of ratios), illustrating the concepts of continuous and discontinuous proportions using both arithmetic examples and musical intervals on a monochord.</summary>
<keywords>proportionality, music theory, monochord, sesquialtera, diatessaron, tritone, ditone, ratios, mathematical music, acoustics</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical proportions as they apply to musical theory, following the introduction of basic numerical relationships. It marks the beginning of the "Third Part" of the treatise.</meta>

-># Third Part<-

Music: again, that which is continuous receives a threefold variation: either the difference <note>original: "excessus," referring to the numerical gap between terms</note> of numbers is considered, or the proportion in both is beheld, or the comparison of differences and terms is made equal. For the first is <term>arithmetic</term> <note>a sequence where the difference between numbers is constant, e.g., 1, 2, 3</note>, the second is called <term>geometric</term> <note>a sequence where the ratio between numbers is constant, e.g., 1, 2, 4</note>. But the third creates a sweet and delightful harmony. Therefore, having discussed these three, we will bring an end to this first volume of practical things.

When, therefore, we find three continuous numbers, or those separated by an equal distance, we say there is an arithmetic proportionality between them—as in these numbers: 1, 2, 3. For the middle term [2] exceeds the smaller [1] by the same quantity that it is surpassed by the larger [3]: namely, by one unit. Therefore, it is an arithmetic proportion because equality is observed in the difference and not in the proportions. Similarly, in these numbers: 2, 4, 6, the number two <note>original: "binarius"</note> makes the difference; and in these: 3, 6, 9, the number three <note>original: "ternarius"</note> does so, and so on in this order. For if one follows such tracks, no error will lead them away from this likeness.

In this <term>mean</term> <note>original: "medietate," the middle point or relationship between numbers</note>, however, it is noted that in smaller terms, larger proportions are found, while in larger terms, smaller comparisons must be found—as in these: 4, 6, 8. In the smaller terms [4 and 6], a <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>a 3:2 ratio, which forms a perfect fifth in music</note> is found; but in the larger terms [6 and 8], a <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>a 4:3 ratio, which forms a perfect fourth in music</note> is discovered.

But let the geometric mean, which follows this, be explained. This alone, or most especially, can be called "proportionality," because in it the observation is placed in the same proportions of terms, whether in larger or smaller numbers. In this indeed, an equal proportion is always maintained, while the specific quantity of the numbers is neglected. For it is the contrary of the arithmetic mean. As in these: 1, 2, 4, or in these: 6, 12, 24, the proportion is double in both cases. So it is also in the triple: as in these: 1, 3, 9, or in these: 2, 6, 18; and in the quadruple and others similarly. In this proportionality, however, the property is noted that in larger or smaller terms, the proportions are always equal.

### Chapter Two, in which the harmonic mean is discerned

But the <term>harmonic</term> mean is that which is established neither by the same differences nor by equal proportions. Instead, it is that in which the relationship of the largest number to the smallest is the same as the comparison of the difference between the largest and the middle to the difference between the middle and the smallest—as in these terms: 3, 4, 6. For the six exceeds the four by its third part, which is two. But the four exceeds the three by its fourth part, which is one. <note>In this example (3, 4, 6), the ratio of the outer terms (6:3) is 2:1. The difference between 6 and 4 is 2, and the difference between 4 and 3 is 1. The ratio of those differences (2:1) matches the ratio of the outer terms (2:1), making it a harmonic mean.</note> Therefore, in these, neither is the same proportion of terms found, nor are the same differences discovered. It is, however, as...

<keywords>arithmetic mean, geometric mean, harmonic mean, musical proportions, sesquialtera, sesquitertia, Pythagorean tuning, music theory</keywords>

# Third Treatise

The ratio of the largest term to the smallest is the same as the ratio of the difference between the largest and the middle to the difference between the middle and the last. This is clear because the difference between the middle and the smallest is one (unity), and the difference from the middle to the larger term makes two. Therefore, a double proportion is found between those differences, which is the same proportion the largest—namely, six—held to the smallest—that is, three. <note>The author is describing the Harmonic Mean using the series 6, 4, 3. The ratio of the terms 6:3 is 2:1. The difference between 6 and 4 is 2; the difference between 4 and 3 is 1. The ratio of these differences (2:1) matches the ratio of the outer terms.</note> Moreover, the property of this mean is the opposite of the **arithmetic mean**. For in that arithmetic mean, the ratio was greater among the smaller terms and smaller among the larger ones. In this harmonic mean, however, it is the opposite: because the greater ratio is found in the larger terms, and a smaller relationship is repeated in the smaller numbers.

And so, the **arithmetic mean** is compared to that state which is ruled by the few <note>an oligarchy</note>. This is because a greater proportion is maintained in its smaller terms. **Geometric proportionality** is in a way "popular" <note>representative of a democracy</note>, for it is composed of an equal proportionality among all, whether in the larger or smaller terms; there is among all a certain parity of the mean, preserving an equal right in proportions. But the **musical mean** <note>the harmonic mean</note> is said to belong to the best states <note>an aristocracy or a well-ordered republic</note>, because in the larger terms the greater proportionality is found.

For this reason, these proportionalities are named thus: one <term>arithmetic</term>, another <term>geometric</term>, and another <term>harmonic</term>. The reason is that the arithmetic arrangement divides quantities only by equal differences. The geometric joins terms by an equal ratio. But the harmonic refers to something else, because it has the observation of proportion neither solely in the terms nor solely in the differences, but in both together. 

For you will frequently find the proportions of the musical consonances themselves—which they call <term>symphonies</term>—almost exclusively in this mean. For the symphony of the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth</note> consists in an <term>epitrita</term> proportion <note>original: "epitrita"; a ratio of 4:3</note> such as 4 to 3. The <term>diapente</term> consonance <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a perfect fifth</note> is in a <term>hemiolia</term> proportion <note>original: "hemiolia"; a ratio of 3:2</note> such as 6 to 4. And the concord of all, the <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave</note>, consists in a double ratio, such as 6 to 3. In this mean, therefore, we find these three simple symphonies by comparing the terms. 

But if the comparison is made to the differences of the terms, we will create other symphonies that are not simple: for instance, if the relationship is made from the smallest term to the difference between the smallest and the middle, it will maintain a triple proportion, from which an octave-plus-fifth consonance <note>original: "diapason et diapente"</note> will result. But if we make a relation from the middle term to the difference between itself and the smallest, we will find a quadruple proportion, which will sound as a double octave <note>original: "bis diapason"</note> consonance. But if these same numbers are multiplied by two, as...

<header>the third part</header>

...if the numbers 6, 8, 9, 12 are produced, the harmonies <note>original: "consonantie"</note> will remain. But between 8 and 9, another number can be inserted which, when compared to the outer limits, will preserve the same proportions that the number 8 held, but in the opposite way. For here, the number 9 maintains a **fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron," a 4:3 ratio</note> relative to the lower parts and a **fifth** <note>original: "diapente," a 3:2 ratio</note> relative to the higher parts. The number 8, however, does the opposite. Relative to each other, they maintain **sesquioctave** <note>a 9:8 ratio</note> proportions, from which the species called a **whole tone** <note>original: "tonus"</note> arises. For this species is the difference by which a fifth exceeds a fourth. If we multiply 9 and 8 by two, we will have 18 and 16, which we know to be in the same 9:8 proportion. Between these—as **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard textbook for medieval music theory</note> says—a middle number is placed, namely 17. When compared to the larger number [18], it yields a **minor semitone**; compared to the smaller [16], it yields a **major semitone**. For the 17:16 proportion <note>original: "sesquidecimesexta"</note> is larger than the 18:17 comparison <note>original: "sesquidecimeseptima"</note>. However, for the sake of new singers, it is permitted to discuss once more and more clearly how we understand harmonies to arise from proportions.

## Chapter Three, in which the primary divisions of the monochord are applied to the ratios of numbers:

<note>Small guide letter 'n' in the margin for an intended initial capital 'I' or 'N'. The text likely begins 'In'.</note>
<insert>I</insert>n the first division of our regular **monochord** <note>A single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate mathematical musical ratios</note>, we said that Boethius subtly divided his own regular monochord by numbers and measurement. We, however, for the sake of beginners, have divided ours through continuous quantity by common fractions, so that it would not be necessary for the student to have first mastered **arithmetic** and **geometry**. To do otherwise would be to fall into the very error we have forbidden. For we said that none of these things is necessary for grasping our teaching, provided the student is instructed in the first rudiments. Because of this, we said the string should be divided in the middle, or the length doubled or tripled, or divided into three parts—terms which are very well known to common folk. Now, however, because we have determined certain things regarding **discrete quantity** <note>The study of individual numbers (arithmetic) rather than continuous measurements (geometry)</note>—that is, regarding numbers and the proportions of numbers—which we recognized to be more necessary for singers, we shall show how those same common divisions of the string we established correspond to the logic of numbers, and in what proportions they consist, in this manner. For example, there is a string in its total length of twenty-four **inches** <note>original: "digitorum," literally "fingers"</note>, which is bounded by points *a* and *d*. When, therefore, we cut it in the middle at point *b*, marking the section, the segment *a–b* of twelve inches corresponds to the segment *a–d* of twenty-four in a **double** <term>2:1 ratio, which creates an octave</term> comparison. If therefore the string in its total length...
