<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals using a monochord (a single-stringed instrument used for measuring intervals). The previous page likely established the total length of the string as 24 units.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>Third Treatise</header>

# Third Treatise

...if you strike the string along its length and, having weighed the sound, place your finger at point **b**, you will find that plucking the string segment **b-q** causes the consonance of the <term>diapason<note>The Greek term for an octave, based on a 2:1 ratio.</note></term> to resound. Thus, the *diapason* is said to exist in a "double" relationship.

When we divide the distance between **b** and **a** in the middle, marking the letter **d** in the center, the string segment **d-q** is established as being 18 <note>The OCR reads '13', but the mathematical context of a 24-unit string requires 18 to create the 4:3 ratio described.</note> fingers [units] long. When compared to the whole, this constitutes a <term>sesquitertia<note>A ratio of 4:3 (literally "one and a third").</note></term> proportion. It is from this that the <term>diatessaron<note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note></term> symphony arises between **a** and **d**. 

Next, when we cut the quantity **b-q** in the middle, marking the letter **p** in the center of the section, it is clear that **p-q** consists of 6 fingers; a number which, when multiplied by four, completes the total of 24. Therefore, its relationship is necessarily quadruple. It follows from this that the melody from the whole string to **p** resounds as a <term>bis diapason<note>A double octave, or a 4:1 ratio.</note></term>. 

When the segment **b-p** is divided in the middle and the section is marked with the letter **l**, we rightly observe that the length **l-q** is 9 fingers. If we compare this to the whole string [24 units], we find a "double superbipartient" <note>original: "dupla superbipartiente"; a ratio of 8:3.</note> relationship, sounding the consonance of the *diapason* and *diatessaron* [an octave plus a fourth]. Regarding this interval, as **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works were the standard music theory textbooks in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> says, only **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the Greco-Roman astronomer and music theorist.</note> admits it among the consonances. However, we shall bypass these matters here, because we will demonstrate them through practical experience in the composition of three and four voices, and we have already spoken much regarding the theory in our later speculations.

Therefore, when dividing the whole string into three parts and—coming from letter **q** or rather from **a**—we place the letter **e** at the first division, we clearly show **e-q** to be 8 fingers. This, multiplied by three, measures 24 exactly; thus, maintaining a triple proportion, it resounds the <term>diapente<note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note></term> and *diapason* [a twelfth, or an octave plus a fifth] relative to the whole string.

->symphony; but e-q is 16, which<-

is repeated as two-thirds of the whole, and through this, it resounds a *diapente* [fifth] with **a**. However, we may cut the distance **b-d** in the middle, marking the section with the letter **f**. Since **b** is shown to have 12 fingers and **d** has 18, we know without doubt that **f** is 15 fingers. If we refer these 15 units to **d** [18], we perceive a <term>sesquiquinta<note>A ratio of 6:5, which produces a minor third.</note></term> relationship. For 18 exceeds 15 by three, which is a fifth part of the smaller number. But if it is compared to **b** [12], we find it to be in a <term>sesquiquarta<note>A ratio of 5:4, which produces a major third.</note></term> comparison. From this latter comparison, the <term>ditone<note>A major third.</note></term> (or *bitonus*) consonance is made; from the former, the species of <term>semiditone<note>A minor third.</note></term> (or *trihemitone*) is generated, which is clearly composed of a perfect and imperfect tone. 

If a comparison of this same **f** [15] is made to **a** [24], a "supertripartient fifths" <note>original: "supertripartiens quintas"; a ratio of 8:5.</note> relationship is found; for the number 24 exceeds the number 15 by three-fifths of the smaller part. By this comparison, the *diapente* with a semitone—otherwise called a minor sixth—will resound. If we then make the relation of the same **f** to **b**...

<summary>This page uses a 24-unit monochord to mathematically define musical intervals, including the octave, fourth, double octave, and major/minor thirds. The author cites authorities like Boethius and Ptolemy to justify the classification of these mathematical ratios as musical consonances.</summary>

<keywords>monochord, diapason, diatessaron, diapente, Boethius, Ptolemy, musical ratios, ditone, semiditone, sesquialtera, sesquitertia</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of Part Three, continuing the discussion of musical ratios and their practical application on the monochord string.</meta>

# Part Three

We find a <term>superbipartient</term> <note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus two of its parts; here, 15:9 or 5:3</note> proportion between them: for the number fifteen exceeds the number nine by six, and six is exactly composed of two-thirds of nine. This arrangement creates major sixths. Thus, we have made all our divisions very easy, as they are common and not difficult fractions.

<term>Guido</term> <note>Guido d'Arezzo, an 11th-century monk and highly influential music theorist</note> however, teaches that his <term>monochord</term> <note>a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals</note> should be divided by nine steps, which is clearly laborious and tedious to those observing it. This is because, as we have said, the <term>genre</term> <note>the whole-tone interval</note> consists of a <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>a 9:8 ratio</note> proportion. It is more difficult for anyone to repeatedly take an eighth part of something than to take a half or a third part. Through our division, just as through his, the <term>tone</term> <note>a whole step</note> is effectively found—such as the step from D to E—which the numbers 18 and 16 fulfill, or L to M, which are bounded by the numbers 9 and 8. But enough of these things for now; now we must consider which <term>semitones</term> <note>half-steps</note> of the monochord are to be sung and which should be avoided, and let us discuss why one is found to be larger and the other smaller.

### Chapter Nine, in which [we discuss] which semitones are to be sung or avoided

Since it has been said that a tone cannot be divided into two equal <term>semitones</term> <note>the mathematical impossibility of dividing a 9:8 ratio into two equal whole-number ratios</note>, and I have shown that all the spaces of the tones on a perfect instrument are divided into two semitones, it remains to be said which of them should be sung and which avoided; and likewise for the other species of intervals that vary by semitones. Thus, we will orderly complete this first volume, which is primarily refined for practical musicians.
<meta>catchword: -icians</meta>

In the first, "imperfect" art—namely in the first division of the monochord—there is only one semitone that should be avoided: that which Plato called the <term>apotome</term> <note>original: "apotome"; the "major semitone" in Pythagorean tuning, which sounds dissonant in most melodic contexts</note>. Therefore, singers or players of instruments should never make a transition from the pitch or string B-flat to B-natural, nor vice versa. For that semitone is not used in "symphony" <note>harmonious music</note>, as it does not fit in the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, nor in the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, nor in the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note>, nor in other <term>imperfect species</term> <note>intervals like thirds or sixths</note>, nor in those that are discordant and concordant in succession; it is never in use. On the perfect monochord, however, there are many places where a transition in singing must be avoided. Therefore, by way of instruction, we will assign these to practical musicians. For the theorists, we will demonstrate the truth with the strongest reasons in the following volume. Now, regarding the measured figure which was placed in Part 1, Treatise 2, Chapter 5...

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical ratios used to divide a monochord string to create musical intervals, specifically major sixths and whole tones. The author criticizes Guido d'Arezzo's complex division method and begins a practical guide on which semitones are musically useful versus those that are dissonant.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, Guido d'Arezzo, superbipartient ratio, sesquioctave, semitone, apotome, diatessaron, diapente, diapason, B-flat, B-natural, musical temperament</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the "Third Treatise" (Tractatus tertius). It continues a discussion of musical intervals, specifically focusing on the layout of a keyboard-like instrument and which transitions between notes are musically "good" or "bad" based on the tuning of the time.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Third Treatise</header>

# Third Treatise

Let us return to where we were: the first voice or <term>string</term> <note>original: "chorda"</note> is **A**. The second, however, is the first **B**, that is, the small **soft B** <note>original: "b mollis"; this refers to B-flat.</note> added in. This transition is good because it is arranged for <term>harmonies</term> <note>original: "symphonijs"</note> to be a <term>semitone</term> <note>A half-step, the smallest interval in this system.</note> apart; but a transition from the first **B** to the **natural B** <note>The text uses the symbol ♮ for "square B" or B-natural.</note> does not occur. This is because that semitone which is called an <term>apotome</term> <note>A "major semitone" in Pythagorean tuning, which was considered dissonant or "un-singable" in medieval music theory.</note> is not sung. From this **B** to **C** is a good semitone, but from **C** to the first **natural B** is bad. From that one to **D** is good, from which to the second **B** [flat] it is similarly good; from there to **E** it is bad. But from **E** to **F** it is good. From **F** to the second **natural B** is bad, but from that one to **G** is good, from which to the third **B** [flat] it is similarly good. From the third **B** [flat] to **B** [natural] is bad; from **B** to **I** or to **B** is good. From **B** to **natural B**, and so on in this manner, the bad transitions correspond to bad ones and the good to good ones in their <term>octaves</term>.

Furthermore, the transition of "good" and "bad" <term>tones</term> <note>original: "tonus"; a whole step.</note>—which does not move to the very next note, but is always made by skipping one in the middle on this instrument—is explained thus: from **A** to **B** [flat] is a good tone because it is composed of a major and minor semitone. <note>The author is describing the construction of musical steps on a physical scale.</note> Likewise, in the same way, from the letter **B** to the first **natural B**, from which one goes to the second **B** [flat], it is bad; in the same way from the second **natural B** to the third **B** [flat], and similarly in their octaves.

As for the rest, the transitions of tones where one is skipped are always good individually. <term>Minor thirds</term> <note>original: "triemtonia"; literally "three semitones."</note> where two keys are skipped are everywhere good, unless an accidental order is mixed with another accidental, such as from the first **B** [flat] to the first **natural B**, and from the second **B** [flat] to the second **natural B**. Therefore, the third **B** [flat] is not good with the **natural B**.

The <term>major third</term> <note>original: "ditonus"; literally "two tones."</note> which is made by skipping four keys is everywhere good, except from the letter **B** to the second **B** [flat], and from the first **natural B** to **F**, nor from **E** to the third **B** [flat], nor from the second **natural B** to **B** or to **I**; and so it is in their octaves.

The <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, which occurs at the sixth key on this instrument, is always a good voice everywhere, except from the third **B** [flat] to the third **natural B** and in its octaves. The <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, which occurs at the eighth key, is everywhere good except from the first **square B** <note>B-natural</note> to the third **B** [flat], because the perfect fifth is at the fourth **B**.

Regarding the **minor sixth**, which always occurs at the ninth string, we feel the same as we do regarding the **minor third**. For the string that was lacking a minor third will also lack a minor sixth. Likewise for the **major sixth** and **major third**: for the string that lacked a major third is deprived of a major sixth. The same applies to the **major or minor seventh**, just as with the tone and semitone; however, these do not relate to the octave in the same way as those others relate to the source.

Our contemporaries arrange these strings, or rather the <term>keys</term> <note>original: "teclae"; the physical levers of the keyboard.</note> as they are commonly called, which strike the strings on the <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used for teaching intervals and tuning.</note> so that the movements of the natural order proceed in a straight path...

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical and musical relationships between notes on a keyboard instrument, categorizing specific intervals (thirds, fourths, fifths) as "good" or "bad" based on their tuning. It concludes by noting that modern builders call these control mechanisms "keys" (teclae).</summary>
<keywords>music theory, keyboard layout, monochord, intervals, semitone, B-flat, B-natural, diatessaron, diapente, medieval tuning</keywords>

->Part Three<-

# Part Three

<note>original: "tertia pars"</note>

As we have shown in the first measured figure, regarding the *synemmenon* <note>The Greek term for the "tetrachord of the conjoined," which introduces the B-flat into the medieval scale.</note>. But truly, regarding the conclusion of the *synemmenon* and the accidental orders: they are placed somewhat higher than these, depicted in a different color as appears in the figure. But it must be noted, and noted very well, concerning that string <note>In this context, "chorda" (string) refers to a specific pitch or note on the scale.</note> placed between **b** and **g**. Some less skillful practitioners arrange it in such a way that there is a good semitone with these <note>notes</note>, but with **g** a bad one; and thus they make it resonate a fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note> with the first **b-natural** <note>original: "b quadra"</note>, which is a useless fifth because it is harsh <note>original: "rasor"</note>, and to speak more truly, it should never be made. 

Indeed, if someone should say it is placed there so that when the tenor <note>The fundamental voice part in medieval and Renaissance polyphony.</note> descends to **a** through **b**, the descant <note>The highest voice part.</note> may have a major sixth <note>original: "sextā maiorez"</note> tending toward the octave **b** <note>original: "diapason b"</note>, we respond that a variation must then be made in the tenor—that is, it should descend through the first "conjoined" **b-flat** <note>original: "b mollis"</note>. This will create a major sixth against **g**, and thus the transition will be not only good, but better, sweeter, and smoother. And if a middle voice is interposed, it has a major third on **b**, from which it will come to a fifth—namely, the regular **e** placed above, following the rule—which it does not have if it descends in another way. For the second **b** is not joined to this for the reason indicated.

And if anyone wished to say that the character and conditions which **b** had should also be obtained by **b** <note>The author is likely distinguishing between the various types of B (natural/flat) in different octaves</note>, and since it is demonstrated <note>original: "monstrarum," likely "monstratum"</note> that it has a semitone below it and above it, then **b** should have the same. We respond by saying that the argument does not hold. For **g** possessed that <note>quality</note>, because it claims for itself a total similarity above and below in the *synemmenon* tetrachord; yet not **b**, because it has two whole tones below it. Although the first octave <note>original: "diapes̄," likely "diapason"</note> is stretched in the disjoined <note>The "diazeugmenon" or standard natural scale</note>, and likewise the fourth <note>original: "diatessarō"</note>; nevertheless, it does not have a fourth above the first fifth, but a second. Therefore, that string in the "conjoined" <note>the B-flat scale</note> is a *deuterus* <note>The third mode in the church system, starting on E, but here transposed.</note>, both authentic and plagal. 

Other practitioners say that if this were to happen, the fifth on **b-natural** would not have a middle third which is major to the lower note and minor to the higher note, as we said in the second part, in the third treatise on composition. But this is no obstacle, because since that note of the Phrygian mode is inciting <note>The Phrygian mode was historically associated with excitement or martial energy</note>, it does not matter if it lacks a middle third, or if the major is placed toward the higher note and the minor toward the lower. 

However, some wishing to satisfy both parties have inserted another string between the third **b** and **b**, which they cause to be distant from the third **b** by the space of a comma <note>A very small musical interval, usually the difference between two types of semitones</note>. This, however, is not praised, because it would then be a different, "mixed" genus and not the simple diatonic. Our friend Tristan de Silva <note>Tristanus uazo de silua</note> used to say that another string should be interposed between **f** and the second **e**, and he found this through numbers... 

<summary>The author discusses the tuning of the B-flat (synemmenon) and warns against "harsh" or "useless" fifths created by poor placement of accidentals. He argues for smoother transitions using major sixths and mentions the theories of his contemporary, Tristan de Silva.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, B-flat, synemmenon, diapente, major sixth, Phrygian mode, Tristan de Silva, intervals, tuning, tetrachord</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the conclusion of the technical discussion in the third treatise regarding musical proportions and ratios.</meta>

## Third Treatise

...attested; however, the first <note>The ancient Greek authorities</note> understood better. We shall explain the truth of this matter in the following volume using the most solid mathematical ratios. Now, however, by summarizing what has been said above, let us bring this work to a close.

## Epilogue

<margin>a</margin> **T**ake away, therefore, O most pleasant reader, the sluggish clouds of your former ignorance from your soul, and, irradiated by the clear brightness of this little work of ours, drive away the pitch-black darkness from your eyes. Gaze upon and examine all this equipment <note>original: "supellectilem," referring to the collection of tools, rules, and principles provided in the book</note> of our music. Cast your eyes about. Survey everything. Scrutinize every place. The more clearly you focus your mind, the more you will agree with us. And when, with God leading, you reach the recognition of this most beautiful truth which we have taken care to bring forward for the common benefit: you will give thanks to God and preserve the memory of me. You will look favorably upon this little work and my labor. Indeed, it is necessary that you do so, if you wish to escape the most shameful crime of ingratitude. 

If, however, you find anything dissonant <note>original: "dissonum," a clever musical pun meaning both "out of tune" and "disagreeing"</note> with reason or not in agreement with the truth, I leave room for correction and submit myself to examination. But I ask one thing: that you understand before you disapprove, and do not rush headlong into judgment. I do not fear a fight, if it is fought with reason present and judging. Please consider with how much sweat we have set forth this little book—by refuting the trivial opinions of certain musicians and singers, by approving those in whom truth was more favored, and by setting straight with a plumb line <note>an architectural metaphor for perfect vertical alignment and accuracy</note> those things which seemed to have been handed down by others in a disorganized and chaotic manner. 

In this work, if you do not see that flower of eloquence found in Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the Roman statesman whose "De institutione musica" was the fundamental music theory textbook for a millennium</note>, you will grant me pardon. For I have always been more studious of truth than of eloquence. Furthermore, for the most part in this little work, our discourse is addressed to the others <note>meaning the general musical community</note> who are proven to be, for the largest part, unskilled and unrefined. And sometimes it was necessary to interject their discordant sayings, woven with barbaric vocabulary, into the work so that they might be rejected. Therefore, so that I may first gather a brief epitome <note>summary</note> of all these sayings, by discussing the sounds produced successively and in sequence up to the whole harmony...

<summary>Franchinus Gaffurius concludes the third part of his treatise, promising a mathematical defense of his theories in the next volume, and begins an epilogue defending his rigorous approach against "unskilled" singers.</summary>
<keywords>Franchinus Gaffurius, music theory, Boethius, musical proportions, musical education, Renaissance humanism, mathematical ratios</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to "Musica Practica" by Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja. It follows the technical discussions of musical ratios and the monochord, providing a summary of the work's purpose and the formal publication details (colophon).</meta>

[The qualities] of these modes, passing through mundane <note>original: "mundanam", referring to the "music of the spheres" or the mathematical harmony of the universe</note> and human music <note>original: "humanam", referring to the harmony between the human body and soul</note>, we have shown to be wonderful and diverse. And passing through two other kinds of melodies, which were subtly and not without purpose scrutinized by the ancients, we have approached the definition of the harmonies <note>original: "symphonias"</note> of both the ancients and the moderns <note>original: "neotericorum"</note>. Then, having entered through the properties of numbers and the frivolous opinions of singers who hesitate regarding proportions, we approached those proportionalities upon which harmonies rest as if on foundations, and we have subtly shown that our <term>monochord</term> <note>a scientific and musical instrument with a single string used to measure and demonstrate the mathematical intervals of the musical scale</note> is correctly divided by numbers. But whoever desires to perceive the true and very easy path of this discipline without the obscurity of arguments or the long windings of proofs and disproofs: let them seek out our little book of music which we have called the *Introduction* or *Isagogicon* <note>original: "isagogicon", a Greek term for an introductory textbook</note>. There you will find the sum of the matter abundantly, briefly, and clearly. And when you wish to strengthen with reasons those things you see there and to look more deeply, you will return to this work, which is like its citadel and its bulwark. From this [work], you can select suitable tools for explaining and defending that one. Therefore, examine diligently those things devised by others and certain things recently discovered by me; for you will perceive no small amount of utility and pleasure in reading these. And you shall give thanks to the immortal God, the giver of all good things, who established all the liberal arts for the perfection and delight of humans, as I have said before. To whom be glory through infinite ages of ages. Amen.

### Here ends the Practical Music of Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja, Spaniard,
### originating from the province of Baetica and the city of Baeza, in the diocese of Jaén.
### Printed in the nurturing city of Bologna while he was lecturing there publicly.
### In the year of our Lord fourteen hundred and eighty-two, on the fourth day before the Ides of May <note>May 12, 1482</note>.
